# Consideration of funding proposals - Addendum II

## Funding proposal package for FP100

### Summary

This addendum contains the following seven parts:

- a) A funding proposal titled "REDD PLUS results-based payments for reducing emissions from deforestation and forest degradation in 2014 and 2015";
- b) No-objection letter issued by the national designated authority(ies) or focal point(s);
- c) Environmental and social report(s) disclosure;
- d) Secretary's assessment;
- e) Independent Technical Advisory Panel's assessment;
- f) Response to comments sent to the Independent Technical Advisory Panel's assessment; and
- g) Gender documentation.

The funding proposal package for FP100 is being submitted for the first time for the Board's consideration at its twenty-second meeting.

## REDD+ results based payments

Version 1.0

Accredited entities are expected to develop a funding proposal in close consultation with the relevant national designated authority (NDA) and submit it to GCF for review in request for proposals for the Pilot Program for REDD plus results-based payments (Decision B/1697). ## In addition to the Amazon biome, which comprises approximately 4,107,500 km² and corresponds to 49% of the national territory, Brazil has five other biomes: Caatinga (2,036,448 km² – 25.5% of the national territory), Mata Atlantica (1,000,000 km² – 12.7% of the national territory), Cerrado (900,000 km² – 11.3% of the national territory), Pampas (176,496 km² - 2.07% of the national territory), and Pantanal (150,300 km² – 1.78% of the national territory) (BRALC, 2010; Volume 1: Table 3.MS).


---
**Page 5**
---

So far Brazil received payments for less than 5% of its total REDD-plus results (for more information on payments see Section B.1.2 of this proposal). All the results already paid for have been excluded from the estimate of results offered to the GCF for payments.

Since 1996, the National Institute for Space Research (INPE) of the Ministry of Science, Technology, Innovation and Communications has been monitoring deforestation rates in the Legal Amazon region. Brazil used this information as input data for its REDD+ PRODES activity database for its REDD+ estimates.

Every year, Brazil releases the deforestation rate for the previous year. The methodology used by Brazil to estimate yearly deforestation rates is similar to that of the PRODES database and is modified compared to the PRODES methodology. This was done in order to extend the scope of the PRODES database to the Amazon biome, following the biome-level approach recommended by FREL (2017). More information on deforestation (more information in Section B.1.1) of this proposal.

REDD+ plus results are presented to the United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change (UNFCCC) through the submission of a report to the UNFCCC Working Group of Technical Experts on REDD+, as part of the domestic reporting process. Brazil has submitted technical annex with REDD-plus results achieved in the Amazon biome between 2011 and 2015, measured against FREL in 2017. In 2017, Brazil’s FREL-BURR technical annex presented results for the entire Amazon biome (including the Amazon biome between 2011 and 2015, measured against FREL) to the UNFCCC in 2019 and should include REDD-plus results up to 2018.

The Legal Amazon region is an area of approximately 5.217 M (5,217,742,300 ha) that covers the totality of the Brazilian states of Acre, Amazonas, Pará, Roraima, Rondônia and Amapá, and part of the states of Mato Grosso and Maranhão. The Legal Amazon region encompasses three different biomes: the entire Amazon biome; 37% of the Cerrado biome; and 40% of the Pantanal biome.

## Between 2014 and 2018, the expected volume of REDD-plus results to be achieved by Brazil in the Amazon biome is around 2,386,665,669 tCO2e, or 2.39 billion tCO2e.

This amount greatly exceeds the limit established by the GCF for results-based payments per country, which is USD 150 million or 30 million tCO2e. The GCF will not be able to cover all of Brazil's share of the total REDD-plus results achieved by Brazil between 2014 and 2018.

REDD+ results are expected to be offered in proportionality for all years of the eligibility period.

The terms of reference for this GCF pilot program indicate that countries will be expected to present a significant, indicative volume of results per year during the eligibility period. A footnote explains that in this context, significant means that the volume of results presented (in each year) should be proportional to the overall level of results achieved in relevant years.

This indicative volume of results to be presented by Brazil to the GCF is not significant compared with the total amount of results expected for all years of the eligibility period and proportionality for all years of the eligibility period.

The total payable volume per country is set by the terms of reference, which are subject to further discussion and input. There are no rules concerning the allocation of payments per year or per project. Countries will be required to present an indicative significant volume.

Specific allocation, however, may vary and will be determined on a case-by-case basis in accordance with specific instructions provided by GCF. There are no specifications or definitions on the volumes of REDD+ results countries need to offer to the GCF.

# REDD-plus RBP FUNDING PROPOSAL

## GREEN CLIMATE FUND | PAGE 8 OF 97

Brazil adopted Approach 3 for land representation, meaning that all data on land conversions and lands remaining in a same land-use category between inventories are spatially explicit. The basis for all activity data is the Amazon biome, which was mapped using Landsat imagery (30 m resolution). The boundaries of its FREL was remotely sensed data of the same spatial resolution (Landsat-class, up to 30 meters).

The same national institutions and team engaged in the development of the land use, land use change and forestry (LULUCF) inventory for Brazil were also involved in the development of the Amazon biome. The aim is the estimation of the rate of gross deforestation for PRODES, ensuring an even greater consistency between the estimates from PRODES and those from the FREL. This was achieved by using the same data, which are the basis for estimating the gross CO2 emissions from deforestation for the Amazon biome included in the Second National GHG Inventory. The area of forest that is mapped as having been cleared equivalent to 0.25 ha on the surface. The high DIGITAS data maintained this threshold to ensure consistency with the FREL, which was developed using the same data and methodology. All areas where forests are monitored are inventoried by PRODES if they evolve into an area larger than 0.25 ha in the year of monitoring. This allows for a more accurate estimation of deforestation events and to provide information on the extent of deforestation areas that are not retrieved only by PRODES, which is important of showing that significant deforestation is excluded from the FREL. This is being done by INPE/FUNCEB.

Furthermore, the experts from the institutions responsible for the development of the Second National GHG Inventory were involved in the validation process and provided technical expertise to the Technical Experts on REDD+ that supported the development of the FREL submission and its quality control.

LULUCF reporting under Brazil's Second National GHG Inventory covered the period 1994 to 2002 and included all land use changes, including deforestation. The data provided in the FREL submission are those provided in the Second National GHG Inventory for the area deforested in both managed and unmanaged forest lands between years 1994 and 2002. In addition, the figures provided in the Second National GHG Inventory for the Amazon biome are based on gross CO2 emissions from deforestation and forest removals from this new category. The Amazon biome data presented in the FREL refers only to gross emissions from deforestation and forest degradation (DFGD) and not to forest regrowth. The Second National GHG Inventory and those estimated for gross deforestation in Brazil's FREL for the Amazon biome are based on the same methodology.

The forest definition used by Brazil in the construction of the FREL was the same as the one that Brazil used in its Second National GHG Inventory (i.e. a minimum area of 0.5 ha, height of 5 m or more and at least 10 percent tree cover).

## Area for future improvement

The AT considered the treatment of non-CO2 gases as an area for future technical improvement.

### Brazil's response:

The FREL included only CO2 emissions, consistent with the Second National GHG Inventory. The Brazilian National GHG Inventory, submitted to the UNFCCC in 2016, two years before Brazil’s submission of a FREL, for results achieved between 2014-2015, included estimates of non-CO2 emissions from biomass burning and land use change in the Amazon biome, but only for a single year (2010).

Box 2 below presents some considerations regarding the treatment of non-CO2 gases. More information can be found in the next sections of this proposal.

## Box 2: Consideration regarding non-CO2 gases

Paragraph 29 of the TAR of the FREL submitted by Brazil to the UNFCCC indicated the treatment of emissions of non-CO2 gases as an area for future technical improvement of the FREL. An analysis of the impact of the inclusion of non-CO2 gases in the Brazilian National GHG Inventory, covering results for year 2010, included in the Third National GHG Inventory includes the following emissions: 6,430 Gg CO2 eq., 18 Gg CH4 and 159 kg N2O eq. respectively.

## Green Climate Fund

### Page 10 of 97

#### Box 3: Approaches to estimate the area of gross deforestation in the Amazon biome

There are several approaches to estimate the areas deforested and different results may be obtained depending on which approach is used. The most common approaches include: from the annual increments of deforestation; from the annual rate of deforestation; or from the adjusted deforestation increment.

The explanations provided below aim to clarify these different approaches and terminologies.

(1) **Deforestation Polygons (at year t):** refers to a new deforestation event identified from the analysis of satellite imagery. The polygons are created for each year, with the most recent deforestation mapped up to year t-1. Each deforestation polygon is spatially identified (georeferenced) and assigned an ID number that corresponds to the date of detection (the date of the satellite image from which it was mapped). For each year, a map containing all deforestation polygons for that year is created. This approach is used for PRODES (and hence, for the Amazon biome, after exclusion of the areas associated with the Cerrado and Pantanal biomes) to estimate the area deforested in the Amazon biome. The map does not include deforestation polygons under cloud-covered areas. However, the deforestation increment may be calculated for each scene that covers the biome.

(2) **Deforestation Increment or Increments of Deforestation (at year t):** refers to the sum of the areas of all observed deforestation polygons within a given geographical extent. This progression is used in PRODES to estimate the area deforested at any time t, for which the same date as the deforestation polygons mapped on that scene, or the entire Amazon biome, for which the deforestation polygons are mapped, is considered. The deforestation increment may be calculated for each scene that covers the biome. The deforestation increment may underestimate the actual area of deforestation (at year t-1 and t), since it does not account for the area of deforestation polygons under clouds.

(3) **Adjusted Deforestation Increment or Adjusted Increments of Deforestation (at year t):** this adjustment is applied to the deforestation increment at years t, t-1, etc. (if applicable) to account for deforestation polygons in areas affected by cloud cover and that are observable at time t. It is calculated according to the following equation:

# REDD+ RBP FUNDING PROPOSAL

## GREEN CLIMATE FUND | PAGE 11 OF 97

Where:

* **IncAdj(t)** = adjusted deforestation increments at year t, km²
* **IncAdj(0)** = initial deforestation increment (base year), km²
* **ZACCOV(i,t)** = area of the deforestation polygons observed at time t over cloud-covered areas at year i-1 (but are cloud-free at year i). Since these 20 km² may accumulate the area of the deforestation increment that occurred at year i-1 but was not observed until year i, the reference date is a reference date of August 1st and provides an estimate of the deforestation increment at year t when the deforestation increment observed at year t+1 is attributed to cloud cover prior to year t.
* **ZACCOV(i,t)** = area of the deforestation polygons observed at year i over areas that were cloud-covered at years i-1 and i (but are cloud-free at year i). Since these 20 km² may accumulate the area of the deforestation increment that occurred at year i but was not observed until year i+1, the reference date is a reference date of August 1st and provides an estimate of the deforestation increment at year t when the deforestation increment observed at year t-1 is attributed to cloud cover prior to year t.
* **ZACCOV(i,t)** = area of the deforestation polygons observed at year i over areas that were cloud-covered at years i-2 and i (but are cloud-free at year i). Since these 20 km² may accumulate the area of the deforestation increment that occurred at year i but was not observed until year t+1, the reference date is a reference date of August 1st and provides an estimate of the deforestation increment at year t when the deforestation increment observed at year t-2 is attributed to cloud cover prior to year t.
* **ZACCOV(i,t)** = area of the deforestation polygons observed at year i over areas that were cloud-covered at years i-3 and i (but are cloud-free at year i). Since these 20 km² may accumulate the area of the deforestation increment that occurred at year i but was not observed until year t+1, the reference date is a reference date of August 1st and provides an estimate of the deforestation increment at year t when the deforestation increment observed at year t-3 is attributed to cloud cover prior to year t.

As an example, suppose that the area of the deforestation increment observed at year i (Inc(i)) is 20 km². Suppose also that the area of the deforestation increment observed at year i+1 (Inc(i+1)) is 50 km². Then the adjusted deforestation increment for year t (IncAdj(t)) equals the area of the deforestation increment observed at year i+1 but is cloud-free at year i-1 and year i (but are cloud-free at year i). Since these 20 km² may accumulate the area of the deforestation increment that occurred at year i-1 but was not observed until year i, the reference date is a reference date of August 1st and provides an estimate of the deforestation increment at year t when the deforestation increment observed at year i+1 is attributed to cloud cover prior to year i.

### Deforestation Rate (α)

The factor α is introduced in PRODES to sequentially address the effect of cloud cover, and, if necessary, the effect of time lapse between consecutive images. The default value for this parameter is 0.5. This means that the deforestation increment observed at year t+1 represents a combination of the deforestation increment observed at year t (the presence of cloud-covered areas in an image at year i may mask the observation of deforestation) and the deforestation increment that occurred between years t and t+1 but was not yet deforested, while the presence of clouds in previous years (e.g., α = 0.5 at year t-1 may lead to an overestimation of the area deforested if all deforestation outside cloud at year t is attributed to year t).

This over or under-estimation may also occur if the dates of the satellites images used in subsequent years are not synchronized, and if there is a significant time lapse between the images used at years i and i+1. The reference date provides a reference date of August 1st and provides an estimate of the deforestation increment at year t when the deforestation rate is constant during the dry season and zero during the wet season.

As an example of cloud connection, suppose that the primary forest area in an image is 20,000 km². Suppose also that the observed deforestation increment is 180 km². As part of the calculation of the adjusted deforestation increment (IncAdj), the reference date provides a reference date of August 1st and provides an estimate of the deforestation increment at year t when the deforestation rate is constant during the dry season and zero during the wet season.

In this case, the adjusted increment of deforestation is 200 km².


---
**Page 14**
---

Note that at any one year, an estimate based on the adjusted deforestation increment may be higher than the actual value if there is a significant underestimation.

For the sake of verifiability, Brazil's FREL submission introduced a slight change in the methodology used to calculate the deforestation increment. The new methodology uses the REDD+ methodology to annualize observed deforestation and to consider unobserved areas due to cloud cover.

The methodology applied in Brazil’s FREL submission relies on a verifiable deforestation map and an associated time series of land use data. The time series is constructed by estimating the area covered by equitably distributing the area of the deforestation polygons observed at year t over cloud-covered years 1 and t-1 (or any year where that area was persistently cloud covered) among years 1 and t-1.

The FREL methodology does not consider areas of deforestation under clouds, since the associated CO2 emissions are considered to be unverifiable. The annualization of the area of deforestation under clouds could result in an underestimate of the annual emissions.

As for future improvements, Brazil noted that the use of alternative satellite data to LandSat is reducing the need for adjustments, as deforestation under cloud-covered areas is assessed using alternative satellite data.

The AT sought several clarifications on the estimation of activity data from Brazil. Brazil clarified that PRODES considers deforestation not only as the clear-cut in "intact" primary forest but also as the clear-cut area of secondary forests (including areas with a high density of trees and low levels of logging). The emissions from deforestation of these areas are estimated using the same carbon map.

Area for future improvement: The AT noted that the carbon density of the areas previously subjected to selective logging is not well known. Consequently, the AT noted that for these areas the emission estimates used in the construction of the FREL methodology may be overestimates or underestimates depending on the extent and intensity of degradation processes that occurred previously. The AT acknowledges the complexity of separating emissions from deforestation and degradation and the need to improve the understanding of this relationship between degradation and deforestation as an area for future technical improvements of the FREL.

best statistical model was made based on the largest coefficient of determination, smallest standard error of the estimate, and best distribution of residuals (Santos, 1998). In the case of the total biomass estimates using the equations developed by Higuchi et al., (1998), Chambers et al. (2001) and Silva (2007), the estimated biomass was consistently below 0%. In addition, the logarithm model using a single independent variable (diameter at breast height - DBH) produced results as consistent and as precise as those with two variables (DBH and height) (Higuchi, 1998).

Silva (2007) also demonstrated that the total fresh weight (above and below ground biomass) of primary forest stands in the Amazon Basin was underestimated by up to 53% using the equations developed by Higuchi et al. (1998), Chambers et al. (2001) and Silva (2007). All three equations developed for CA tend to overestimate the total biomass of small trees, while underestimating that of large trees and very large trees. Despite this, the total biomass of the sampled trees estimated using the equations derived by Higuchi et al., Chambers et al. and Silva, 2007, respectively, were 95%, 3% and 1%, respectively. It is recognized that the application of the allometric equation developed for a specific area of the Amazon biome may increase the uncertainties of the estimates when applied to other areas.

In this sense, the work by Nogueira et al. (2008) is relevant to be cited here. Nogueira et al. (2008) tested three equations derived from the RADAMBRASIL database and found that the equation developed by Higuchi et al., Chambers et al. and Silva, 2007, respectively, were 95%, 3% and 1%, respectively.

The AT noted that the "carbon map" used in the FREL based on the RADAMBRASIL database, combined with an equation developed by Higuchi et al., Chambers et al. and Silva, 2007, respectively, resulted in a significant effort aimed at recognizing the heterogeneity in carbon densities within the Amazon biome, and consequently in the total biomass estimates of the forested areas (LULUCF experts). However, due to inconsistencies in the carbon map, Brazil explained that these were partly associated with the specific circumstances of the RADAMBRASIL database (e.g., lack of information on tree species composition), and noted that the widely used RADAMBRASIL database is the best available information now.

Area for future improvement: The AT considers better understanding of the relationship between degradation, deforestation and forest carbon stocks in the Amazon biome. In this regard, it was noted that when emissions from deforestation are included in the FREL, Brazil will need to demonstrate how double counting can be avoided (e.g., by using a consistent methodology for estimating biomass). The AT is subject to ongoing logging and subsequently clear cut.

The LULUCF experts encouraged Brazil to continue its efforts to improve its carbon map for the Amazon biome. The LULUCF experts also recommended that Brazil work with Brazil to assess the spatial distribution in carbon densities in the Amazon biome and commended Brazil for continuing to work on updating and

### Improving the carbon map based on new and improved ground data from the National Forest Inventory (NFI)

#### Brazil's response:
Brazil explained that the time series available is too short to allow an adequate understanding of the degradation process. Since 2015, Brazil has been working on building an understanding of the dynamics of forest degradation and secondary vegetation in the Amazon and Cerrado biomes and ensuring there is no double counting of emissions when considering deforestation and forest regrowth (Secondary Vegetation) in these biomes.

#### Experts' response:
Experts noted that, unlike other deforestation drivers, understanding of forest degradation and forest regrowth may require the production of new data by research institutions, as well as the assessment of the use of land for agriculture. Brazil has not yet provided information on how it will ensure that the data is adequate (in relation to the anthropogenic contribution to the associated emissions) lies in the ability to identify the drivers and causes of forest degradation and regrowth, particularly about biomass.

#### World views:
With regards to improvements in the carbon map, Brazil indicated that this will be possible with the conclusion of the country's first NFI, which is currently collecting the necessary data for the whole national territory. The information collected by the NFI has been used to make adjustments to the TNC (finalized in 2016 after the submission of Brazil’s FREL and AT).

12OP.17 All the elements in the annex of decision 12/CP.17 were considered in the constructions of Brazil’s FREL.

Brazil noted that in a FREL, paragraphs 10 and 11 of Decision 12/CP.17 (FCCC/CP/2011/Add.2) “agree that a step-wise approach to a national FREL may be useful, enabling Parties to improve the FREL by incorporating additional elements as they become available.” The decision also states that the FREL “acknowledges that subnational FRELS will be elaborated as an interim measure, while transitioning to a national level.”

Brazil is a country of continental dimensions and with a large diversity of forest types, which makes the development of data at the national level a huge challenge. Brazil submitted a subnational FREL for the Amazon region in 2014 (FREL submission to the UNFCCC, Annex). The Amazon biome covers an area of approximately 5.8 million km², the fifth-largest country in the world, and it is two times the total area of the European Union.

In 2014, Brazil’s FREL submission to the UNFCCC included an Annex detailing the approach that the country plans to undertake to construct its national FREL for reducing emissions from deforestation. Six individual biomes will be constructed (see Figure 2) and the Brazilian territory will be constructed and their subsequent sum will be the national FREL.

Figure 2: The Brazilian biomes

Source: FREL submission, 2014, Brazil.


In 2015, Brazil launched its National Strategy for REDD+ (ENRESDH) with the objective to scale up the implementation of potential actions and projects in all Brazilian biomes to the national level. To do that, the National REDD+ Committee (CONAREDD) was established and responsible for coordinating the development of a comprehensive strategy. The ENRESDH includes policies, laws, regulations, actions and initiatives from various stakeholders contribute to REDD+ plus implementation at the national level. It is also an important document in the implementation of the main policies, law, regulations and funds that contribute to REDD+ plus implementation in Brazil.

At the strategic level, the National Policy on Climate Change outlines the objectives and guidelines for addressing climate change and its impacts. The policy sets out a series of actions aimed at reducing GHG emissions by 36.1% to 38.9% in relation to the projected emissions until 2020. Regarding

### Figure 3: The ENREDO+ legal framework

| Strategic Level | Tactical Operational Level |
| --- | --- |
| National Policy on Climate Change and Forest Code | National Climate Change Plan and Plans to Prevent and Control Deforestation (Forest Code) |
| Amazon Fund, National Climate Change Fund, National Forest (Dispersed Fund) and Amazon Forest (Protected Area) |

Source: Brazil's ENREDO+, 2015

Brazil's sovereign commitment to the protection of native vegetation and the integrity of the climate system for the well-being of present and future generations was reiterated by Law No. 12,651/2012 (Forest Code). The national policy for the recovery of native vegetation is based on the Forest Code, which establishes mandatory regulations in the Amazon biome, where the rural properties are located. In the Amazon biome, the share of RL corresponds to 80% of the property area covered by forest vegetation and 60% of those in other types of natural vegetation; for the remaining biomes, that is, those in native grass-covered regions or in regions outside of the Amazon biome, the share of RL is 20%.

Promoting the environmental regularization of private rural areas is crucial for improving land uses practices in the context of climate change. The Forest Code established mandatory regulations on the National Environmental System (NAT) and the National Forest Code established mandatory regulation on the Rural Environmental Registry (CAR) for all rural lands, regardless of their location or type of vegetation. In addition to these two instruments, the Forest Code relating to the insufficiency of AP and RL shall present a Designated or Altered Area Recovery Project (PRA), which must be approved by the competent environmental agency within 180 days from the date of issuance of Law No. 9.395/1996 and R. 24/2014.

Finally, in addition to implementing the Forest Code, the Federal Government has instituted the National Policy for the Recovery of Native Vegetation, known as Proveg, through Federal Decree No. 8.872/2003. Proveg aims at promoting the recovery and environmental regularization of forests and other forms of native vegetation and to provide the environmental regularization of those areas that are not covered by the Forest Code. The implementation of Proveg is based on two main measures, by December 31, 2030. The main instrument of implementation of Proveg is the National Plan for the Recovery of Native Vegetation (Planaveg), launched through Inter-ministerial Ordinance No. 250/2009.

* * *

> **Second** to the revision of the Forest Code, in 2012, a series of advances in environmental policies and law occurred in Brazil, including: Law No. 13.465/2017 (National Policy for Environmental Management and the National Environment System) (Federal Law No. 10.805/2003); Law No. Provisória for Conservation, Sustainable Use and Restoration of Forests (Federal Law No. 11.994/2009); Law No. 13.765/2019 (National Policy on Public Forest Management) (Federal Law No. 11.240/2005); the National Policy for Environmental and Territorial Management of Indigenous Peoples and Traditional Communities (Federal Decree No. 8.619/2005); and the Amazon Programme - ABRA (Federal Decree No. 8.505/2011), among others.


---
**Page 20**
---

November 14, 2017 This funding proposal will support the continuation of the implementation of the Forest Code in Brazil, with a focus on the Amazon biome and its relationship to small-scale, indigenous peoples and traditional communities, having as a basis the information from the National Rural Environmental Registry System (SIS-AR). 

At the national level, the Brazilian government has developed a series of RBP plus RBP funding proposal Green Climate Fund) Page 18 of 97 biome-wide action plans for the prevention and control of deforestation, which are, at present, the main instruments to promote integration and coordination between federal and state governments in the implementation of the Forest Code. These action plans are being implemented by states under implementation. In addition to that, the nine states in the Legal Amazon region have similarly structured state-level plans.

The national and state plans feature analysis on land tenure issues, forest governance, the dynamics of deforestation and its main drivers, as well as an indigenous peoples and traditional communities. The plans also identify the most important actions for each of these areas, including those aimed at addressing the drivers identified; a detailed operational plan, assigning which body is responsible for each action; and the monitoring and evaluation framework to assess progress over time.

Due to their relevance, the Action Plan for the Prevention and Control of Deforestation in the Legal Amazon (PCD/AM) and the Action Plan for the Prevention and Control of Deforestation and Forest Fires in the Brazilian Amazon (PCD/BAF) are included as annexes. These plans are part of a series of nine state Sectoral Plans: Climate Change Mitigation and Adaptation to Establish a Low-Carbon Economy in Agriculture, Forestry and Other Land Uses (Sectors Plans), which were developed by states and cover plans form the pillars of the PRM/C for mitigation in the LULUCF sector, contributing directly to REDD-plus.

The Action Plans will continue to be developed, as appropriate, at the biome level, to ensure that the particularities of each biome are taken into account. These action plans will include specific actions and measures aimed at addressing the drivers at the local level. In other biomes, for example, the RBP plus RBP funding proposal Green Climate Fund) Page 18 of 97 action plan is being developed in the Atlantic Forest for example. At the same time, the national REDD+ scheme is in place to ensure that these actions are coordinated at a national level and that the benefits from one biome could positively influence the other regions.

In 2015, to improve environmental monitoring at the national level, the Brazilian Environmental Ministry (MMA) developed the National Monitoring System for Forests (SIN-AMAZON). The MMA is currently implementing this system through partnerships between the MMA, MCTI through NBI-MMA, and INPE. This system will allow the country to monitor forest cover changes at a national level from the Federal Budget, as well as from international cooperation and established funds, such as the Amazon Fund. The National Monitoring System for Forests is aligned with the UNFCCC's REDD+ Program and is in line with the objectives of the ENEDD and will deliver the enhancement and improvement of environmental monitoring at a national level.

In 2017, as a sign of commitment to the scaling up of REDD+ at the national level and using the data produced as part of the program detailed above, Brazil submitted to the UNFCCC a FREL for reducing emissions from deforestation and forest degradation in the Amazon biome. The results are being assessed by two LULUCF experts selected from the UNFCCC roster. The results reducing emissions from deforestation in the Brazilian biome are expected to be submitted to the UNFCCC as part of the country's 3rd BUR in 2018.

Together, the Amazon and the cerrado biomes account for approximately 75% of the total national territory and cover an area of about 6 million km².

In 2018, following paragraph 12 of Decision 12/CPR.17, Brazil presented an updated version of its Amazon biome FREL (CBI) for results achieved between 2016 and 2020. The FREL is currently going through the UNFCCC's review process.


---
**Page 21**
---

In FREL C Brazil indicated that its approach to scale up REDD+ to the national level does not imply that the same approach will be used in each biome. The characteristics and dynamics of REDD+ activities in each biome are different. Two important elements. In the view of Brazil, the following aspects should be considered: (i) the results presented at the level of the FREL and (ii) emission reduction results presented at biome level in the future are consistent with the corresponding biome FREL.

Regarding other carbon sink REED plus activities, Brazil will include emissions reductions from forest degradation in the biomes where this activity is considered by the Working Group of Technical Experts on REDD+ under the UNFCCC. In addition to the above, Brazil will continue its efforts to scale up REDD+. Brazil will focus its efforts to have a better understanding and methods for accounting for the processes related to land use change and forest cover, including regrowth of secondary forests, which would result in a transition from gross to net emissions.

The AT commended Brazil for showing a strong commitment to continuous improvement of its FREL, consistent with its step-wise approach.


### (ii) FRELL/FRL

The AT noted that the data and information used by Brazil in constructing its FRELL are transparent. Digital PRODES allows INPE/MTIC to make available through the web the deforestation maps in vector format. The AT also noted that the data and information provided by Brazil, including the FRELL and PRODES data, which is made available on the website of the Ministry of Environment of Brazil, the most important elements, before accuracy, are ensuring consistency and transparency of the data submitted.


### (iii) FREL

The AT also considered that the additional information provided by Brazil in the modified submission after the inputs from the LULUCF experts considerably increased the transparency of its FRELL. The AT noted that the use of the FRELL and PRODES data, without the need to alter the approach used to construct the proposed FREL.

The information provided in the FRELL, including through the data made available on websites and the explanatory text, is sufficient for the validation of the methodology and reproducibility of FREL calculations.


### Details about sources and how the information used by Brazil in constructing its FRELL can be easily accessed

Below are details about sources and how the information used by Brazil in constructing its FRELL can be easily accessed on websites that are freely and openly available to the public:

- **Satellite imagery**: Remotely sensed data is the major source of information used to map deforestation patterns every year. The availability of all satellite images used since 1988 allows for the reclassification of historical data, which has been done by INPE. These images are freely available on the Amazon biome as well as the cloud-covered areas. Since 2003, INPE adopted an innovative policy to make freely available all satellite images acquired in the Brazilian territory. The images used at the satellite images from the China-Brazil Earth Resources Satellite (CBERS-2 and CBERS-2B) through 1998 are freely available on the website of the Ministry of Environment of Brazil. The American LandSat satellite and the Indian satellite ResourceSat-1 were also made available. With the above mentioned satellites, it is possible to map deforestation patterns in the Brazilian biome.

- **Deforestation polygons**: All deforestation polygons mapped for the Amazon biome (i.e., aggregated until 2007; aggregated for years: 1998, 1999 and 2000; and annual from 2001 until 2016) are freely available on the website of the Ministry of Environment of Brazil. Through this information, it is possible to identify the areas deforested in each year. The data can be downloaded through the following website: <https://www.ibge.gov.br/ibgeportal>. This information is a subset of that made available since 2003 by INPE through the PRODES system. Through this website, for each satellite image, a vector map in shapefile format is generated and made available, along with all the previous deforestation polygons; the areas not deforested, the hydrology network

(a) FREL/FIREL consistency: Please provide any additional information that supplements the information contained in the Technical Assessment Report in relation to the consistency of the methodology used over the time series. If possible, please provide a plan and address outcome issues that were not material for the consistency of the FREL/FIREL as per the AT’s request. This should be revised due to time and data restrictions.

In assessing the extent to which the FREL is consistent with the information and descriptions provided by Brazil, the AT considered the time series comparison in the Amazon-Basin with PRODES/INPE deforestation date (the rate of the Legal Amazon).

The consistency of the PRODES time series is ensured by using the same forest type definition, same minimum mapping unit, same methodology for the interpretation of Landsat images, and the same methodological approach to analyze the remotely sensed data at every new assessment.

Forest areas affected by forest degradation that do not have a clear pattern in the satellite imagery are not included in the PRODES time series. This is because the PRODES deforestation date is based on forest degradation. This ensures the consistency of the PRODES deforestation time series over time.

At the start of PRODES, deforestation polygons were identified by visual interpretation on false color composites and then verified using Landsat images. The same methodology was used to identify the aggregated deforestation up to the previous year. Subsequently these deforestation polygons were manually updated with new information from satellite imagery and field surveys. This analogical approach to assess deforestation (Analog PRODES) was employed from 1988 until 2002.

Due to the increased computing capability built by INPE, it was possible to transition to digital annual assessment of deforestation in 2003. The new methodology is called Anisotropic PRODES and uses a previous year’s deforestation map as input for the current year’s deforestation map.

Digital PRODES implements full consistency with the Analog PRODES data. This includes consistency with the forest boundaries in Analog PRODES and the aggregated deforestation polygons. Despite the evolution to a digital assessment, the identification of the deforestation polygons continued to be carried out

Through visual interpretation in the screen and not through digital classification methods. This ensures even greater consistency between the Analog and Digital.

Due to the lack of availability of satellite imagery, INPE PROCES started, INPE decided to map the deforestation polygons from years 1998 to 2000 on an aggregated deforestation map until 1997 (digital base map). The resulting database was then converted into a single digital database, with no discrimination of the specific year when deforestation occurred.

From year 2000 onwards, the deforestation polygons have been annually assessed and included in the Digital Forest Map. This means that the database is updated every year, in a single file. Thus, the geographical expansion of deforestation, as well as its spatial pattern, can be assessed.

In summary, the digital database does not have individual deforestation information for years prior to 1997 inclusive; it has information for years 1998 to 2000 in an aggregated format; and information (deforestation polygons) from year 2000 onwards is available on a yearly basis.

Digital PRODES allowed INPE to make available through the web the deforestation map in vector format, as well as the satellite images used. This ensures full transparency to the public in general. Since 2003, INPE has been providing the data from the Digital Forest Map (DFM) and the corresponding satellite imagery used to generate the information, and the maps with the identification of deforestation polygons. The DFM is available through the web at the following addresses: Landsat5/8 (or similar data as CERSISCO, Resourcesat-1LSS and DMIC). Each image is accompanied by the metadata file in XML format.

The DFM is used to generate the FREL (Forest Reference Emission Level) for each state and to manage large-scale projects. A few non-governmental organizations, state level entities and research centers use this information as a basis for their own calculations of deforestation rates.

For more information on the efforts of INPE to make deforestation data more accessible please see: <http://www.inpe.br/engbr/portal/inpe-deforestation-data-for-the-brazilian-amazon-available-at-global-forest-watch/>

Since then, Brazil received inputs from various international stakeholders, with the general understanding that historical information before year 2000 should not be used to a future national FREL submission from Brazil to REDD-plus at the national level. Brazil’s efforts now are focused on building deforestation data for all Brazilian biomes to scale up REDD-plus to the national level, rather than improving the historical data before year 2000 for the Amazon biome.

### Accuracy of the activity data

Brazil uses the information from PRODES as a basis for its activity data. The use of adjusted deforestation increments and the application of the time series data to the deforested area provides a greater accuracy, verifiable figure for the deforested area through time than the deforestation rate. As the analysis is conducted retrospectively, deforestation increments can be redistributed over time.

See Box 3 for more information.

### Due to the characteristics of the time series data (e.g., annual well-to-well assessments, adjustment for differences in forest structure and age, etc.), it was not possible to use the PRODES data as a substitute for the data from the National GHG Inventories that do not present annual estimates but consider annual averages. The PRODES data is considered to be the most accurate for the purposes of the FREL construction for the Amazon biome.

Brazil assumes that the biomass immediately after the forest conversion is zero and does not consider any subsequent regrowth, which means that the deforestation increment is equal to the area converted. This assumption is made since Brazil has a consistent, credible, accurate, transparent, and verifiable time-series for gross deforestation and reforestation.

The accuracy of PRODES data was analyzed by INPE in 2014 using satellite imagery from 2013 and 2014. The analysis was conducted by Adam et al. (2017) who analyzed the accuracy of PRODES data, taking the data for the year of 2014 for the whole Amazon biome.

Results show a global accuracy of 94.5 ± 2.0%, with high-level agreement estimated by the expert panel. The results are consistent across all biomes and land cover types in the Amazon biome since the focus of their study was on the accuracy of the deforestation increment mapping using satellite imagery from 2013 and 2014, which is the same period used for this analysis.

### INPE is currently working on a plan to assess the accuracy of PRODES data for the whole Legal Amazon region. PRODES maps will be compared against remotely sensed satellite data with finer spatial resolution. Recently, the first results were obtained from the comparison between PRODES and Landsat 8 imagery. The quality of this type of accuracy assessment has become available. A sampling design is being constructed to define the number of samples needed for a representative sample in each biome. Currently, the higher spatial resolution data are not freely available, so a cost estimate is currently underway to define the scope of the assessment and the resources required.

The results from the 2014 PRODES map will be used as a baseline for the development of the national FREL by 2020.

### Soil organic carbon

Brazil explained in its ECLC (2018) that it has limited information on subsequent land-use after deforestation and conversion for the period considered in the FREL. However, Brazil mentioned two sources of information that were used to estimate the rate of soil carbon of the conversion from forest to grassland: one is a study by LULUCF (2015) which was published at the same time as the submission of the FREL, which includes data from a specially explicit database for conversion to other land uses. The second source of information is the Brazilian Agricultural Research Corporation (EMBRAPA) database. The dominant conversion for 2008 and 2010 is again from forest land to grassland (approximately 85 per cent).

Given this information, Brazil carried out a literature review to assess the impact of forest conversions on carbon stocks in the soil. The results show that there are two main trends: an initial loss of carbon after the first years of conversion, the subsequent trend is strongly influenced by rainfall and land use management practices (e.g., fertilization). This is consistent with other studies under those native forests. The soil depth and the lifespan considered also influence this analysis. The conclusion drawn from these studies should be taken as indicative conditions and may not be representative of many situations that might occur in the Amazon basin.

### Area for future improvement

The AT identified the improvements in the understanding of soil carbon dynamics, including the role of dead wood, as a priority area for future research and stimulated new research. Brazil stated that it plans to intensify efforts to better understand the functioning of forest soils and their potential for sequestration and emission review and stimulating new research, bearing in mind that changes may not occur rapidly after the conversion and that they are dependent on pasture management.

### Dead wood

Regarding emissions from dead wood, the AT requested clarification on the reasons for the omission of this pool. Brazil explained that the rationale behind the non-inclusion was the consideration that emissions from dead wood are only released when decomposition occurs (i.e., the process of decomposition = the issue is related only to the time when the emissions are released).

The AT considered that when deforestation occurs, there are emissions from dead wood to the atmosphere that occur during the initial stages of decomposition. Brazil explained that it has developed a method for estimating carbon stock changes in dead wood (refer to Chapter 3.4.12.1 for forest land use change) and the corresponding default emission factor (refer to table 3.2.2, 18.2.1 C/kg for dead wood stock in tropical forests).

## Area for Future Improvement

The FREL has noted that the current emissions of pools and gases is likely to be conservative in some contexts (i.e., the omission of dead wood). Brent explained in more details the justification for the omission of dead wood pool in its FREL C (2018) submission. The Third National GHG Inventory (2016) included dead wood as a separate carbon pool, while the Second National GHG Inventory for the Amazon biome were applied to the deforestation data from 2002 and 2003.

The effect of the carbon map in the Second and Third National GHG Inventory is presented in Table 9. Since the inclusion of dead wood in the Third National GHG Inventory includes living biomass, litter and dead wood, the effect was assessed as follows:

(i) maintain the same carbon pools i.e., excluding the dead wood pool from the carbon map in the Third National GHG Inventory.

(ii) maintain the carbon map from the Third National GHG Inventory, with the four carbon pools.

In the Third National GHG Inventory, the percent contribution of the dead wood pool to the total biomass per hectare was discriminated for dense and non-dense forests. The main ratios of the carbon in the dead wood pool were 10% and 25%, respectively, for dense and non-dense forests.

The effect on the inventory estimates is presented in Table 9. The results are compared with those from the Second National GHG Inventory with consideration of the same pools (living biomass and litter), as well as with the Third National GHG Inventory without dead wood pool. The results were obtained using deforestation increments and not from the adjusted deforestation increments.

## Emissions from gas
Please provide any additional information that supplements the information submitted in the previous section. This includes, but is not limited to, estimates of significant sources of emissions. If not all of the gases are included, justify whether gases that are significant sources of emissions have been excluded and why. In case of data and/or methodology changes, please communicate this to FREL. The submission of information on methodologies and assumptions underestimates results. Provide also a plan to include all significant points in future FREL submissions.

Brazil's FREL for the Amazon biome includes CO2 emissions. NOx emissions in the Amazon biome are normally associated with deforestation and land use change, which is not included in other activities. The exclusion of NOx-CO2 gases is conservative because a decrease of deforestation is associated with a decrease of NOx-CO2 gases.

Any future FREL submissions should incorporate the inclusion of NOx-CO2 gases as an area for future technical improvement to maintain consistency with the GHI inventory included in the national (long-term) GHG inventory.

### Brazil's response
Following this recommendation, Brazil presented in FREL C (2018) an analysis of the impact of non-CO2 emissions of carbon dioxide (CO), methane (CH4), nitrous oxide (N2O) and NOx for prior 2017. The results show that the inclusion of these gases would increase the total emission by Gg: CO 16 Gg; CH4 139 Gg, N2O 58 Gg and NOx 129 Gg respectively. Non-CO2 emissions from deforestation in the Amazon biome are not available at this time. The inclusion of these gases would increase the emission estimates to include non-CO2 emissions would not be possible at this point.

Estimation of emissions from fire resulting from deforestation is expected to be improved in the next historical period (2018-2020). This will allow Brazil to provide a more accurate FREL for the biome when there is a significant source of emissions if consistency of the time-series can be assured at the development stage.


---
**Page 31**
---

For the construction of the FREL of the Amazon biome, Brazil followed the methodological guidance contained in the IPCC Good Practice Guidance for Land Use, Land-Use Change and Forestry (IPCC, 2003) as a basis for its REDD+ RBP funding proposal. The same methodology used in the Second National GHG Inventory is considered for all land-use categories.

For any land-use conversion occurring in a given year, GPG LULUCF considers both the carbon stocks in the biomass and soils that are emitted immediately after the conversion and those that remain in the same land-use category over inventories by applying explicit stock changes. Brazil adopted approach 3 for representing emissions from all land conversions and lands remaining in the same land-use category.

### Issues related to applying IPCC guidance
Please mention any significant issues related to the alignment of the Brazilian REDD+ RBP funding proposal with the IPCC Good Practice Guidance for LULUCF, including any differences between the two. Please also indicate whether there are any other issues related to the alignment with the methodologies of the IPCC GLR/GHG that were raised in the TA report and whether any of these issues have been resolved.

### Area for technical improvement
The TA report considered the treatment of emissions from dead wood (i.e., the inclusion of this post or the provision of more information justifying its omission) as an area for future improvements. Brazil's response: Considering that the main difference with the inclusion of dead wood is less than 10%, Brazil considers that this post is not a significant source of emissions. Furthermore, by not including this post, Brazil would be in compliance with the FREL.

### Additional information needed to refine FREL/RBP
(xiii) Reference period for the FRELRBP: Please indicate the reference period (number of years) applied for the corresponding FRELRBP.

The reference period used by Brazil in the construction of the FREL for the Amazon biome was 1996 to 2010, a total of 15 years.

### If previous reference level submitted
Please indicate whether any previous reference level applied to the current FRELRBP compared with the previous one and the current one. Describe any adjustments made to the current FRELRBP compared with the previous one.

The FREL for the Amazon biome (FREL A and B) was the first reference level submitted to the UNFCCC in the context of result-based payments. This submission, in June 2014, came right after the rules and procedures for REDD+ were adopted by the Conference of the Parties in December 2013 at COP 19. No previous reference levels had been submitted to the UNFCCC by any Party.

Even though REDD+ UNFCCC decisions do not require developing countries to be conservative in their approach to construct their FRELRBP, Brazil's FREL for the Amazon biome included an adjustment discovered during the construction of the FRELRBP. This adjustment was made after the reference level adjustment was included in Brazil’s FRELRBP methodology to reflect the progress made in the implementation of policies and measures to reduce deforestation in the Amazon region since 2013.

### [ix] Uncertainty in forest cover and biomass estimates

The country has provided information on aggregated annual emissions or removals for the Amazon biome, considering national capabilities and circumstances, including uncertainties related to the data used. The data should be accompanied by a description of any sources. If applicable, include the process implemented to minimize systematic and random errors.

### The AT commented that the information provided in the revised FREL submission on ongoing efforts to estimate forest cover and biomass is insufficient.

There is a level of uncertainty associated with the activity data used for the construction of the FREL for the Amazon biome. The definition of deforestation adopted for PRODES and maintained in the FREL (i.e., permanent loss of tree cover) may be different from that used by other sources, such as satellite imagery of high spatial resolution (up to 30 meters). This can lead to differences between the forest area estimated using the FREL and the forest area estimated using other methods. The uncertainty in deforestation estimates is also related to the quality of the data used for the construction of the FREL.

The literature on uncertainties indicates that the largest uncertainties for REDD+ activities relate to the spatial distribution of biomass and to the spatial pattern of forest cover change, rather than to total global or national emissions.

### Uncertainties associated with the carbon map may arise from the atomistic equation

It is recognized that the application of the atomistic equation developed for a specific area of the Amazon biome may increase the uncertainty in the estimation of biomass. For example, the atomistic equation used by Hugelius et al. (2009)1 tested three atomistic equations previously published and developed for dense forest in Central Amazonia. The results indicated that the atomistic equation developed specifically for dense forest in Central Amazonia was more accurate than other equations. However, the atomistic equation developed for CA land to overestimate the biomass of the smaller trees in South America and underestimate the biomass of the larger trees.

The literature on uncertainties indicates that the largest uncertainties for REDD+ activities relate to the spatial distribution of biomass and to the spatial pattern of forest cover change, rather than to total global or national emissions.

### Other sources of uncertainty

Other sources of uncertainty in the REDD+ estimates include: (1) data collection; sampling design; (2) aggregated forest types; (3) rules used to estimate the carbon density of the forest types per RADAMBRASIL. Some of these uncertainties are related to the use of different methods and definitions for the same instance, as collected under strenuous circumstances in the 1970s, by different teams. Also, by that time, the technology was not as advanced as it is today, which may have led to some differences between the diverse forest types in the Amazon forest classes; may also generate uncertainties, but these are diffuse.

1 EDWARD T.M., SANTOS, M.C.; SAATCHI, A.S.; BACCHIO, G.P.; NAPPY, F.R.; SCOTT J. GOZET, N.; LIMA H.; and BRASIL, R.J. Mapping the Amazon forest biome using Landsat imagery and a hierarchical-ecological maps (2013).

2 RIGELMAN, L.M., FEARNside, P.L., NELSON, B.W., BARBOSA, R.J.R., KEDER, W.E. 2008. Estimation of forest biomass in the Brazilian Amazon: New atomistic equations and adjustments to biomass from wood volume.

3 CHAMBERS, J.G., SANTOS, J.; RIBEIRO, R.J., HUGHES, R.L. 2001. Tree damage, atomistic relationships, and aboveground net primary production in central Amazonian forest. Forest Ecology and Management 153: 73-84.

A paper published in 2014 highlighted the influence of the use of different biomass maps for the Amazon biome on carbon emission calculations due to land cover change in recent years and in future scenarios. This was based on the comparison between the RADAM data (1:1,000,000) used by FERL (MCT 2010) and Baccini et al. (2012). Some results indicate that the map used in the FREL (MCT 2010) and that from RADAM are not consistent with each other.

The paper also states: "It is important to note that the methodology used in the Second National GHG Inventory, based on the RADAM data resulted in a significant underestimation of deforestation rates. This was due to the differences between adjacent surveyed areas and regions (corresponding to different RADAM data sets) within the map."

It concludes that "the methodology used to construct the carbon map, based on the RADAM data (1: 1,000,000)" resulted in large differences in biomass with respect to other maps, and large changes in biomass over time. The authors also note that the use of different versions of the same map can lead to significant variations in the results.

The REDD-plus decision under the UNFCCC value the continuous update and improvement of relevant data and information over time. Work is underway as part of the process of development of Brazil's first NFI to reduce these uncertainties. Brazil will contribute to improve the data for the construction of national FREL.

The Amazon Fund baseline (2014)

The Amazon Fund REDD+ mechanism created by Brazil in 2008 (Federal Decree n°5572/2008) raise donations for non-reimbursable investments in efforts to prevent, monitor and combat deforestation, as well as to promote sustainable land use. The Amazon Fund is a public-private partnership managed by BNDES: the Brazilian Development Bank, which is responsible for raising and investing funds. Donors of the Amazon Fund are the governments of Norway and Germany, and the Brazil company Petrobras.

The Amazon Fund has a Guidance Committee - COFA, responsible for establishing guidelines and monitoring the results obtained, and a Technical Committee - CTFA, in charge of carrying out the calculations made by the Brazilian Institute of Geography and Statistics (IBGE) and the Brazilian National Institute of Space Research (INPE). GHS estimates these emissions using a baseline and method (see Table 10 for more information) that were bilaterally agreed upon between Brazil and Norway. The methodology used to estimate deforestation increments for the MRY for REDD+ plus were agreed upon in 2013 with the adoption of the Warsaw Framework for REDD+.

The Amazon Fund baseline utilizes a conservative carbon content and does not correct for cloud coverage It is based on an average carbon content rather than the emission factor being derived from the carbon map. The Amazon Fund baseline uses the same methodology as Brazil's REDD-FREL, which was adapted from GHS Inventory and Brazil's REDD-FREL to the UNFCCC. The Amazon Fund method to estimate results from reducing emissions from deforestation is based on the MRY for REDD+ plus, which was agreed upon in the Warsaw Framework for REDD+ in 2013. This conservative approach adopted for the Amazon Fund through the COFA and CTFA allows Brazil to communicate and inform the public about the climate change mitigation impact of reducing deforestation.

The idea was to have a proof of concept of the potential impact that REDD+ plus could have as a finance mechanism, which would be used in the future. The results were very successful showing how effective REDD-plus can be. The general concept of this bilateral agreement is to use the MRY for REDD+ plus as an input into the Warsaw Framework for REDD+ plus.

Table 10 provides a detailed comparison of the elements used in the Amazon Fund baseline and Brazil's REDD+ reference level for the Amazon biome.


| Elements | Amazon Fund baseline vs. Brazil's FREL to the UNFCCC | Brazil's FREL for REDD-plus under the Warsaw Framework |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Area | Legal Amazon region (271,433 km²) | Amazon biome (4.9 million km²) |
| REDD+ action | Reducing emissions from deforestation | Reducing emissions from deforestation |
| Activity data | PRODES Annual deforestation rate* | PRODES Adjusted deforestation increments** |


*See detailed explanation in Box 3 of this funding proposal: The PRODES methodology to estimate observed deforestation increments is based on the annual deforestation rate. The annual deforestation rate and associated carbon emissions are adjusted backwards.
**See more details in Box 3 of this funding proposal: The use of the adjusted deforestation increments to estimate gross deforestation area and associated gross emissions is appropriate for the purposes of REDD+, since, when using PRODES data, the adjustment of deforestation increments due to deforestation under clouds could result in an underestimation of the annual emissions.

The LULUCF experts noted that Brazil has overall ensured consistency between the FREL and the estimation of results from the implementation of the activity reducing emissions from deforestation in the Amazon biome during the period 2011–2015.

(a) Using the same methodology for the calculation of the baseline date on gross deforestation of primary forests, in particular the single forest monitoring system (the PRODES), which defines deforestation as an area with a minimum diameter of 30 m and a minimum length of 80 m. The PRODES also assesses the deforestation area for each year; using the same maximum mapping unit (625 ha) and using a equally single forest monitoring system to identify deforestation.

(b) Using the same methodologies in terms of greenhouse gas factors, the same carbon map and the same classification of primary forest of the Brazilian Amazon biome into 22 different forest types with different carbon stocks depending on forest type and location.

(c) Including the same three carbon pools: above-ground biomass, below-ground biomass and litter; (d) Including the same gases: CO₂ only;

(e) Covering the same area of primary forests: the Brazilian Amazon biome of approximately 4.197.000 km²;

(f) Using the assumption that all carbon from the three carbon pools is lost in the year of the deforestation and that the carbon stock is initialized to zero at the start of the FREL (2015);

(g) Using a forest definition that is fully consistent with the forest definition used for the construction of the FREL.

In view of the above, the LULUCF experts concluded that the quality presentation of the results from the implementation of activity reducing emissions from deforestation for the years 2011–2015.

Beyond the transparency of information and data used in the construction of the FREL and of the REDD+ technical Annex to the BUR, the LULUCF experts also noted that Brazil provided a description of the forest monitoring system and the methodology for the calculation of deforestation rates. The results of the reporting and verification of the results in the technical annex, together with weblogs for accessing further information on the data used, the methods applied, and the roles and responsibilities of the agencies and institutions involved were transparently reported.

The LULUCF experts noted that the estimation of results from the implementation of the activity reducing emissions from deforestation and forest degradation (REDD+) in Brazil was based on a combination of established sources of data and a transparent and consistent methodological approach, as with the assessment for 1990-2010.

Brazil has a long-term, credible, accurate, continuous and verifiable historical time series for annual gross deforestation in the Legal Amazon (and, consequently, for the Amazon biome). PRODES is part of a system that monitors forest cover change on an annual basis. The system uses satellite imagery to detect changes in areas of primary (natural) forest in the Legal Amazon through use of satellite imagery. Since 2003, INPE began to generate maps of deforestation polygons using the same methodology and with the same resolution as the maps with the observed deforestation polygons, ensuring complete transparency of the deforestation estimates.

Approximately 215 Landsat 5/7 scenes (or similar data, as for instance, from ObserversCC, ResourcesatLSISD and CMRDC) are annually available and each scene is accompanied by the respective mapping of deforestation polygons. The images are processed in a multi-task, multi-processing environment.

INPE continuously improves its tools to better manage large-scale projects such as PRODES. In the latest development, the TerraMaxim system, that manages the entire workflow of PRODES, annually storing approximately 100 terabytes of data. This system allows for the parallelization of tasks and the pre-processing and enhancement of images for subsequent analysis in a multi-task, multi-processing environment.

PRODES, which for decades has generated reliable deforestation data for the Amazon, is key to the context of expanding land cover monitoring to other Brazilian Biomes. The Project, open coded and evaluated by national and international experts, ensures the quality of the data used by Brazil on its REDD+ plus submissions.

The LULUCF experts commended Brazil for its significant long-term efforts to build up a robust NRFS that can provide data for transparent estimation of emissions from deforestation.

(v) Accuracy of the data: Please provide any additional information that would help us understand the information contained in the Technical Analysis Report in relation to the accuracy of the data and information provided in the document. The TATR has reviewed the data and information presented in the Technical Analysis Report and made no material changes to the data or information. The TATR also made no significant issues raised in the Technical Analysis Report and whether these were raised and resolved in the Technical Analysis Report. The TATR has not identified any other issues with the data or information contained in the Technical Analysis Report, that were not material to the accuracy of the data on results and that could not be resolved due to limitations of the data.

The TATR of Brazil’s REDD+ Technical Annex for results achieved in 2014 and 2015 concluded that these results are accurate to the extent possible, based on the activity data, emission factors, methodologies and assumptions used, which are consistent with the assessed FREE for the Amazon biome.

The LULUCF experts noted that Brazil uses a well-calibrated approach for accurately tracking gross deforestation and forest degradation over time. This ensures that any gross deforestation of primary forests is included in the estimates.

The increments of deforestation were adjusted to avoid the overestimation or underestimation of emissions from deforestation, which could be caused by the use of satellite images with low resolution or cloud, enhancing the accuracy of the data.

Brazil explained that it is possible the results for 2011-2015 will see some adjustment due to cloud-covered areas and limitations in images after 2010. The risk of such adjustments is nevertheless minimal because it now uses new technology with more images and a longer observation window. The LULUCF experts commended Brazil for the clarification provided and assuring Brazil to

# REDD-plus RBP FUNDING PROPOSAL

## GREEN CLIMATE FUND | PAGE 42 OF 97

### Provide such clarification on adjustment of estimates for cloud-covered areas in future FREL and results submissions.

The LULUCF experts noted that Brazil continues to develop its NFMs and made remarkable progress in the areas identified in the FREL assessment for future improvement, including the development of new carbon maps, data on deadwood and non-CO2 gases and ongoing work on forest degradation.

The LULUCF experts commended Brazil on the progress reported in these areas identified for technical improvements. They also welcomed the submission of the draft methodology for the REDD+ MEL and results submissions to enhance transparency and improve the accuracy of the estimates.

More information on accuracy of data and information used on the REDD+ estimates for the Amazon biome can be found in the annexes.

### Area for future improvement

The LULUCF experts note that Brazil could consider the continuation of the ongoing efforts in developing the NFMs to improve estimates of forest degradation to improve the accuracy of the REDD+ results.

Brazil's response: Brazil continues its efforts to progress discussions on the best tools to generate estimates of forest degradation that are as accurate as possible. The major challenge of monitoring and estimating forest degradation is the lack of information about the number of trees removed, their species and emissions (loss in the ability to accurately assess the changes of carbon stocks in the areas affected by deforestation). In addition, there may be a significant difference between the actual state of the land (where few trees are removed) to very high (when, most likely, the land will be deforested at some point in time). As such, Brazil is currently working on developing an approach that can account for this.

The Working Group of Technical Experts on REDD+ has worked intensively over the past few years and which with the support of the UNFCCC, provided a draft methodology for FREL submissions to the UNFCCC in the future.

### B.2.2 Additional criteria related to the achieved results

#### (vi) Uncertainties: Explain how Brazil will address uncertainties or acknowledge uncertainties of the REDD+ project national capabilities and circumstances. Include the percentage of aggregate uncertainty that can be attributed to systematic errors and sources, if applicable. Indicate the process presented to arrange systematic and random errors.

As part of the TA process, Brazil provided additional information related to uncertainty estimation considering item 40CP, paragraph 16.

Brazil noted that the uncertainties in data used for REDD-plus submissions related to the Amazon biome are undertaken during the mapping phase and for the biomass values. The deforestation data on the Amazon biome were subject to quality control by the Brazilian government.

Uncertainties associated with biomass values are usually related to the absence of studies on some phytophysiologies (relating the use of values from other biomes or phytophysiologies with similar climate and soil conditions) and limitations in the spatial representativeness of the secondary data obtained from the scientific literature.

The LULUCF experts concluded that the REDD-plus results presented by Brazil on its 2nd BUR (including years 2014 and 2015) are accurate to the extent possible.

**Area for future improvement:** The LULUCF experts commented Brazilian for sharing this information on ongoing work to reduce uncertainties in biomass values. This will allow Brazil to provide more robust estimates to efforts to provide uncertainty estimates as encouraged in decision 17/C.P.8, annex, paragraph 24.

Brazil's response: Work is underway to address and reduce uncertainties and this process will contribute to the reporting of results for the years 2016-2019. The information on ongoing work to reduce uncertainties was presented in Brazil’s most recent FSB submission to the UNFCCC (section 8.4, “Accurate Information”). It is noteworthy that Brazil has already provided this information as part of its response to the comments made by the LULUCF experts.

The REDD-plus results presented for Brazil are based on biomass values estimated using secondary data and will also result in the reduction of uncertainties associated with the use of secondary data for REDD-plus technical submission to the UNFCCC.


### (iii) Preventing double payments

Preventive measures against claims that have been, or are expected to be received from other sources of funding for results recognized by the country for the same area and for the same period, for which the country is applying payments from the GCF:

- Indicate in the table of results whether amounts paid or expected to be paid (including the year(s), results value in ICOS, quantities for which payments were received/expected to be received) are subject to any type of analogy agreement.
- Provide sufficient assurances that the results that have been paid, or are expected to be paid for by other sources (or are under any type of analogy agreement) been excluded from the volume offered for REDD-plus results.
- Provide a description of measures to ensure that if the results paid by the GCF will not be transferred, offered for future payment or otherwise used (for example as offsets) and information on how the results will be accounted for in the country’s national accounts.
- Provide information on how different financing contributed to the achieved results.


### Avoidance of double payments for the same REDD-plus results

Tables 12 and 13 provide detailed information on payments received by Brazil for results achieved reducing emissions from deforestation in the Amazon biome in 2014 and 2015. This information is available on GCF’s website (https://www.greenclimate.fund/financial-transparency). The tool described below can be used to inform and prevent double counting of results and double payments for the same results. This tool is currently being tested by Brazil.

Table 12 shows that only 4% and 5% of the total REDD-plus results achieved reducing emissions from deforestation in the Amazon biome in years 2014 and 2015 respectively have been paid for. About 95% of the total REDD-plus results achieved reducing emissions from deforestation in the Amazon biome in these two years are not yet accounted for.

The total amount of REDD-plus results that Brazil will offer to the GCF corresponds to only 2% of the total results achieved reducing emissions from deforestation in the Amazon biome in 2014 and 2015. These payments will be discounted from the 40% of total results allocated to the Federal Government by the CONAFOR for the years 2016-2019.

The large amount of total REDD-plus achieved by Brazil assures that there is no risk of double payments. Even after receiving payments from the GCF, Brazil would still have more than 90% of its total REDD-results from 2014 and 2015 available for other payments.

The payments received by Brazil were part of bilateral agreements (Amazon Fund and REMI) signed with donors using methods to estimate the REDD+ results that are different from Brazil's FREL for the period 2014-2015. The new phase of REDD+ results-based payments through the Amazon Fund agreed upon under the UNFCCC. The details about these methods are presented in section B.2.1 (xvi) of this proposal.

The payments made to the Amazon Fund were discounted from the 40% limit assigned to the Federal Government for funding by CONAREAD® Resolution n. 8/2017. The payments made to Acre as part of the REMI Program with the State of Acre, are not subject to this discounting rule under the same Resolution. Moving forward, technical adjustments are being made in coordination with donors, to fully separate the REDD+ results-based payments from the FREL.

The new phase for REDD+ results-based payments through the Amazon Fund (2015 to 2020) does not include new payment years for 2014 and 2015. Hence, to the Amazon Fund only pay for the results achieved in 2016 and beyond as per the agreement between Brazil and the donors under the UNFCCC.

In the case of the REMI Program with the State of Acre, one more payment is scheduled for the results achieved in 2015 and the retirement of emission reductions from 2014 and 2015. This will be discounted from the other 2% that were allocated to Acre by the CONAREAD® Resolution n. 8/2017, which is not part of this proposal.

For more details on REDD+ results-based payments and the bilateral agreements signed so far, see Table 13.
