## Measures to ensure that the results paid by the GCF will not be transferred, offered for future payment or otherwise used (for example for offsets) and information on how the results proposed for payment are calculated.

The CONAREDD+ is responsible for authorizing eligible entities access to REDD-plus result-based payments achieved by Brazil and recognized by the UNFCC. So far, only the States of Acre and Mato Grosso have received such payments. The Brazilian government has also authorized the Amazon Fund to receive result-based payments for REDD-plus (Resolution n° 15 of December 7, 2017). The Brazilian development Bank (BNDES) is responsible for disbursing these payments. In order to ensure that the results paid by Brazil are not used in any other way than as intended, the CONAREDD+ created the CONAREDD+ (Federal Decree n° 859/2015). The Amazon Fund was already authorized to receive result-based payments for REDD-plus.

This means that according to national legislation on REDD+, only four entities will receive REDD-plus results-based payments in Brazil: the State of Acre, the State of Mato Grosso, the MMA and the Amazon Fund. These payments are not transferable or offered for future payment. The result-based payments are no longer available for payments. The Secretariat of the UNFCC is also informed of these payments which are also published in the Hub Brasil.

Since there is no transfer of results/emission reductions to the entities that paid for it, there is no need to track these results through serial numbers. The results that have been paid for, are simply discounted from the total results achieved by Brazil and recognized by the UNFCC. The only information that will be tracked is the entity that received the payment. This information per entity and per result is presented in the Hub Brasil website.

The Amazon Fund complies in a transparent and complete manner all the information about the donations received, including the details of each donation. More detailed information on the donations received by the Amazon Fund are normal, nontransferable, nonnegotiable, and they shall not grant ownership rights or any kind of benefit to the entities that made the about with the agreements with donors can be found in the Info Hub Brasil and in the Amazon Fund website.

In the near future, all diplomas recognizing payments received for REDD-plus results achieved by Brazil will be issued by the MMA. The MMA is responsible for issuing these diplomas. The CONAREDD+ establishes that the MMA, as the Executive Secretariat of the CONAREDD+, will (Vi) issue diplomas recognizing payments received for REDD-plus results achieved by Brazil. This measure also applies to REDD-results-based payments and their respective diplomas may not be used, directly or indirectly, to fulfill mitigation commitments of other countries to the UNFCC. Article 2 states that:

* As per RPM agreement with the State of Acre, half of this total results have been effectively paid for and half has been recognized by the UNFCC. The remaining results are considered paid for and no longer available for new payments. For more information, see [Detailed information on aspects](http://hub.brasil.org) management, etc.

The document refers to item V of the caput of art. 4th will be nominal and non-transferable, will not generate credits, and therefore, will consume the amount equivalent to the payment by result and can be consulted on the MWA website.

Furthermore, Brazil emphasized in its Nationally Determined Contribution (NDC) to the UNFCCC and its Paris Agreement that any carbon sink or source of greenhouse gases located within Brazilian territory will be subject to prior and formal consent from the Federal Government. Brazil will not recognize the caput of art. 4th for activities implemented on Brazilian territory that have been acquired through any mechanism, instrument or arrangement established outside the Convention, as Kyoto Protocol or a Paris agreement.

Article 5, paragraph 2: The Parties mentioned in Article 2 that have interest to raise resources using existing modalities of the Green Climate Fund (GCF) are invited to submit a request to the GCF Board for this Resolution to apply for eligibility, that they are aware that this funding is limited to a modality of finance. Article 5, paragraph 3: The Parties mentioned in Article 2 that have interest to raise resources using existing modalities of the Green Climate Fund (GCF) are invited to submit a request to the GCF Board for this Resolution do not generate, to the Parties mentioned in Article 2, the right to internationally transfer these results. Article 5, paragraph 4: The Parties mentioned in Article 2 that have interest to raise resources using existing modalities of the Green Climate Fund (GCF) are invited to submit a request to the GCF Board for this Resolution for demonstrating the fulfillment of Brazil's Nationally Determined Contribution to the Paris Agreement. Article 5, paragraph 5: In case of compliance with the obligations established in the previous paragraph, the Parties mentioned in Article 2 that have interest to raise resources using existing modalities of the Green Climate Fund (GCF) are invited to submit a request to the GCF Board for this Resolution for results-based payments agreements of the terms of this Resolution and other norms established by the COMARES+.

Article 5, paragraph 6: In case of non-compliance with the obligation established in the previous paragraph, the interested Party will be considered ineligible by the COMARES+ to access REDD+ results-based payments.


Resolution n.° 7, of July 4th, 2017: Defines the eligibility rules for access to direct funding for results-based payments.

Article 6: The Amazon States or federal entities eligible shall have full legal responsibility for managing and investing in projects that generate REDD+ results-based payments agreements with the COMARES+, providing accounting information. 

Note: Particularities of the roles of federal entities eligible shall inform in a clear and concise manner the roles and attributions of all entities directly involved in fundraising and executing the results-based payments agreements.

According to the Annexes of this Resolution, entities applying to receive the approval of the COMARES+ and for the execution of the results-based payments agreements with the COMARES+, shall inform whether or not the funding for REDD+ results-based payments achieved by Brazil represent exclusively a modality of finance that is consistent with the terms of this Resolution and other norms established by the COMARES+.

Resolution n.° 8, December 1st, 2017: Defines the guidelines for use of resources and monitoring of REDD+ results-based payments agreements.


Article 6: Results-based payments agreements shall be submitted to the COMARES+ after their formulation.


Finance that directly and indirectly may have contributed to the achievement of REDD+ results in years 2014 and 2015

It is impossible to attribute specific REDD+ results achieved in different years to specific sources of finance. A variety of finance sources, both national (federal, state and municipal level public budget), NGOs, private sector) have contributed directly or indirectly to the Brazilian government efforts to reduce deforestation.

These resources are difficult to track since the sources do not always report this information to the Federal Government. Brazil has been working on accounting methodologies to estimate the total amount of climate finance received by Brazil for REDD+ results-based payments agreements and other modalities of finance used to compile climate finance information for its BURs. As indicated in Figure 9, in the last five years, the relative contribution of international finance to combating deforestation in the Amazon biome has increased. This was due to an economic crisis that started in 2014 and led to cuts in federal budget spending. The Brazilian government has been increasingly involved in actions related to the prevention and control of deforestation in the Amazon biome, followed by an increase in deforestation rates (more details in the sections below).

Source: MMA, 2018, based on the Ministry of Planning and Budget Multi-year Budget Plan/PPA, 2017 and the Amazon Fund, 2017.

To maintain the low rates of deforestation in Brazil, high and continuous investments are needed, to support command and control operations on the ground (including teams, helicopters, vehicles, technology, etc.), the implementation of better data (NIFS, Embrapa, etc.) and the implementation of positive incentives mechanisms created. In this context of economic crisis and budget cuts in all areas of the federal budget spending, climate finance becomes even more essential. #### C.1. Cancon safeguards
Please provide any additional information that supplements the information included in the "Summary of safeguards" that allows understanding how each of the safeguards has been designed and implemented, and how they have been generated in a way that ensures transparency, consistency, comprehensiveness and effectiveness.

(i) Those actions complement or are consistent with the objectives of national forest programs and/or other relevant policies.

Comments:
From a strategic level, the National Policy on Climate Change and Brazil's Forest Code provide the overarching guidelines for Brazil’s REDD+ actions. At the tactical-operational level the main instrument to consolidate these guidelines is the Programa de Desenvolvimento da Amazônia (PDDA) in the Legal Amazon (PDDAM is the acronym in Portuguese). The PDDAM is an overarching suite of programs that includes the National Forest Policy, the National Strategy for Sustainable Development in the Legal Amazon (PDDAM is the acronym in Portuguese), and the National Program for REDD+ actions in the Amazon region with national and sub-national forest programs, international conventions and agreements, and other relevant policies. The PDDAM is coordinated by the Brazilian ministries and work along 3 main axes, including land tenure regularization and land use planning, monitoring and control of deforestation and forest degradation, and sustainable development.

Brazil also has their own state-level action plans which are similar to the federal level plan to ensure further consistency in REDD+ actions. The states have developed their own state-level action plans with different vectors through a range of actors including the Brazilian Institute for Environment and Renewable Natural Resources (IBAMA), the Amazon Environmental Research Institute (IPAM), the Amazon Biodiversity Fund (ABF), the Community and Family Forest Management Program (PMFC), the National Policy for Territorial and Environmental Management of Religious Lands (PNAATR), the Legal Land Program, the Environmental Conservation Support Program (Bolsa Verde or Green Grant), the Policy


---
**Page 57**
---

# REDD+ RBP FUNDING PROPOSAL

## GREEN CLIMATE FUND | PAGE 50 OF 97

To guarantee Minimum Prices for Socio-biodiversity Products (PGPBMs) and the Amazon Protected Areas Program (APAP), which are implemented by indigenous peoples, local communities, and activities of, which there are over 200, can be found at http://correlaciondesegmentos.org/gm/str.

Brazil's National REDD+ strategy (ENREDD+) is also structured around three main action lines: the first of which is to ensure the effective implementation of the Brazilian Forest Code and other legislation related thereto; the second cementing Brazil’s commitment to a coherent and comprehensive deforestation policy, which supports broader efforts in the country towards sustainable development and poverty reduction; and the third, which relates to the strengthening of Brazil's international commitments under the United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change (UNFCCC), the United Nations Convention on Biological Diversity (UNCBD) as well as the United Nations Declaration on Forests and Forest People (UNDFP). The National REDD+ Strategy for Amazonia (ENREDD+) established through the Presidential Decree 8.579 in 2013, was created with the purpose of coordinating actions to reduce deforestation and forest degradation in the Amazon region, as well as to promote, the integration and synergy among public policies on forests, biodiversity and climate change across administrative levels, including federal, state and municipal governments, civil society organizations, indigenous peoples, and advisory boards. According to the stated objective of the Thematic Advisory Board on Safeguards (CCT-Safeguards), this document provides inputs for the accompaniment of how the Cancun safeguards are being addressed and respected by Brazil.

Through its work, the CCT-Safeguards advisory board has selected relevant policies, programs and information systems that can be used to ensure the implementation of the Safeguard Principles (SP) in order to ensure consistency, coordination and complementarity at the various levels. The substantive challenges related to the SPs are addressed through a series of thematic areas, including: (a) the alignment with the 2nd SSI, including the harmonization and compatibility of national and subnational policies related to REDD+ actions; (b) the integration of the SPs into the National Action Plan on Forests and Biodiversity (NAPFB); and (c) the development of a National Strategy for Safeguards. This is a joint initiative between the Ministry of Environment, the Ministry of Foreign Affairs, and the Ministry of Federal Relations (CCT-Pac), which serves as an additional forum for debate, participation and transparency on the SPs.

For further information on the definition, objectives and details related to the interpretation of safeguard (a) by Brazil in relation to the 2nd SSI, please refer to the document titled "Interpretation of Safeguard (a)" of the 2nd SSI presents an in-depth overview of how safeguard (a) has been addressed and respected by the policies, programs and information systems that are being implemented. Additional information on the SPs' complementarity with CPUNs can be found in the Environmental and Social Assessment (ESA) of PPCDAn found in this Annex to this FP.

## Comments:

The CCT-Safeguards advisory board considers the general objective of this safeguard as follows: Ensuring broad participation in the national REDD+ governance structure by society, compliance with the applicable laws and regulations, and respect for the rights of indigenous peoples and traditional communities under the Federal Constitution. Ensuring this broad participation, fundamental to the transparency and efficacy of forest governance structures, is achieved through the creation of a National Forum on Forests and Biodiversity and information sharing between the Ministry of Environment (who chairs the committee with the Ministry of Foreign Affairs), the Special Secretariat for Climate Change, the Special Secretariat for Technology Innovation and Communications, the Ministry of Agrarian Development (current the Special Secretary for Indigenous Peoples), the Ministry of Justice and Public Security, the National Council of the Office of the Chief Staff of the Presidency. In addition to ensuring the transparency and efficacy of REDD+ actions in Brazil, this safeguard also seeks to ensure that the rights of indigenous peoples and traditional communities under Brazilian civil society. In addition, the composition of the CONARED is currently being revised to entail participation by representatives from the Ministry of Environment, the Special Secretariat for Climate Change, and the National Council of Indigenous Peoples. This safeguard also explicitly recognizes the importance of indigenous peoples, traditional peoples and communities in the design and implementation of REDD+ actions, as well as their role in monitoring and assessing policies. This is accomplished by ensuring the CCT-Safeguards advisory board not only includes experts on biodiversity, environmental monitoring, transparency and forest governance, and public policy


---
**Page 58**
---

among other key topics, but also representatives from indigenous peoples, traditional peoples and communities, civil society organizations, academia, and international organizations, to assess the potential impact matrix, to continuously monitor and report on the effectiveness of the implementation of policies and measures related to REDD+, as well as to provide recommendations for their improvement. These issues have also been developed by this society and the integration with the national level tools has been discussed.

The commission is composed of representatives from various sectors, including government, civil society, the private sector, academia, and international organizations. The commission will work towards the transparent and effective implementation of forest policies, by producing and organizing additional information on the subject matter, as well as to provide recommendations for their improvement. These issues have also been developed by this society and the integration with the national level tools has been discussed.

REDD+ Brazil website consolidates and presents information on the latest REDD+ related news, COMMUNITY FOREST MANAGEMENT (CFM) projects, and other relevant topics. The website is available for the various thematic advisory boards. To further promote transparency, ENEDRO+ information is also available in the Brazilian version of the Amazon Fund website, as well as in the Portuguese version of SISREDD+, (http://www.mma.gov.br/sisreadd) and its omnibus, info Hub Brazil (http://www.infohubbrasil.org/). The website is updated regularly.

As stated above, PPCDAM is the guiding plan for controlling deforestation in the Amazon biome, and the implementation of PPCDAM is subject to both the annual monitoring of actions, as well as general evaluation. The Brazilian government has been working on a new version of PPCDAM since 2013. This version is available and directly informed the design of the third phase. A key recommendation in regards to governance was to establish a national committee for the implementation of PPCDAM, which would include representatives from existing technical boards such as law enforcement and sustainable forest management, among others.

It should also be noted that the Amazon Fund is also subject to regular assessment and audit, with annual accounting and compliance audits publicly available, and an Amazon Fund Activity Report produced annually (as noted in Section 4.1 of the 2nd SOI). The Brazilian government has also established a National BMDHS in order to meet the requirements laid out in Dec. 6/527/08 regarding the supporting actions, guidelines and criteria for the implementation of PPCDAM. # REDD-plus RBP Funding Proposal

## Green Climate Fund | Page 57 of 97

### Comments:

Approximately 450,000 Indigenous Peoples live in the Brazilian Amazon, making up 60% of Brazil's indigenous population and covering over 400 indigenous lands over an expanse of 115 million hectares (over 80% of the total area of the Amazon). The Brazilian Constitution recognizes the rights of Indigenous Peoples to their lands, territories, and resources. Brazil has its own definition of local communities, established in Federal Decree 694/2003, which includes indigenous peoples. These groups are recognized as having a special relationship with nature on a scale and central importance of these groups in accomplishing Brazil's National Development Plan (PND). The recognition of the rights of Indigenous Peoples to their lands, territories, and resources is respected in the context of implementing REDD+ actions in Brazil; it is of central importance. According to Brazil not only does the Brazilian Constitution recognize the rights of Indigenous Peoples to their lands, territories, heritage and territories, as well as the local knowledge associated with the sustainable use of biodiversity, but also Brazil has a National Policy on Territorial and Environmental Management of Indigenous Lands (PNCT). This policy includes prohibition of restrictions on the use of management of territories, emphasis on the right to self-determination, recognition of the rights of Indigenous Peoples to their lands, territories, and resources, including the right to Free, Prior, and Informed Consent (FPIC). Furthermore, Incorporating this knowledge into national law supports the implementation of REDD+ actions in Brazil. The Brazilian Constitution is also supported by an array of national laws and international agreements. The knowledge and rights of indigenous peoples are recognized as a fundamental principle of the Federal Constitution, which is complemented by supporting international and national instruments: Articles 21st and 48 of ADCT; the Federal Constitution; Legislative Decree No. 30/96; Law No. 5.764/71; Law No. 10.601/2002; Law No. 11,287/2006; Law No. 13,123/15; and the United Nations Declaration on the Rights of Indigenous Peoples.

The National Policy for Territorial and Environmental Management of Indigenous Lands (PNCT) and The National Biodiversity Policy (PBN) are two important instruments that guide Brazil's actions in this regard and respecting this safeguard, and contributed to the implementation of the 3rd Phase of PPCDAm (2012-2016). These policies recognize the rights of Indigenous Peoples over their lands, territories, and resources; natural resources found in indigenous lands and territories; ensure integrity indigenous heritage; improvement of quality of life for Indigenous Peoples; and the conservation of biodiversity. The PNCT also seeks to legalize the use of 55 million hectares of state-owned land by granting land titles to some 160,000 inhabitants. This is part of Brazil's commitment to the implementation of the Amazon Biodiversity Law, the ongoing Indigenous Land Titling process, and other efforts to improve land tenure (such as the registration of property rights). The PNCT also seeks to ensure that the use of these lands is in accordance with sustainable development undertakings, not least because of the scale of the Legal Amazon, and the isolation of many communities. More information on the PNCT can be found at: <https://www.mma.gov.br/portal/legislacao/cpct>. The main challenges related to the implementation of REDD+ actions in Brazil are the lack of capacity for the implementation of these actions in the Amazon region, Brazil therefore recognizes the need to govern systems in this regard and to create an environment conducive to the implementation of such actions.

Finally, it is worth noting that knowledge and rights of IPs and traditional communities also integrate a crosscutting matter in other relevant national laws and policies. Particularly relevant is the National Biodiversity Policy (PBN) which recognizes the importance of the sustainable use of biodiversity and its components with the fair and equitable sharing of benefits derived from their use, as well as the associated knowledge. Also relevant is the Access and Distribution of Biodiversity Benefits Act Law 12.895/2013, which seeks to ensure that all parties involved in the use of genetic resources have equal knowledge, and share benefits. It sets the national definition of associated traditional knowledge, ecological services, and biodiversity components, as well as the conditions for access and benefit sharing, with the definition of parameters for prior and informed consent, and the fair and equitable sharing of benefits.

This proposal presents details about how safeguard (c) has been addressed and respected by the policies and initiatives aimed at supporting the full and effective participation of indigenous peoples and local communities in the implementation of the activities eligible for results-based payments under REDD+ in Brazil, as well as the role of these actors in the implementation of the "Catalyzing the Contribution of Indigenous Lands to the Conservation of Brazil's Forest Ecosystem" drawing on the collective knowledge of IPs.

(v) The full and effective participation of relevant stakeholders, in particular indigenous peoples and local communities, in the actions referred to in paragraphs 70 and 72 of 1CP.6.


Comments:

Ensuring the full and effective participation, voice and leading role of stakeholders, including the public and private sector, civil society organizations, traditional knowledge holders, representatives of indigenous peoples and traditional family farmers, to promote shared management and social control in the implementation of REDD+ is a key element for the success of this initiative. This is also reflected in the Brazilian Forest Code (2nd SOI). The governance of the CONAREDD+ and the creation of Thematic Advisory Boards (including the CCZ-Safeguards) are examples of mechanisms that seek to ensure the full participation of stakeholders in the ENERGID+ interactions with both the Climate Change Technical Chamber of the Management Committee of PROINFOR and the Brazilian National Commission for Forests, as well as the implementation of the 2nd SOI. Furthermore CONAREDD+, as well as CCT-Safeguards advisory board itself, include representatives from indigenous peoples and traditional communities. Civil society representatives have also been elected by the Brazilian Forum of Climate Change (FMC), which is a consultative body that includes representatives from indigenous peoples and traditional people and communities in Brazil.

In regards to Fase 2 Prior and Informed Consent, Brazil legislation has provisions that support these instruments, including the Federal Constitution Art. 321; Decree n° 565/2004 promoting ILO Convention 169; Decree n° 78/2007 on the protection of traditional knowledge and associated cultural expressions and traditional knowledge associated with biodiversity. Of particular interest in regard is Chart B of Brazil's Second SOI, which includes a number of conservation protocols developed since 1442 by indigenous peoples and traditional communities.

A groundbreaking initiative in Brazil, that also supports full and effective participation of stakeholders, in particular indigenous peoples and local communities, is the National Program for Indigenous Territorial Land Management Plans (PITAs). The National Policy on Territorial and Environmental Management of Indigenous Peoples (PNTEMI) was created to support the development of PITAs by indigenous groups and other institutions to endorse indigenous-led conservation, natural resource management and environmental protection. PITAs are territorial land management plans developed by indigenous peoples for their lands, covering three main priorities: 1) Territorial control and protection, including the demarcation of indigenous territories; 2) Sustainable use of natural resources for the promotion of food security and income generation, as well as conservation, restoration and sustainable development of ecosystems; and 3) Strengthening the capacity of indigenous peoples to implement the policy plans, by the Ministry of Environment (MMA) and the Brazilian Foundation for Indigenous Peoples (FUNAI). PITAs are also being used in the implementation of REDD+ pilot projects under UNDP Implemented PGTA's in 32 indigenous lands, serving as a pilot phase for policy implementation to be shared throughout the almost 700 indigenous lands in serving as a pilot phase for policy implementation to be shared throughout the almost 700 indigenous lands in Brazil.

### (v) That actions are consistent with the conservation of natural forests and biodiversity diversity

Ensuring that the actions referred to in paragraph 70 of this document are not used for the conversion of natural ecosystems into systems with low levels of biodiversity (although they may represent a high potential for GHG mitigation), nor into activities that could compromise the conservation of natural forests and their ecosystem services, is consistent with the objective of this safeguard, as defined by the CCT-Safeguards advisory board. It is also important to note that the objective of this safeguard is not only related to the protection of natural forests but also to the conservation of natural vegetation, which provides key ecosystem services. That is, Brazil's definition explicitly values the existing and future potential for biodiversity in all its forms, including genetic diversity, species diversity, and social for those who live in the forest. In addition to carbon benefits, the incentives programs also recognize other environmental services provided by forests such as: 1) Carbon Sequestration; 2) Conservation of Soils; 3) Conservation of Water and Water Services; 4) Climate Regulation.

Traditional knowledge is an important element for the conservation of natural forests. Brazil has a host of other policies and programs, which incentivize the protection and conservation of natural forests as described in the 2nd SBI, including the creation of Protected Areas in the Amazon, encompassing indigenous lands, private reserves, national parks, and other protected areas.

In regard to biodiversity, it is important to note that in response to the recommendations made by CBD Decision XIII/10, the coherence of REDD+ and biodiversity actions is further ensured through the integration of actions related to biodiversity conservation into the project design. The Brazilian government has also developed a National Biodiversity Strategy and Action Plan (NBSAP) based on the guidelines from the National Commission for Biodiversity (with information provided by the Brazilian Biodiversity Information System - SISBI). This plan includes specific measures aimed at the monitoring and conservation of endemic, rare or endangered and high biodiversity value species or ecosystems.

## Actions to address the risks of reversals

### Comments:

Promoting the long-term reduction of GHG emissions within the scope of REDD+ actions, and ensuring consistent and continuous results is considered the principal objective of this safeguard, as defined by the CCB definition. The Brazilian government has taken several actions in order to ensure that these objectives are met, including the implementation of a series of measures aimed at ensuring that the results of REDD+ actions exclude, within the Atlantic Forest Reserve (Serra da Mata Atlântica), which is located in the Legal Amazon, areas with high levels of environmental degradation. The Brazilian government has also taken several actions to ensure that rural landholdings must conserve 80% of their area with native vegetation cover.

The PPCDAN includes a host of actions to monitor, analyze and improve the coordinated actions for maintaining reduced deforestation rates in Brazil. These actions are aimed at identifying the causes of deforestation and the risks of reversals, which leads to iterative implementation of the action plan, and improving the effectiveness of these actions over time.

It should be noted that Brazil has one of the most advanced systems for satellite forest monitoring in the world, the Amazon Deforestation Monitoring Program (PRODES), which is described above as used in the construction of the deforestation data presented in Figure 12. PRODES was developed by INPE and is based on Landsat 7 and CBERS-2, CBERS-2R, Resources-1, and UK-CAMAC, with all PRODES data is publicly available at the Brazilian Institute for Space Research (INPE). The Deforestation Early Detection System (DETER), a satellite-based system that enables frequent and quick identification of deforestation hot spots, was developed by INPE in 2008. DETER has been used to identify areas with high levels of environmental degradation and areas with illegal deforestation activity.

The Environmental Control and Monitoring pillar of PPCDAN was principally responsible for the development and implementation of the actions described above. Recently, Assunção et al (2017) evaluated the effects of the monitoring and law enforcement between 2007 and 2015 on deforestation rates in Brazil. The law enforcement efforts avoided an amount of 23 million hectares of deforestation per year between 2007 and 2015.

The deforestation data (presented in Figure 12 of the 2nd SOI) is the main indicator to assess potential risks of reversals, as described above. The Brazilian government has taken several actions in the 3rd PPCDAN phase (2012, 2013, 2014 and 2015). The recent increase in the deforestation rate observed in 2016 marks a deviation from this trend; however it should also be noted that an increase in deforestation is


---
**Page 63**
---

one single year does not necessarily present a trend of reversal, given the complexity of economic and political factors that influence deforestation rates. These factors can be influenced by external circumstances, such as budget, the approval of specific rules or even commodity prices. After a slight increase in deforestation rates between 2015 and 2016, Brazil reduced deforestation rates again in 2017. This reduction was part in a result of changes implemented after the 4th Phase of PRODES (Brazilian National System for Monitoring Forest Cover). The main challenges related to SOI include the possibility that deforesters are adjusting their practices to monitoring strategies. Assunção et al. (2017) found that, between 2015 and 2016, there was a reduction in the number of alerts issued by PRODES in the relative proportion of areas smaller than 25 ha (first DETER detection), in small, medium and large properties. This suggests that deforestation is occurring on smaller plots, which may be more difficult to monitor. The Brazilian government has been implementing several actions aimed at reducing deforestation and further improving monitoring systems and strategies. Finally, operationalizing REDD+ results-based payments by the Brazilian government will also contribute towards reversing deforestation.

For further information on the definition objectives and details related to the implementation of safeguard (ii) by Brazil are presented in section 4.1 of the 2nd SOI, which also provides details about the approach defined by Brazil for implementing this safeguard. The main challenges related to the implementation of this safeguard include: effective, comprehensive, frequent and accurate monitoring of all relevant areas and promoting compliance with the Brazilian Forest Code. In addition, Brazil is currently implementing REDD+ in other biomes, as established in the ENREDD+. Section 5 of the 2nd SOI presents information about how safeguard (ii) will be implemented by Brazil in the Amazon biome. The main challenges related to the implementation of this safeguard identified by the CCT-Safeguards are: effective and comprehensive monitoring of all relevant areas, including those outside the level of investments in law enforcement actions and other initiatives to reduce deforestation, including through the support for indigenous and traditional peoples.

### Actions to reduce displacement of emissions

The CCT-Safeguards advisory recognizes that the principle objective of this safeguard is promoting legislation and actions, which prevent the implementation of REDD+ activities in one area, having the opposite effect on another. Brazil has been implementing several actions aimed at reducing deforestation by natural and planted forests, accounting for the second largest forested area in the world, and that Brazilian forests are also important to reduce displacement of emissions from other biomes. In order to address deforestation trends, presenting unique technical challenges, Brazil is currently implementing REDD+ per biome, as established in the ENREDD+. Brazil has been investing resources aimed at reducing displacement. In order to address and reduce the displacement of emission to other biomes, Brazil is committed to promoting legislation and actions that prevent the implementation of REDD+ activities in one area having the opposite effect on another.

To address both the risk of displacement between biomes, as well as potential trans boundary displacement to adjacent national territories not yet as advanced in the operationalization of REDD+, Brazil has also invested resources aimed at reducing displacement. Brazil is currently implementing actions related to payments received by the Amazon Fund. Just over 20% of the total resources received through results-based payments have been used for investments in monitoring systems and other initiatives, such as strengthening control systems in other Brazilian biomes and other countries. In this regards, Brazil has invested US$12 million since 2015 to support the Amazon Fund to monitor deforestation, changes in the use of land and forests in other countries in the Amazon basin**.

For more information see http://www.greenclimatefund.org/en/reports/monitoring-Forest-Cover-in-the- National-Socioeconomic-Context-of-Brazil.

Section 4 of the 2nd SOI presents more details about the approach defined by the CCT-Safeguards advisory board to systematically implement REDD+ safeguards in Brazil from the local level up to the national level.

Stakeholder engagement and discussions regarding REDD+ safeguards in Brazil began in 2009, before safeguard implementation under the UNFCCC in December 2015. This process was initiated by non-governmental organizations, engaging social movements, smallholders, private sector actors, environmental NGOs, and indigenous peoples. The process aimed to ensure that the REDD+ safeguards were consistent with the “SBCD: Social and Environmental Principles and Criteria.”

In 2011, the process led to the development of the National Social and Environmental Software in the biodiversity-rich Amazon region, with the participation of several civil society organizations. In 2012, the MMA and the National Confederation of Peasants People’s Federation (CPT) developed a draft summary of the safeguards related to the implementation of REDD+ in Brazil. The workshop resulted in the drafting of the “Guidelines for the Implementation of REDD+ in Brazil: Technical Manual for the Implementation of Safeguards” by the MMA and CPT.

In 2013, the MMA also coordinated a Technical Panel with experts from civil society organizations to develop the guidelines. In April 2014, Brazil submitted to the UNFCCC its SOI on safeguards.

After that, in May 2014, the MMA established the CCT-Safeguards advisory board to coordinate a participatory process for the systematic implementation of REDD+ safeguards in Brazil. This includes the corresponding sections of the SOI and the development of an operational plan for implementing the SBCD+ and reviewing the summary of information on the safeguards, among other activities.

The CCT-Safeguards advisory board when summarizing past consultations and discussions, considered the social principles and criteria as well as the environmental principles and criteria. The board also provided a participatory definition of the safeguards in the Brazilian context by the CCT. This exercise is the basis for all the systematic implementation of REDD+ in Brazil.

The systematic implementation of the safeguards encompasses a large set of laws, policies and programs established in the national context that are relevant to the implementation of the safeguards. The board also provided guidance on addressing and reporting the safeguards. As suggested by the CCT-Safeguards advisory board, the summary of information on the safeguards should be divided into three sections: definition, objective and further details for each of the safeguards, aiming at providing a clear scope for the implementation and monitoring of the safeguards.

It is important to note that these three sections (definition, objective and further details) are equally important and complementary.

C.2 Use of proceeds and non-carbon benefits


C.2.1 General description


Provide a description on how the proceeds will be reinvested in activities consistent with the country’s NDC (national determination contribution) and REDD+ strategy. The information provided should also include how the process will be used in a manner that contributes to the long-term sustainability of biodiversity, including the conservation of forests and other ecosystems.


The EMERGEC was approved in the end of 2015 with the overall objective to contribute to climate change mitigation by eliminating illegal deforestation, promoting conservation and restoration of forest ecosystems and Amazon biodiversity. The project will also contribute to the achievement of Brazil’s NDCs and economic benefits. This will be done through three specific objectives: (i) improving the monitoring and impact assessment of the Amazon region’s forests and other ecosystems; (ii) contributing to the conservation of forests and biodiversity at all levels; (iii) contributing to the mobilization of resources at the scale compatible with Brazil's commitments to mitigate climate change in accordance with Brazil's NAMAs by 2030.


[2] Brazil has 26 states and the Federal District. The Amazon region comprises 9 states.


---
**Page 66**
---

Also, in 2015, Brazil submitted its NDC to the UNFCCC. Brazil’s NDC intends to reduce GHG emissions by 37% below 2005 levels in 2025 and a subsequent reduction of GHG emissions by 43% below 2005 levels in 2030.

Brazil's NDC is economy-wide, based on flexible pathways to achieve its objectives. In the annex of its NDC Brazil outlined the measures that will be implemented for each sector to reach the country’s quantitative targets. With regards to the forest sector, the following measures have been preliminarily identified for the implementation of Brazil’s NDC:

- strengthening and enforcing the implementation of Brazil's Forest Code (Code June 23 [1965/2012]), at federal, state and municipal levels;
- intensifying law enforcement with a view to achieve, in the Brazilian Amazon, zero illegal deforestation by 2030 and compensating for GHG emissions from legal suppression of vegetation by 2030;
- restoring and reforesting 12 million hectares of forests by 2030, for multiple purposes: enhancing carbon sequestration; increasing biodiversity conservation; and tracking systems applicable to native forest management, with a view to curbing illegal and unsustainable practices.

Eliminating illegal deforestation, promoting the enhancement of forest carbon stocks and fostering a sustainable forest economy are common objectives of both Brazil's ENED+ and the NDC. Both the ENED+ and the NDC consider that the implementation of the Forest Code is essential for achieving these goals considered by Brazil as means for the implementation of its NDC. Brazil’s NDC states that “the implementation of the Forest Code will be strengthened through a continuous basis, of adequate and predictable results-based payments in accordance with the relevant COP decisions.”

The results-based payments received by Brazil from the GCF will be contributed to the implementation of the forest sector actions of Brazil's NDC. This project proposal has two main outputs:

1. Development of a pilot of an Environmental Services Incentive Program for Conservation and Restoration of Native Vegetation (Floresta); and
2. Strengthen the implementation of Brazil’s ENED+ through improvements in its governance structure and systems.

These two outputs will contribute to the achievement of the ENED+ overall objective and the country's NDC main objective.

Output 1: Floresta Pilot Program

The Floresta is a new and innovative pilot program that aims to provide incentives for environmental services (EES) in the Legal Amazon region. In accordance with Brazil’s Forest Code, the ENED+ and Brazil’s NDC consider that the implementation of the Forest Code will be strengthened through a continuous basis, of adequate and predictable results-based payments in accordance with the relevant COP decisions.

The Floresta pilot program aims to:

1. provide monetary compensation to incentivize native vegetation conservation and recovery and improvement of ecosystems that generate environmental services (including but not limited to: carbon);
2. prevent the occurrence of deforestation, forest degradation and forest fires through financial incentives;
3. incentivize the conservation and recovery of native vegetation of rural properties; conservation areas and indigenous lands in the Legal Amazon region;
4. promote compliance with the environmental legislation, especially that related to the protection and recovery of native vegetation (Forest Code).

These estimates are based on Brazil’s Second National GHG Inventory which was part of the SNC to UNFCCC.


---
**Page 67**
---

5. offer a financial mechanism to foster the development and implementation of public policies aimed at conservation and recovery of native vegetation.

The target audience for the Floresta+ Pilot Program is comprised of:
- smallholders, according to art. 3°, V. of the Forest Code (Law no. 12.651/2012), up to four fiscal modules
- indigenous peoples and traditional communities
- traditional peoples and communities according to I. or III. d) of decree no. 6.049/2007 (that use their lands for conservation)
- public institutions or agencies (including States and municipalities), civil associations, cooperatives and other non-profit foundations that act in topics related to conservation and recovery of native vegetation.

The prioritization of areas to be selected as beneficiaries for the Floresta+ Pilot program will consider:
- regions with high pressure from deforestation, forest degradation and forest fires;
- priority areas for conservation and the recovery of native vegetation, according to norms defined by the MMA;
- buffer zones in protected areas;
- regions with higher density of small farmers;
- regions where there is a greater presence of indigenous peoples and communities;
- integration with other public policies related to the conservation and recovery of native vegetation.

The Floresta+ Pilot Program will operate through resource distribution modalities such as:
1. Modality I (Forest Conservation): incentives to landowners and land users of rural properties according to the classification of forest areas in accordance with article 3°, V., of Law no. 12.651/2012, with the objective of conserving native vegetation remnants in addition to the legal obligation.
2. Modality II (Forest Recovery): incentives for landowners and land users according to the classification of item V. of article 3° of the Forest Code (Law no. 12.651/2012), with the objective of recovering native vegetation remnants in addition to the legal obligation.
3. Modality III (Supporting Communities): support to associations and representative entities of indigenous peoples and traditional peoples and communities;
4. Modality IV (Incentives for Environmental Services): incentives and arrangements to develop, implement and leverage public policies for conservation and recovery of native vegetation.

Additional criteria and priority areas will be developed and refined in consultation with the relevant stakeholders, including representatives from civil society organizations, local communities, indigenous peoples, state government representatives, federal agencies, non-governmental organizations, among others. Direct payments will be calculated based on the area of native vegetation remnants and environmental liabilities (e.g., deforestation, forest degradation) according to the Brazilian Agricultural Certification System (SAC). Amounts of payment per hectare will be defined in norms to be published by the Project Advisory Committee.

a) the relationship between the preserved native vegetation area and the area of deforested, degraded or burnt native vegetation within the rural property limits;
b) the reference values for each fiscal module;
c) the reference values of other programs of incentives for environmental services, when applicable.

# The REDD+ RBP Funding Proposal

## Forests Pilot Program alignment with the ENEDDO and Brazil’s NDC

As of the time of submission of this proposal, policies and measures to reduce deforestation in the Amazon region are already in place. These policies and measures have been shown that government actions are very effective but also have an intrinsic limit on the fight against illegal deforestation. A high degree of enforcement is required for these policies and measures to be effective.

The Forests Pilot Program will provide a framework for Brazil’s NDC, which will not only reduce deforestation in the Amazon region by 80% compared with business as usual but also provide positive incentives to the people that contribute to forest conservation and enhancement. The Forests Pilot Program will also provide a basis for the REDD+ Plus results-based payments to further expand the results achieved in the Amazon region. This will be the core of this GCF proposal.

The Forests Pilot Program will incorporate conservation and the recovery of native vegetation in accordance with Brazil’s NDC, which is aligned with the Brazilian National Action Plan (NAP) for forests. These forests therefore consider with the ongoing efforts to eliminate illegal deforestation and promote the restoration of degraded areas. The following elements are considered as part of the Forests Pilot Program and are listed as potential activities in the forest sector for the achievement of Brazil’s NDC and national policies.

## Output 2: The implementation of Brazil's ENEDDO

About 43 developing countries have signed and in part already received a total of USD 1.5 billion from the World Bank (IBRD) and the Global Environment Facility (GEF) for the World Bank’s “Forest” and other readiness initiatives to develop all the elements for REDD+ including a national REDD+ strategy (NRS), FERD, ENS, and ENEDDO.

Brazil was part of any international REDD+ funding fund or initiative. All REDD+ elements were developed by the country itself, using its own limited national budget (details presented in section B.2.2 (vii) of this proposal). Brazil has already received USD 10 million from the GCF for the implementation of the NDC through REDD+ results-based payments. Brazil intends to improve its REDD+ elements to strengthen the implementation of the ENEDDO and contribute to a more effective and transparent implementation of the NDC.

The resources recovered by Brazil from the GCF through REDD+ payments will be in part directed to support:

1) Expansion of the forest monitoring system and MRV to include additional REDD+ activities, pools and other elements. This includes the development of a National Forest Monitoring System (NFMS), Forest Monitoring Program, for all biomes, as appropriate. Following the guidance from the Working Group on Forest Monitoring (WGFM) of the UNFCCC, Brazil will develop a NFMS to monitor forest cover change.

2) Development of a tool to monitor and measure the impacts of REDD+ policies and investments and their contribution towards the NDC.

3) Improvement Brazil’s Safeguards Information System for REDD+ (SIS-REDD+) and its own bilateral analysis, as appropriate.

4) Enhancement of the capacities and access of the various stakeholders for participating in the COB and other bodies established under the Brazilian National Action Plan on Climate Change Strategy 2020.

5) South-South Cooperation Program in Forests and Climate Change Designated by the MMA and the Brazilian Agency of Cooperation of the Ministry of Foreign Affairs (ABCMA).

A stronger governance structure and more transparent data and information systems will contribute to the long-term sustainability of Brazil’s NDC. The GCF funding for the Forests Pilot Program will support the implementation of the measures needed in the forest sector for the achievement of the national target indicated in Brazil’s NDC.

The REDD+ plus results-based payments from the GCF will also fund a South-South Cooperation Program in Forests and Climate Change designed by the MMA and the Brazilian Agency of Cooperation of the Ministry of Foreign Affairs (ABCMA).

**Output 1:**

The first six months of implementation of Forestx will be used to conduct extensive stakeholder consultations and social and environmental impact assessments in line with the ESME, in order to establish the project’s baseline. The activities for this output include the selection of the beneficiaries in the Modalities 1, 2, 3 and 4. It includes the establishment and consultation of the Community REDD+ Strategy (CENRED+) and the Forestx Pilot Program. This will also include upgrades in SICAR for registering and monitoring processes; the development of a simplified online platform for project implementation and payments; the selection process of the beneficiaries and projects; the establishment of the contracts.

The direct payments for each household in Modalities 1 and 2 shall be made up to four years during the project, with the first payment being made within six months after the start of the project. The subsequent payments will be based on the monitoring results of forest conservation and restoration. The projects duration is expected to last until 30 June 2024.

**Output 2:**

The activities in this output will cover the collection of project support to enhance the products and processes. The focus of the first year is to support the preparation of the national FREL, and to improve the implementation capacity of the National REDD+ Strategy (ENRED+) and Community REDD+ Strategy (CENRED+) according to requirements for GCF. After that, the Public Policy Mission will review the revision of the National REDD+ Strategy with the objective of ensuring its alignment with the national development plan and the Sustainable Development Goals. The access of the various stakeholders for participating in the CENRED+ and its Consultative Chambers and the South-South Cooperation initiatives are cross-cutting issues that will be implemented during all the project.

## C.2.5 Implementation arrangements:

List and describe the institutions involved in the activities that will be funded with proceeds from this pilot programme.

The Government of Brazil (GOB) through MMA has requested UNDP's assistance for the design and implementation of this Project after a selection process, which showed UNDP's comparative advantage, including its extensive experience in the area of climate change and biodiversity conservation. GOB's presence, its large portfolio of biodiversity, climate change and REDD+ both nationally and globally, and its status as an Accredited Entity (AE) for GCF, makes it a unique partner.

At the request of the GOB, UNDP will therefore serve as both Accredited Entity (AE) and Executing Entity (EE) for this Project. In line with the United Nations Control of External Funds (UCF) there is a clear distinction between UNDP's oversight function as GCF EE, and its role as AE. A firewall will be created between execution and oversight functions.

As an Accredited Entity to GCF, UNDP's overall role is to provide oversight and quality assurance through its Headquarters, Panama Regional Hub and Brazil Country Office. This role includes: (i) project preparation oversight; (ii) project implementation oversight; (iii) project completion and evaluation oversight. It also includes oversight in relation to reporting and knowledge management activities. The Project Board will be responsible for the overall oversight by carrying out objective and independent project oversight and monitoring functions. This role ensures appropriate quality assurance of the project, including its design, implementation, and results.

Independent of the Project Manager and Project Management Unit; therefore, the Project Board cannot delegate any of these responsibilities to UNDP's Panama Regional Hub or Brazil Country Office, nor to UNDP Programme Officer, or M&E Officer, at the Country Office with support from the UNDP’s Global Environment Facility (GEF) Team.

The project will be implemented under UNDP's Direct Implementation modality (DIM). UNDP will be the Executing Entity/Implementing Partner. The Implementing Partner is responsible and accountable for managing the project, including ensuring that all project interventions achieve project outcomes, and for the effective use of the project resources.

As executing entity, UNDP offices will carry out operational and administrative support activities which include the provision of the following services:

Project Board (PB)

The Project Board is responsible for making by consensus or majority management decisions when guidance is required to ensure that the project remains within its specified constraints and objectives. The PB reviews all aspects of project plans and revisions, and addressing any project level grievances. To ensure UNDP's ultimate accountability for the project, the PB will be responsible for ensuring that the project achieves its intended results in terms of management for development results, best value money, fairness, integrity, transparency and effective implementation of the project. The Project Board will also be responsible for making decisions which will result with the UNDP Programme Manager. Specific responsibilities of the Project Board include:

- Provide overall guidance and direction to the project, ensuring it remains within any specified constraints;
- Address project issues as raised by the project manager;
- Provide recommendations on possible countermeasures and management actions to address specific risks;
- Agree on the Annual Work Plan;
- Analyze and discuss the development of the Project activities and recommend changes as required based upon the analysis of the project's progress against the approved work plan and budget policies;
- Discuss and approve the Annual Work Plans ensuring that required resources are committed;
- Approve the Project Manager's performance evaluation, including the quality assessment rating report; make recommendations for the workplan;
- Provide guidance on how to address any exceptional or situational situations where the project manager’s tolerances are exceeded; and
- Discuss and agree upon the lessons learned from the Project and Record first of the Project.

The PB will be composed of UNDP, the Brazilian Agency for Cooperation (ABC) and the MMA and their respective alternate members. The Board can be expanded, upon agreement between the Parties. UNDP will represent the project and will be responsible for ensuring that the project is implemented in accordance with the approved work plan and budget policies. ABC will represent the Brazilian government and will ensure that the project is focused throughout its life cycle on achieving its objectives and delivering outputs that will contribute to Brazil’s development priorities, while also ensuring cost-effective approach to the project, balancing the demands of beneficiary and supplier.

The MMA will be responsible for providing guidance for the technical feasibility of the project and ensuring its implementation leads to the achievement of the project's objectives. The MMA will also provide guidance on how to address any exceptional or situational situations where the project manager’s tolerances are exceeded. In addition, the PB will approve the appointment and responsibilities of a Project Manager who will be responsible for implementing the approved work plan.

The composition of the Project Board will include the following roles:

Executive: The Executive is an individual who represents the main Executing Agency of the project who will chair the Project Board. The Executive for this project is UNDP. The Executive is ultimately responsible for the project and will ensure that it remains within its specified constraints and objectives.

Specific Responsibilities (as part of the above responsibilities the Project Board):

- Ensure that there is a coherent project organization structure and logical set of plans;
- Set baselines to the AWP and other plans as required by the Project Manager.

## Senior Supervisor
The Senior Supervisor is an individual or group representing the interests of the parties concerned which provide funding and/or technical expertise to the project (designing, developing, financing, implementing). The Senior Supervisor must have a thorough understanding of the project. The Senior Supervisor provides guidance regarding the technical feasibility of the project. The Senior Supervisor must have the authority to approve all project activities and ensure that they are consistent with the project’s objectives. Typically, the implementing partner UNDP and/or donors will be represented under this role. However, considering it to be a project of REDD-plus fixed payments, MMA will also perform this role.

* Make sure that progress towards the outputs remains consistent from the supplier perspective.
* Promote and ensure consistency between the project and the point of view of supplier management.

* Contribute supplier opinions on Project Board decisions on whether to implement recommendations or proposed changes.
* Arbitrate, on and ensure resolution of, any supplier priority or resource conflicts.

## Senior Beneficiary
The Senior Beneficiary is an individual or group of individuals representing the interests of those who will benefit from the project. The Senior Beneficiary must have a thorough understanding of the project. The Senior Beneficiary is responsible for ensuring that the solution will meet those needs within the constraints of the project. The Senior Beneficiary monitors progress against targets and ensures that the project meets its objectives in terms of time, cost, quality, and other interests. The Senior Beneficiary for this project are the MMA and the ABC. Specific Responsibilities (as part of the above responsibilities for the Project Board):
* Prioritize and promote beneficial/cost-effective options on Project Board decisions on whether to implement recommendations or proposed changes.
* Sponsoring organization ensures that all data is accurate, complete and unambiguous.
* Implementation of activities at all stages is monitored to ensure that they will meet the beneficiary’s needs in terms of time, cost, quality, etc. and other interests.
* Impact of potential changes is evaluated from the beneficiary point of view.
* Risk management frequencies are monitored.

## Project Assurance
UNDP provides a three-tiered supervision, oversight and quality assurance role involving UNDP staff in Country Offices and at regional and headquarters levels. Project Assurance must be totally independent of the Project Board and the project implementation team. The Project Assurance function is carried out by the Project Management Unit by carrying out objective and independent project oversight and monitoring functions. This role ensures that all project management milestones are managed and completed.

The PCU will be led by the National Project Technical Coordinator (NPTC) and composed of an environmental economist, a social scientist, and two project technical advisors. The NPTC will be MMA staff, while the two project technical advisors will collaborate with the MMA in project technical coordination.

### Project Management Unit (PMU)

A Project Management Unit (PMU) will be responsible for overseeing the day-to-day execution of Project activities, including but not limited to: (i) operational planning, managing and executing the project including direct supervision of project activities subcontracted to specialists and other national partners; (ii) coordinating the management of financial resources and procurement; (iii) reporting on the application of resources and progress towards achieving project outputs and outcomes in accordance with the DNA to the GCF and UNDP including annual reports and any proposals for the adaptive management of the Project; (iv) ensuring consistency between the various project elements and activities provided or funded by other donors; (v) promoting inter-institutional linkages; and (vi) disseminating project results.

The PMU will consist of one Project Manager, Project Technical Advisors (PTA), Administrative Finance Associate (AF), and a Communications Officer. The PTA will collaborate with the MMA in project implementation, channeling PTAs' technical inputs into the MMA's work plan and ensuring consistency between the various project elements and activities provided or funded by other donors.

## C2.6 Non-carbon benefits

Provide information on the non-carbon benefits associated with the implementation of REDD+ activities, highlighting their importance in achieving the long-term sustainability of REDD+ pilot activities and providing evidence to this regard.

The main objective of Brazil's ENERHED is to contribute to climate change mitigation by eliminating illegal deforestation and forest degradation. The project aims at contributing to the achievement of a low-carbon and sustainable forest economy, delivering environmental, social and economic benefits in the Amazon region. Since 2014, Brazil has produced important results in the three pillars of sustainability: economic, environmental and social.

Amazon biomes since 2004 have produced important results in the three pillars of sustainability: economic, environmental and social indicators were extracted from the 2016 SUDAM summary report.


## Environmental benefits

Implementation of REDD+ plus activities in Brazil have contributed to the Aichi biodiversity targets including:

- National objective 5: Brazil has reduced deforestation in the Amazon well over the aim to reduce deforestation by 70%.
- National objective 11: Conservation of 30% of lands in the Amazon through protected areas.


---
**Page 80**
---

For additional information on environmental benefits of REDD+ implementation please refer to Annex XIII.

Beyond biodiversity conservation, PPCS4Ann has also contributed to the enhancement of forest carbon stocks in previously deforested areas. The TerraClass data indicates that on average 22% of the total deforestation area was reforested between 1985 and 2013 (see Table 17). This enhancement of forest carbon stocks, due to reduced pressure on forests since the establishment of the Amazon Fund, has important implications for climate change mitigation; it enhances resilience and produce multiple environmental benefits to local, regional and the global community.

**Economic benefits**

The regional economic growth of the last decade was based on the production and export of raw materials. In 2014, more than 28% of the raw materials exported by Brazil came from the Amazon region, especially soybeans (35%), beef (27%) and timber (26%). The Amazon Fund has supported the development of sustainable roundabout for soy, had a key role to ensure that production and protection were reconciled in the Amazon region.

The reduction in deforestation was combined with an increase in agricultural production in the northern region of Brazil. Decoupling the production of agricultural commodities from deforestation is a major challenge for the Amazon region. The Amazon Fund has supported the development of sustainable agriculture as an opportunity cost rather than a resource. The value of the forests standing is lower than the cleared land. By increasing the productivity and profitability of existing farmland, the Amazon Fund has helped to reduce and control actions, restrictions to access credits in public banks, etc.) the GDR has significantly increased the economic costs associated with opening new forest areas for agriculture.

**Social benefits**

The large reduction in deforestation in the Amazon region through the implementation of REDD+ policies and measures have not negatively affected the social development in the region. Since 2005, the HDI for the Amazon region has been higher than the national average (HDI of the Amazon region was more than 30% between 2005 and 2010; and Rosario was the only state in which the increase was the same as the national average). The GDR has also supported the development of social indicators.

Education levels have improved in the whole Amazon region since 2000 but remained lower than the national average in 2013. The variation in improvement was higher in the Amazon region than in the national average. The number of students per teacher and the literacy rate for adults aged between 15-64 years has also increased on average in the Amazon region more than the national average. The income indicator has also been improved in the Amazon region since 2000, but remained lower than the national average. The Amazon region between 2000 and 2013 was higher than the national average, except for two states, which had similar values to the national average.

**Governance benefits**

REDD+ has contributed to better integrate the governance structures of climate change, forest and biodiversity related policies, seeking to promote consistency and synergies among them at the federal, state and municipal levels. The implementation of the Amazon Fund was a key factor in the success of this proposal, with the aim to contribute to the implementation of Brazil's NDC in the forest sector.

PPCS4Ann 3rd phase (2012-2015) revised the policy governance structure to promote greater integration and coordination between the different actors involved. The new governance model was divided into three circles: Executive, Consultative and Transparency. This sought to facilitate the exchange of information among the actors in a more structured way, creating a coordinating body and the solution of problems and conflicts that may arise between federal agencies and even between states.

## D. Investment Framework

Describe in this section how the proposed REDD+ results-based payment program aligns with each of the objectives and criteria for investment described in Section C. This section should be linked to the relevant items from the second annex.

### D.1 Impact Potential

Describe the potential of the program to contribute to the achievement of the Fund's objectives and results.

Since 2006, Brazil has achieved significant results through reducing emissions from deforestation in the Amazon biome. Between 2008 and 2015, Brazil reduced a total of 6,125,591,727.00 tCO2e of emissions from deforestation and forest degradation (LULUCF) in the Amazon biome over an area of approximately 34 million hectares. This corresponds to half of the total CO2 emissions from all Annex I countries in 2012 (UNFCCC, 2018).

According to data from the Third National GHG Inventory, the LULUCF sector represented 83% of Brazil's net CO2 equivalent emissions for the period between 2006 and 2015. The reduction in deforestation in the Amazon biome had an important impact on Brazil’s national GHG emission profile, as shown in Figure 15.

Because of this significant progress achieved by Brazil to reduce deforestation in the Amazon biome, the forest sector is no longer the highest emitting sector in Brazil.

The PPCDAM, launched in March 2004, has the objective of reducing the rates of deforestation in the Amazon, by implementing actions related to land use and territorial planning, fostering sustainable production activities and environmental monitoring, control and enforcement. The PPCDAM is reviewed periodically with the aim of improving its effectiveness and efficiency. In addition, lessons learned from the actions being implemented and the progress made.

Although PPCDAM 3rd phase made important progress (2012-2015), the main challenge remains deforestation in the Amazon region. The PPCDAM has been implementing actions related to the identification and repression of illegal activities and organized crime, the promotion of a forest-based economy and land-use planning.

For its 4th phase (2016-2020), a new Action Axis was included in PPCDAM aimed to create regulation and economic acts to combat deforestation. In addition to the reduction of gross deforestation, one of the major challenges is the reduction of net deforestation rates. The main objective is that the actions related to the illegal deforestation be effectively eliminated, and policies promoted to reduce legal suppression of native forests.

It is important to note that REDD+ results cannot be directly attributed to a single policy or measure for multiple reasons. From a conceptual standpoint, there is not always a direct and linear relationship between a specific policy or measure and its impact on deforestation rates. This is due to the complex interrelationships of different enabling policies (e.g., inter-institutional coordination) and direct investments made in the field (e.g., subsidies to farmers).

#### D.2. Paradigm shift perspective

### Contextualization of the impact which the REDD-plus activity catalyzed beyond a one-off program investment.

The Brazilian Amazon is home to about 27 million people (PNAD, 2013). Several regional developing programs and the global demand for agricultural commodities have increased the pressure on natural forests over the years, leading to the expansion of cattle ranching, agriculture, and other human uses in forested areas. The PPCZon was created by the Brazilian government through INPE, MCTI, and Embrapa MAPA to track the subsequent use of deforested areas so as to improve the understanding of the dynamics of drivers of deforestation in the Amazon.

Through TerraClass it is possible to understand the dynamics of land use and coverage in the Brazilian Amazon. The results obtained from this tool are presented for the years 2004, 2010, 2012 and 2014). With these results it is possible to make an evaluation of the dynamics of the land use and occupation of the deforested areas in the 10 years of the implementation of the PPCZon.

The greatest challenge faced by the Amazon region is to maintain the ecosystem services provided by the forest while ensuring that the economic development of the region contributes positively to the economy of the region. The REDD-plus policies and measures, specifically PPCAms for the Amazon region, have an important role in this regard. In 2014, there was a significant increase in agricultural output production, maintaining the upward trend in agricultural output in States in the northern region of Brazil in years prior to 2013.

In the last five years (2010-2015), deforestation trends have changed significantly. In the Amazon biome, there is a change in pattern; agricultural output trends are no longer following deforestation trends.

**Figure 17:** Deforestation rates and agricultural output in the Amazon region

![Deforestation rates and agricultural output](image.png)

The success story of the Amazon biome led to emission reduction in the Cerrado biome even before the PPCerrado was created in 2010. Figure 18 shows deforestation trends for the Cerrado biome from 2001 to 2017. The values are biannual averages of deforestation measured by INPE.

In 2004, deforestation rates started to decrease in the Cerrado biome, in the absence of a specific policy to combat deforestation. This was due to the implementation of PPCs in other biomes or positive leakage to the Cerrado biome; the paradigm shift went beyond the Brazilian Amazon.

The paradigm shift triggered by Brazil's experience with the implementation of the REDD+ policies and measures has been a major driver for the development of national forest monitoring systems in several countries in Africa, Latin America and Asia since the 1990s to assist them in the development of their own forest monitoring systems. These experiences have also provided Brazil with lessons learned that can be used as a basis for many countries' REDD+ policies and their technical REDD+ submissions to the UNFCCC.

Brazil has also invested in south-south cooperation initiatives for climate change and deforestation through results-based payments by the Amazon Fund, which allows for the understanding of potential regional displacement effects. Beyond that, the MMA and the Brazilian Agency of Cooperation of the Ministry of Foreign Affairs of Brazil have worked on the development of a South-South Cooperation Program in Forests and Climate Change.

This Proposal aims to finance the development of a South-South Cooperation Program in Forests and Climate Change. The Program will contribute to the achievement of Parties commitments under the UNFCCC and the Paris Agreement, through their NDCs, by strengthening the capacity of developing countries to monitor deforestation, changes in the use of land and forests of other countries.

This proposal includes financing for the development of a South-South Cooperation Program in Forests and Climate Change. The Program will contribute to the achievement of Parties commitments under the UNFCCC and the Paris Agreement, through their NDCs, by strengthening the capacity of developing countries to monitor deforestation, changes in the use of land and forests of other countries.

# REDD-plus RBP Funding Proposal

## Sustainable Development Impact of the Implementation of REDD+ Policies that Lead to Emission Reductions in Brazil

The main objective of Brazil's ENREDD+ is to contribute to climate change mitigation by eliminating illegal deforestation and promoting sustainable forest management. The project aims to create a low-carbon and sustainable forest economy, delivering environmental, social, and economic benefits.

Since 2006, the Amazon has produced important results in these three pillars of sustainability: economic, environmental, and social. This document provides an overview of the project's progress. For more information on economic and social indicators was extracted from the 2015 SUDAM summary report.

### Economic Benefits

The Gross Domestic Product (GDP) of the states in the Amazon region grew between 2005 and 2012, with a share of the federal state of the national GDP has increased from 7.9% to 8.4%. This process needs further fostering so that the Amazon can increase its economic potential in a sustainable manner.

The project aims to improve the quality of life for the local Amazonian population by providing them with better access to education and health services, while also protecting the environment and preserving biodiversity.

Furthermore, the economic potential of the Amazon region includes activities that are not yet adequately accounted for within the GDP, such as extraction, payment for ecosystem services, and part of the family's agricultural production.

In addition, about 200 jobs per year were created for every 1,000 hectares recovered. Most of them for low-income families and small producers. Besides that, 92% of incidences occur in areas with ecosystem change, so forest recovery would bring greater security to the population by avoiding natural disaster.

## Environmental Benefits

In 2015, the country presented its 4th National Report to the CBD. The information in section C.2.6 above highlights the relationship between the progress made by Brazil for the achievement of Aichi Targets and the reduction of deforestation in the Amazon biome promoted by policies such as PPCMAs. Maintaining these efforts is essential, since environmental conservation is the core of this GCF proposal.
