# Social benefits

The large reduction in deforestation in the Amazon region through the implementation of REDD-plus policies and measures has not negatively affected the social development in the region. The information in section 10 shows that the Human Development Index (HDI) for the states of the Amazon region is higher than the rest of Brazil. Education levels have also improved in the whole Amazon region since 2005 but remains below the national average.

The HDI has increased from 0.68 to 0.71, which represents an increase of 4% and is above the national average (in four states above 70%, while the national average was about 60%). Since 2003, the HDI in the Amazon region has improved by more than 5 percentage points compared to other regions.

The income indicator has also seen important progress during that decade. The increase in income in the municipalities of the Amazon region between 2003 and 2010 was higher than the national average. The Figure 19 presents this information in more details.

Even though there has been a reduction in inequality between 2003 and 2010 (from 0.58 to 0.47), as of 2003, this remains an important challenge for Brazil. Comparing the 2003 and the 2010 data, less states in the Amazon region now have levels above 0.55. But the Gini for the state Maranhão remained high.

PPCDAM has been a key factor to improve social development in the Amazon region through the implementation of social policies and projects aimed to provide alternative livelihoods to poor communities and to promote access to education, health services and basic sanitation. The results of this project have been strengthened through the implementation of this project with the support from the result-based payments to be received by Brazil from the GCF.

# D.4. Needs of the recipient

Describe the vulnerability and financing needs of the beneficiary country and population.

Maintaining and further expanding the results achieved reducing emissions from deforestation in the Amazon region is a key objective for Brazil as described in this REDD+ RBP funding proposal. A study shows that the net present value of the net marginal cost of the command-and-control actions to reduce deforestation in the Amazon region between 2008 and 2015 was on average R$ 37 billion (USD $9.4 billion) per year, which corresponds to about 0.1% of the total yearly GDP.

As in all countries around the globe, the budget assigned by the Brazilian Government to the MMA has always been small compared with the total expenditure. Most of the national budget is spent on social security and health care. The average annual budget for the MMA between 2008 and 2015 was on average R$ 2.5 billion (USD $750 million) per year, which corresponds to about 0.1% of the total yearly GDP.

Brazil is currently going through an economic crisis that resulted in significant reductions in government spending. The MMA budget has been reduced by approximately 30%, from R$ 4.2 billion in 2016 to R$ 2.9 billion in 2017, a reduction of about 30% compared with the previous year.

Studies point to the role of enforcement campaigns carried out by the MMA, environmental enforcement agency, and the Institute of Environmental and Renewable Natural Resources (IBAMA) in MMA in reducing deforestation in the Amazon region between 2008 and 2015. Figure 21 shows the relationship between fines imposed on illegal actors and reduction in deforestation.

The IBAMA still relies on teams of inspectors on the ground to monitor and tackle illegal activities in the vast Amazon region.

Although these studies suggest a linear relationship between fines and reduction in deforestation, there is also evidence that suggests that enforcement campaigns are not the only factor involved. Investments in positive incentives are key to maintain and further expand low deforestation rates. High and continuous investments in positive incentives are required for the MMA to be able to continue using the teams, helicopters, vehicles, technology, etc.) but it also the production of new and better data (INPE, Embrapa, etc.) and the provision of positive incentives to forest dwellers.

Figure 21. Illegal Deforestation (Deforestation Area) in Relation to Fine Amount and Percentage of the Acre Gross by IBAMA in Relation to Deforestation Measured by PRODES (dotted line).


---
**Page 89**
---

The national budget cuts from 2014 onwards were followed by an increase in deforestation rates in 2016. This increase was due to a reduction of resources for forest monitoring and control of deforestation, as well as combating deforestation. In years 2014 and 2015 more than 80% of the total resources allocated to the federal budget for forest monitoring and control of deforestation came from the national budget (BRN in Figure 6). Still, in this context, the reduction of the national budget cuts in 2017 and 2018 on budget spending, climate finance becomes even more essential.

The Amazon Fund resources were extremely important in this scenario of contingency. Considering the budget cuts for forest monitoring and control of deforestation, the main April 2015 COFCA agreement was to allow for additionality of the resources from the Amazon Fund to allow for the support of public agencies in projects that aim at reducing deforestation rates. In this context, it is important to highlight that investments must be made in IBAMA and other command and control law enforcement activities at the federal and state levels. Hence, the availability of funds from the Amazon Fund is essential for the effective implementation of SINDEED and NDC.

Brazil's fiscal scenario for 2018/2019 is very challenging and the government will have to cut expenses in all areas. The achievement of the targets set by Brazil under the Paris Agreement and ambitious targets set by Brazil under REDD+ will require large scale investments, including in the forest sector. In this situation, an investment of 150 million USD per year for deforestation reduction payments for reducing emissions from land abandonment in the Amazon biome is essential for the effective implementation of SINDEED and NDC.

D.S. Country ownership

COFA approved a new focus of the Amazon Fund in the 2016/2017 biennium in the following terms: "projects aimed at continuing or improving environmental monitoring and control of deforestation, presented by federal or state agencies, will be considered for funding if they are aligned with the National Strategy for Forests (SNF) and the Amazon Fund's Strategic Plan 2016-2020. The projects must also be in line with the criteria set out in item B8. For this purpose, a technical justification formally presented by the Ministry of Environment will be required. In addition to the project description, the proposal should include an estimate of the resources available for the financial support requested. The technical justification and statement are mandatory and joint financing is not possible."

# REDD+ RBP Funding Proposal

## Green Climate Fund Page 80 of 97

### Describe the beneficiary country ownership of, and capacity to implement a funded project or program

#### Decision 1/CP.16 para 70: REDD+

REDD+ plus activities should encourage developing country Parties to contribute to mitigation actions in the forest sector (Decision 1/CP.16 para 70). REDD+plus implementation should be country-driven and implemented through national strategies, policies, programs, and projects. Developing countries should aim to scale-up their activities in accordance with their national circumstances and the impact of climate change on forests. The funding for REDD+ plus activities is provided as grants, not loans or projects. For developing Country Parties undertaking the REDD+ activities with an aid to results payment mechanism, payments will be made only after results are measured, verified and reported and countries should have in place all REDD elements (Decision 2/CP.16).

### The PPCDA

The PPCDA, Brazil’s core REDD+ policy for the Amazon region, was first launched in 2004 by the Brazilian government with support from the World Bank. It is a result-based mechanism that involves various ministries and entities that had direct or indirect competencies that affected forest conservation in the Amazon region. The PPCDA has been revised several times since its launch to ensure it remains relevant, streamlined and to make it more efficient. The MMA has been responsible for coordinating the implementation of the PPCDA.

### The PPCDA+ and REDD+

The PPCDA+ is in full consistency with the ENEDEE and Brazil’s NDC to the UNFCCC and the Paris Agreement. REDD+plus is a means for the implementation of Brazil’s NDC in the forest sector. The resources mobilized through the PPCDA+ are used to finance projects that have been identified, designed, and re-assessed in initiatives and projects that are in alignment with PPCDA and the ENEDEE. The same will be applied to the REDD+plus program, which is a result-based mechanism for payments of results for its REDD+ plus results.

### Project Proposal

This project proposal is fully aligned with the ENEDEE, Brazil’s NDC to the UNFCCC, the resolutions approved as far by the CONRED and will be implemented by the MMA with the support from UNDP. The MMA has obtained approval from the CONRED to use resources from result-based payments for REDD+ (Resolution 3/2015 of the CONRED). The project proposal is also aligned with the GCF, as it includes all the discussions on use of proceeds as well as providing all input necessary to inform sections on potential co-financing and other sources of funding.

It is worth mentioning that this proposal is in full alignment with the strategic areas of investment of the Brazil Country Program to the GCF, developed by the National Designated Authority in consultation with the Brazilian Society, and the proposal is also included in the Project Pipeline of the retired Country Program.

### D.E. Efficiency and effectiveness

REDD+ plus is implemented by developing countries through policies and programs, not projects. Assurance that mitigation results have indeed been achieved is a fundamental element for a result-based payment mechanism to be effective.

Result-based payments are a new modality of climate finance through which resources are received ex-post for mitigation results achieved in the past, like payments for environmental services. Developing countries should aim at scaling up their REDD+ activities with an aid-to-results payment mechanism as an assessment by two LULUCF experts appointed by the UNFCCC. This evaluation process is conducted annually and includes a review of the national forest monitoring system (NFMS) against this reference level and reported on a Technical Annex to the BUR. This Technical Annex also includes information on the institutional arrangements, the quality of results, information on the national forest monitoring system, institutional rules, among other information.

The processes for assessing the FULF/REL/FEL (decision 13/CMP.9) and for Measuring, Reporting and Verifying (MRV) are described in detail in the Technical Annex to the BUR. The assessment should provide an adequate level of confidence in the system, with a view to attract scaled-up investments. This approach

### E. Compliance with GCF Policies

#### E.1 Environmental and Social Safeguards

##### E.1.1 Environmental and Social Assessment (ESA)

The ESA report, which is included as an Annex to this FP, describes how the project addressed environmental and social risks and impacts in the context of the REDD-plus proposal. The main findings of the ESA are that the project’s entity-level environmental and social safeguards were consistent with the requirements of the GCF’s Environmental and Social Safeguards (ESS) and the UNDP’s Social and Environmental Standards (SES). The report demonstrates that their entity-level environmental and social safeguards are consistent with the GCF’s safeguard standards. The ESA also provides a review of how the project addressed and reported on the GCF’s safeguard requirements, including the importance of the Carcass safeguards in the achievement of results.

##### E.1.2 For the Use of Processes

The proposal includes an annex describing how environmental risks and social risks will be identified, screened, assessed, managed, and monitored in a manner consistent with the GCF’s ESS standards, UNDP’s SES, and the appropriate environmental and social assessment tools and management plans.

As an accredited entity of the GCF, UNDP’s Social and Environmental Standards (SES) were reviewed by the GCF for alignment with its safeguard policies. The project has been demoted to a moderate risk category. A comprehensive review was conducted on the project’s potential risks in terms of environmental and social and environmental risks, as well as their associated mitigation measures based on UNDP’s Social and Environmental Standards (SES) and the GCF’s ESS standards. This review included internal consultations and assessments that will be necessary to complete the first phase of implementation.

The REDD+ plus RBP Funding Proposal

# Guidance from key national stakeholders and UNFCCC SEES provided the basis for the draft Environmental Social Management Framework (ESMF). As a measure to ensure that environmental and social management measures are required to assess and manage risks effectively at an early stage of project implementation, the ESMF will be developed in close consultation with all relevant stakeholders. The ESMF will conduct a comprehensive environmental and social impact assessment (ESIA). The ESIA will be carried out by qualified third-party consultants, in accordance with international best practices for ESIA, including consultations and engagement, with a range of stakeholder including direct beneficiaries of the project. The ESIA will assess the potential impacts on biodiversity, water resources, land use, human health, livelihoods, and the FlorEsta+ pilot program as listed above, and assess the social and environmental risks and benefits around the project area. It will also consider the potential impacts of the project on forest conservation, biodiversity conservation and natural resource use, forest restoration and avoided deforestation efforts, overall carbon emissions, and other relevant aspects.

The ESIA will be conducted in close consultation with all stakeholders including state and municipal governments, as well as indigenous peoples' organizations, civil society organizations, local communities, smallholder enterprises, as well as impacts on cultural heritage. The ESIA will also screen for gender sensitivity at the various stages of project implementation. The ESIA will be completed by June 2018.

The draft ESMF also includes an indicative approach to stakeholder engagement, including the stakeholder consultation process and the identification of key stakeholders. This will be complemented with ongoing consultation with stakeholders, the consultations required for the Gender Assessment and Action Plan, cultural heritage assessment plan, and indigenous peoples' action plan. The gender-sensitive mechanisms described in the ESMF, will also be established and will complement the traditional grievance redress mechanism.

The ESMIF implementation will be overseen by UNDP. The project team will include a senior environmental and social safeguards expert as well as local level safeguard officers responsible for monitoring and implementing the ESMA. A gender-sensitive complaints mechanism will be put in place, with a dedicated complaints line in place and for coordination with the SISREGED+ and a gender expert responsible for the implementation of the Gender Assessment Action Plan. The project team will also be responsible for the formulation and follow-up of these frameworks and to the bi-annually review these actions with oversight from the UNDP, government representatives, civil society organizations, indigenous peoples' organizations’ traditional community organization as well as Federal, State and Municipal level governments. The project team will also be responsible for ensuring that all relevant stakeholders are informed on the use of proceeds which will contribute directly to the SISREGED+, with information generated from the implementation of the ESMA and ESMF being shared with all relevant stakeholders.

The preliminary findings and conclusions of the amended ESMF will be reviewed again based on the findings of the ESIA. The project team will then develop a revised ESMF, which will include a Gender Action Plan, Cultural Heritage Management Plan, and an Indigenous Peoples’ Plan. A preliminary Gender Assessment and Action Plan will also be developed in close consultation with all relevant stakeholders updated through gender-specific consultations. Consistent with UNDP SEIS requirements, no activities that may result in significant adverse impacts on the environment or human health shall proceed until and per the revised ESMF, adoption of appropriate mitigation and management measures are completed. Additionally, the project team will ensure that all environmental and social risks and benefits are assessed timely completion of all social and environmental risk assessments – including the prompt completion of the ESMA, ESIA, and ESMF. The project team will also ensure that all relevant stakeholders have been adoption and readiness of all recommended mitigation measures. Close coordination will be required with the national government to ensure that all relevant stakeholders are informed on the use of proceeds, which indicates that so there is not a duplication in efforts and that monitoring and mitigation measures are consistent across all projects. To ensure that all relevant stakeholders are fully engaged throughout stakeholder participation in the design and implementation of the project going forward, roles will be designated across stakeholder groups, with particular emphasis on the inclusion of primary beneficiaries.

E.1.3. Consultations with stakeholders

Provide adequate and sufficient information on the consultations undertaken with all the relevant stakeholders (including indigenous peoples, traditional communities (and their representative organizations) and family farmers), including how they were consulted, what was discussed, who participated in these consultations and how these are responded to and considered in the proposed activities. Information on the stakeholder engagement process should be provided for each activity.

National REDD+ Strategy development in Brazil, as well as pioneering jurisdictional REDD+ programs, have had a long history of broad stakeholder engagement since their inception, as shown in Appendix A of the 2G Soil Theorist’s Guide. This has been achieved through various mechanisms such as public consultations and monitoring the implementation of the ENREDS+ in Brazil. The governance for the implementation of REDD+ in Brazil is based on a multi-stakeholder approach that includes the participation of civil society, indigenous peoples, the CONAREDD and the CCTs governing the implementation of policies in the forest sector. This governance model has been designed to ensure the implementation of policies in the forest sector by providing a framework for dialogue between government agencies and representatives of civil society, particularly with regard to the representation of indigenous communities. Furthermore, it ensures that existing government institutions are involved in the process.

Representative processes have also increasingly prioritized the participation of indigenous representations and traditional communities in the activities of REDD+ implementation, particularly in regards to the activities in indigenous territories and sustainable conservation units throughout the Legal Amazon region.

Through CONAREDD and its CCTs, a participatory process was conducted to create the rules for developing the National REDD+ Strategy. The process involved 30 stakeholders from various sectors through a Consultative Chamber on Funding Mechanisms (CCFM) that was established in 2015. This chamber aimed at ensuring access to direct funding (i.e., having the appropriate policies in place, presenting results, etc.), as well as fair negotiations with the government and other actors involved in REDD+ implementation (e.g., the Ministry of Environment). The CCFM also ensured that the rules were developed based on the results of consultations. These rules were then presented to the CONAREDD for its 10th ordinary meeting on September 27th, 2015.

The discussions continued in the following CONAREDD meetings and were finalized with the approval of the rules by the CONAREDD Resolution No. 8/16 on December 7th, 2017. The discussions concluded in the following CONAREDD meeting on January 3rd, 2018.

The funding proposal is fully aligned with CONAREDD principles and guidelines and it directly contributes to the implementation of the ENREDS+ and Brazil’s NDC. Brazil has a comprehensive Anti-Money Laundering (AML) regulatory regime in place. In 2018, the GCB enacted Federal Law n° 9.611 criminalizing money laundering related to drug trafficking, terrorism, arms trafficking and other serious crimes. The law also established the Financial Intelligence Unit (FIU), the Financial Activities Control Board (COAF), which is housed within the Ministry of Finance.

Federal Law No. 8032/2005 criminalizes terrorist financing as a predicate offense for money laundering. This law also establishes civil actions against foreign governments as predicate offenses, requires the Central Bank to establish a system for reporting suspicious transactions and authorizes the Financial Intelligence Unit (FIU) to request from all government entities financial information on any subject suspected of involvement in criminal activities. The Federal Law No. 12583/2007 establishes the National System for Prevention and Control relating to UNODC and for information provided bilaterally, was signed, closing a longstanding gap in Brazil's ability to combat serious financing

**Besides the Criminal Code, the Brazilian Legal System also have several instruments against corruption such as Law n°12.853/2013 (Clean State Law) and Law n°14.130/2011 (Clean Public Administration Law), which amended LC no 64/1990 to establish new hypotheses of impropriety, along other legal instruments.**

**Achievement of target impact**

Through the implementation of policies, notably the PPCDAM, Brazil dramatically reduced deforestation in the Legal Amazon region. The total volume of REDD+ achieved by Brazil in the Amazon biome that are recognized by the GCF is 234,651,087 metric tons of CO2e, which represents a substantial contribution to the GCF's mitigation objectives at a global scale.

**Policy and regulatory support**

The PPCDAM is fully enshrined in Brazil’s policy and regulatory framework through the Brazilian federal government created by Decree No. 6934/2013, which was approved by a Presidential Decree on July 5th, 2013. The measures proposed by that Working Group were gathered in the PPCDAM. Throughout three execution phases, the PPCDAM has been implemented and monitored through a monitoring framework that was established at the time of being implemented. The PPCDAM was monitored through a monitoring framework that monitored:

- Institutional capabilities to implement the program were reflected by the number of Brazilian institutions involved (ministries, technical teams, and established institutional capacities), and ultimately reflected in the achieved results.
- Execution and country-specific risks: Risks were identified for the period of the achieved results through the analysis conducted by the inter-ministerial working group that designed the PPCDAM and oversaw its execution.

### E.3 Gender considerations

#### E.3.1 For each of the achieved results:

Provide adequate and sufficient information on the assessment describing the extent to which the measures implemented have contributed to achieving gender equality.

Brazil is a signatory to various international conventions focused on gender equality and women's empowerment, including the CEDAW, a convention by the United Nations that aims to eliminate all forms of discrimination against women. Brazil has also ratified several laws aimed at promoting gender equality in promoting gender equality and women's empowerment. These include Brazil's 1988 Constitution by which women have equal rights with men; the National Plan for Women (PND), approved by the Federal Government created the National Secretariat for Women's Policies (SPM), which is now hosted at the Ministry of Social Development and Human Rights (MDH); and the National Policy on Gender Equality, which was established in 2013. The SPM has also developed a preliminary Gender Assessment provided as an Annex to this FP.

Gender equality has also been recognized as relevant to environmental governance and forest governance. Since 2005, Brazil has implemented several programs that have focused on gender equality, with women exercising decisions and proposing actions to ensure gender equality in programs and policies, specifically those they relate to Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs) 1, 3, 5, 6, 7, 8, 9, 10, 12, 14, and 15. These programs have benefited more than 13 million Brazilian women living in rural or forest areas. In recent decades, several fundamental changes have been made to the Brazilian Constitution, which has led to a significant increase in the number of women who are able to participate in decision-making processes at all levels of government. The most important of these is the program known as Bolsa Família, which targeted women as preferred beneficiaries. Bolsa Família has contributed significantly to reducing poverty and inequality by providing cash transfers directly to poor families out of poverty. The Program has 2.9 million beneficiary families in Amazonian States, of which R$300 billion are transferred annually. This program has also been instrumental in the design of the Flexstar+ pilot project and other components of the program. Another important program is the National Program for Women's Empowerment (PNE), which provides cash transfers to women living in extreme poverty while improving their livelihoods. The program includes cash transfers in return for the maintenance of forest cover, Bolsa Vida shares targeting and cash transfer channels, and other components that are designed to improve the well-being of women living in ecosystems while also improving the livelihoods of people living in extreme poverty – the condition of nearly


---
**Page 97**
---

17% of the population in the Amazon region. As part of this program, traditional communities such as "balejau" received land titles and were able to access credit for their activities. The Brazilian government is currently implementing this initiative. Lessons from Botá Venda in regards to gender responsiveness will also be incorporated into the REDD+ program (Programa de Reforma Agrária in Portuguese) which has also highly improved the women's access to land titles by means of prioritizing the access for women who are householders.

The involvement of women and indigenous peoples in decision-making processes at all levels has also increased since 2006. As an important example, in 2015, the Brazilian indigenous movement founded the Union of the Indigenous Women of Brazil (UWIB), which is a national organization that represents more than 300 indigenous women. The Secretary of Extractivist Communities (CNJ), which has built on strengths of forested rural-women by strategizing and working across sectors and scales, helping to shift the paradigm in development, environment and health in Brazil.

It is the above international and national legal frameworks, as well as national efforts and movements related to gender equality that have influenced the design and implementation of the REDD+ program. The governance of the governance for REDD+ in Brazil gender balance was a key consideration. Most representatives from the Ministry of Environment are women (50% of the members). In the COTs, which are made up for 14, more than 50% of the representatives are females. Some CTCs, including the one dealing with safeguards, also have equal representation of female participants.

### E.2.3. For the use of gender-sensitive information

UNDP is committed to avoiding long-range sex-disparity gender inequality, promoting gender equality and the empowerment of women, reducing gender disparities and inequalities in climate funding and overall access to resources. UNDP will ensure that all activities are designed with a gender perspective and that they are responsive in its design and implementation, and more generally, mainstreaming the gender focus into the REDD+ program. The gender-sensitive information will be used for the following purposes: ensuring that the project does not discriminate against women or girls or reinforce gender-based discrimination and/or inequalities as well as take into account the different needs of men and women in relation to climate change adaptation and mitigation, and are able to participate meaningfully and equitably, have equitable access to training, capacity building, technical assistance and resources, and receive comparable social and economic benefits and opportunities.

Regardless of the positive experiences in regards to gender balance in REDD+ process described above, as well as the commitment made by UNDP to avoid long-range sex-disparity gender inequality, it is recognized that significant gender inequalities remain in regards to political participation, access to land and resources, decision-making power, and representation. These inequalities are reflected in the existing laws and additional issues in regards to livelihoods, health, and gender-based violence. There is also growing recognition of the need for a more comprehensive approach to gender equality in REDD+ beyond the scope of ENREDD+ programming. In order to capture the gender context in which ENREDD+ implementation occurs as well as the potential impacts on women and girls, the following activities will be undertaken: identifying constraints, contributions and priorities of women, men, boys and girls within the action sites, as part of the completion of the Gender Action Plan (GAP). The GAP will also identify any gender-sensitive information and corresponding measures within the action plans to ensure 1) any possible adverse gender impacts are avoided or mitigated; 2) that all genders have equal access to resources and opportunities for climate change adaptation and mitigation, and are able to participate meaningfully and equitably in the process of decision-making. Gender budget and indicators will be also assigned within the action plans to ensure the periodical monitoring of progress towards gender equality.

As a first step in completing this GAP, a forward-looking desk-based Gender Assessment was completed in anticipation of this FF (see Annex). The Assessment examined the proposed activities in the context of

The Brazilian Central Bank (BACEN) is responsible for implementing the AML regulatory regime, including anti-money laundering, counter-terrorism financing, and combating the financing of terrorism. The BACEN has a comprehensive legal framework in place to combat money laundering and terrorist financing. This includes Federal Law No. 9613/1998 which created the Central Bank and created a registry of information on all bank account holders, and enables the Brazilian Financial Intelligence Unit (UFC) to monitor suspicious transactions and report them to the Central Bank for further investigation.

### E.S. Indigenous Peoples

Provide sufficient information on how the activities to be implemented with the use of projects will meet the requirements of the GCF environmental and social safeguards standards and policies and also ensure that they are consistent with the national laws, regulations and other obligations of the countries directly applicable to the activities under relevant international treaties and agreements.

The application of UNDP's Social and Environmental Standards ensures that the program will protect and foster respect for human rights of Indigenous Peoples under the United Nations Declaration on Human Rights and also comply with the international social safeguards standards and policies relating to indigenous peoples. The consultation processes affording these protections are based on the national laws, regulations and other obligations of the countries directly applicable to the activities under relevant international treaties and agreements.

In the Amazon biome, more than 108 million hectares are legally protected as indigenous lands, with further lands being proposed for protection in the future. In 2013, the Brazilian government signed a Memorandum of Understanding (MoU) on the Sustainable Development of Traditional Peoples and Communities and in 2016, the government also created the National Policy for Environmental and Territorial Management in Indigenous Territories. The indigenous communities are equally represented. In 2012, the National Policy for Territorial and Environmental Management was signed by the Brazilian government and the National Congress of Indigenous Peoples. The consultation process with representatives of 186 Brazilian Indigenous Peoples (more information can be found at <https://www.mma.gov.br/portal/indigenas>) is a key element in this policy, as indigenous communities are equally represented and the Presidency of the Committee is regularly alternated between government and indigenous representation. The National Policy for Environmental and Territorial Management has four main objectives: 1) recognition and protection of indigenous territories; 2) strengthening of indigenous governance over their territories; 3) prevention and recovery of environmental damage; 4) sustainable use of natural resources in the territories.

The National Policy for Environmental and Territorial Management is a key element in this policy, as it provides the basis for the support of indigenous peoples and traditional communities in this proposal. The National Policy for Environmental and Territorial Management also sets out guidelines on REDD+ implementation in indigenous lands, which will shape the design of the Floresta pilot program.

Consultations with Indigenous Peoples and traditional communities will take place in the context of the proposed project activities. These consultations will be conducted according to the Brazilian government's guidelines, including those of Free, Prior and Informed Consent. As a preliminary step, MMA has held meetings with indigenous representatives from the National Coordination of Indigenous Territories (CNI) and the Management Committee of the National Policy for Environmental and Territorial Management in Indigenous Territories (PNCAT). These consultations have been conducted according to the guidelines of Free, Prior and Informed Consent. The PNCAT has also provided a framework for indigenous peoples to effectively access resources through their own organizations, to meet their climate change adaptation needs and to participate in decision-making processes on REDD+ projects. According to the guidelines of PNCAT: Indigenous stakeholders have emphasized and reinforced the need of effective participation of indigenous communities in the design and implementation phases of projects related to REDD+ GCF funds were also held on August 18th, 2018 with the Management Committee of PNCAT. All these consultations will be conducted according to the guidelines of Free, Prior and Informed Consent, including autonomous development, including incorporation of the use of Indigenous Environmental

## E. Monitoring and evaluation

### Project monitoring and evaluation arrangements that will take place for providing annual monitoring reports based on the information provided for the use of proceeds in sections C.2.3 and C.2.4.

#### Project-level monitoring and evaluation will be undertaken in compliance with the UNDP-PPF and the UNDP Country Office (UNDCO) Monitoring, Evaluation, Accountability and Learning (MEAL) guidelines and procedures by the project team and the UNDCO Country Office (UNDCO). C.2.4 provides outcome indicators and corresponding means of verification. The MEAL plan includes: an inception report; annual ARI reports to GCF; progress reports to UNDP; and a final evaluation report. All these documents will be made available online via Info Hub Brasil (http://redc.minsa.gov.br/informes/).

The following sections contain the principal components of this plan. The project’s MEAL plan will be presented at the inception meeting and the Project Board Meeting, providing a collective briefing of indicators, means of verification, and the full definition of project staff M&E responsibilities.

#### Project Inception Workshop

A Project Inception Workshop will be held within the first 2 months of project start, involving those with expertise in monitoring and evaluation, including representatives from the UNDCO Country Office (CO) and Regional staff as well as the project team. Discussion topics include: defining the project’s decision-making structures; including reporting and communication lines; and conflict resolution mechanisms.

Before the workshop, the Project Board will review the MEAL plan and agree on the indicators, targets and their means of verification, and recheck assumptions and risks.

The Inception Workshop will address key issues related to planning for the first year annual report. At the Inception Workshop will address several key issues including:

- To assist all partners to fully understand and take ownership of the project.
- To define the project’s decision-making structures, including responsibilities of UNDCO Country Office (CO) and Regional staff as it fits in the project team.
- Discussing reporting lines and communication lines within the project; the project’s decision-making mechanisms, including reporting and communication lines, and conflict resolution mechanisms.

#### Annual Project Report

This key document is prepared by the Technical Project Advisors, consolidated by the Project Manager, validated by the Project Coordination Unit (PCU) and final approved by the Project Board to monitor progress made since previous report and for the previous reporting period. The format and content of this annual report will be adopted based on the simplified reporting regime which will be established for RBP in the GCF.

# REDD+ RBP FUNDING PROPOSAL

## GREEN CLIMATE FUND | PAGE 99 OF 97

Using traditional post-facts, paper-based, descriptive M&E and reporting methods it is very challenging to consolidate the large quantity of geographically explicit information. Verify it, analyze progress towards policy milestones in compliance with land-use commitments and report on it in an accurate, transparent and interactive manner.

New tools and technologies exist that respond to this problem and drastically change the paradigm in land use monitoring and reporting. These tools are based on satellite imagery and can be used for a wide range of applications. They have been tested and validated by UNDP in a context as challenging as the Amazon. Once operational, the marginal cost of using these tools is close to zero. The tools are also very flexible and can be adapted to different contexts and requirements at low cost. As such, the use of these tools greatly benefits impact investments and related monitoring and reporting. They can also be used for other purposes including: (i) providing evidence on the effectiveness of implementation of projects, especially if the various actors involved (beneficiaries, governments, project teams, etc.) were monitored this way in real time (as opposed to post-facts, paper-based, descriptive monitoring).

UNDP will use an open source turn key M&E solution to do the following:

* Collect, transfer, consolidate, backup and analyze spatial and non-spatial data to facilitate the tracking of land-use changes.
* Transparently track and demonstrate progress against beneficiary performance milestones;
* Monitor compliance with project implementation milestones;
* Monitor compliance towards land-use commitments made by stakeholders, collectively or individually.

In addition, and as needed, the UNDP will conduct visits to project sites based on the agreed schedule in the Project's Inspection Report/Annual Work Plan to assess first hand project progress. Other members of the Project Team (e.g., UNDP Country Office) may also visit projects at their discretion. The UNDP Country Office will be circulated no less than one month after the visit to the project team and Project Board members. In addition, the UNDP will conduct visits to project sites periodically and final site reports will be prepared.

## Mid-term of project cycle

The project will undertake an independent mid-term review at the mid-point of project implementation. The mid-term review will determine progress being made towards the achievement of outcomes and will identify course correction needs. The mid-term review will also provide a summary of lessons learned, highlight issues regarding decisions and actions, and will present initial lessons learned about project design, implementation and monitoring and evaluation. The mid-term review report will be available for enhanced implementation during the final half of the project's term. The final MTR report will be available in English and Portuguese.

## End of Project

An independent Final Evaluation will take place three months prior to the closure of the project, undertaken in accordance with UNDP and GCF guidelines. The final evaluation will focus on the delivery of the project as initially planned (and as corrected after the mid-term review, if any such correction took place). The first evaluation report will be available for enhanced implementation during the concluding the capacity development and the achievement of global environmental benefits.

The Final Evaluation will also provide recommendations for follow-up actions that requires a management response. The final evaluation report will be submitted to the UNDP Regional Technical Advisor and the Project Coordination Unit (PCU) and will be approved by the Project Board. The UNDP PCU will include the project in its annual evaluation report, which will be made available to the public.

The final evaluation report is in English and Portuguese and the management response to the public UNDP Evaluation Resource Centre.

During the first three months, the project team will prepare the Project Terminal Report. This comprehensive report will be made available to the public through the Info Hub Brasil (http://meda.mma.gov.br/infohub). It will summarize the results achieved (outcomes, outcomes, outputs), lessons learned, problems met and

## F. Legal arrangements

### 5.1 LEGAL FRAMEWORK FOR REDD-plus results

- **Legal title to REDD-plus results in Brazil**: The Brazilian Government will provide a legal title to REDD-plus results achieved within the country.
- **Covenant that no other party has a competing claim on the results proposed to be reported to the GCF**: This covenant is made by the Brazilian Government.

As presented in section B.2 (v) of this proposal, REDD-plus results achieved in the Brazilian territory will be used for the fulfillment of Brazil's Nationally Determined Contributions (NDCs) under the UNFCCC and its Paris Agreement. Below is the relevant regulation regarding REDD+ results:

The Federal Decree 8579/2015 created the CONAREDD+, which coordinates and monitors the implementation of REDD+ activities in Brazil. The Brazilian Ministry of Environment (MMA), as the Executive Secretary of the CONAREDD+, will issue diplomas recognizing REDD+ results-based payments received by each project. These diplomas must be kept for 10 years, and neither the MMA nor their respective diplomats may not use it directly or indirectly to fulfill mitigation commitments under the Kyoto Protocol or its Paris Agreement. The amount of the cap of art. 4° shall be nominal and non-transferable; will not generate rights or credits of any nature, and will contain the annual equivalent to the REDD+ result and can be consulted on the website of the MMA.

Furthermore, Brazil emphasized in its NDC to the UNFCCC and its Paris Agreement that "any transfer of units resulting from REDD+ activities implemented in Brazil must be made with prior and explicit consent by the Federal Government. Brazil will not recognize the use by other Parties of any units resulting from REDD+ activities implemented in Brazil, unless such units are transferred through any mechanism, instrument or arrangement established outside the Convention, its Kyoto Protocol or its Paris Agreement."

The CONAREDD+ has approved a few Resolutions that reinforce the understanding that there is no formal legal title to REDD+ results in Brazil and that these results cannot be transferred. Below are key elements from some of those resolutions:

- **Resolution n. 5, dated December 16th, 2017**: General principles for the implementation of the National REDD+ Strategy through the CONAREDD+.
- **Article I: VII**: Mitigation benefits achieved through REDD+ activities implemented in Brazil will be reflected only as a contribution to the fulfillment of Brazil's NDCs under the UNFCCC and its Paris Agreement.
- **Article 2**: The distribution of funding limits for results achieving reduced emissions from deforestation in the Amazon biome do not generate ownership or guarantee of income.


---
**Page 103**
---

Article 5, paragraph 2: The Parties mentioned in Article 2 that have interest to raise resources using oversight mechanisms established under this Resolution are reminded of their obligation to ensure that this Resolution applies for eligibility, that they are aware that this fundraising is limited to a modality of Result-Based Financing.

Article 5, paragraph 3: The payments for REDD+ results made based on the limits established in this Resolution will not be considered as financing under the Kyoto Protocol. These payments will cover these results, for the fulfillment of international mitigation commitments, and will not affect the national accounts or the calculation of the Parties' share of the United Nations' Clean Development Mechanism Contribution to the Paris Agreement.

Article 5, paragraph 4: The Parties mentioned in Article 2 that have interest to raise resources using oversight mechanisms established under this Resolution are reminded of their obligation to comply with the terms of the Kyoto Protocol and the Bonn Agreements, as well as the United Nations' Clean Development Mechanism Contribution to the Paris Agreement.

Article 5, paragraph 5: In case of non-compliance with the obligations established in the previous paragraph, the relevant Party will be considered ineligible by the COMAREDO+ in access REDD+ results-based payments.

Resolution n.° 7 of July 18th, 2017: Defines the eligibility rules for access to direct financing for result-based payments.

Article 6: The Amazon States or federal entities eligible assume full legal responsibility for managing and implementing projects under this Resolution, in accordance with the applicable national laws and safeguards and providing accounting information.

Accounting Entity Fee (AE) and Project Management Cost (PMC): Parties applying to receive the approval of the COMAREDO+ and be eligible to access result-based payments need to sign the following: "I declare to be aware that the European Union is not responsible for any costs related to the implementation of projects under this Resolution, finance and that I will inform all the Parties involved in the REDD+ results-based agreements about the terms of the Article 6.4 and other norms established by the COMAREDO+"

Dear Mr. Manzanares,

1. Firstly I would like to express Brazil's appreciation for the approval of the Green Climate Fund's Pilot Programme for REDD+ results-based payments (RBP). Brazil has taken significant steps to materialize the UNFCCC Warsaw Framework for REDD+ at the domestic level and thus further advance national REDD+ efforts. Therefore we very much look forward to being able to engage with the GCF on the pilot programme.

2. The National REDD+ Committee has set the rules for national entities to access results-based payments. The Committee's Resolution nº 05 defines the distribution of REDD+ RBP funding between Brazil and other countries. According to this resolution, Brazil is entitled to receive 40% of the total government can raise payments for 40% of the total results achieved and verified under the UNFCCC process, while the Amazonian states are entitled to 60%. It is worth to mention that according to National REDD+ Committee's Resolution nº 12, the Brazilian Federal Government has already received the RBP it received by Brazil cannot be used, direct or indirectly, to fulfill mitigation commitments of other Parties to the United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change.

3. In accordance with decisions by the National REDD+ Committee, the Ministry of the Environment is one of the entities eligible to raise REDD+ results-based payments. The Government of Brazil sees the GCF Pilot Programme as an important source of funding to boost Brazil's successful policies and actions in the area of REDD+.

4. In order to access GCF funds, we conducted a prospection process to identify the accredited institution that would support the Brazilian funding proposal. Based on Brazil's priorities and criteria established, this is the National Designated Authority (NDA) for Brazil, which is the Ministry of Environment as an institution.

5. In my capacity as the REDD+ national focal point at the UNFCCC duly designated pursuant to the National Decree nº 876/2017 and the Ministerial Ordinance nº 299/2017, I hereby express my consent for UNDP as the accredited entity to submit a funding proposal entitled "Brazil REDD+ Results-

Based Payments (RPB) for results period 2014-2015" under the Green Climate Fund's Pilot Programme for REDD+ RBP:

7. The Government of Brazil counts on the GFCC's Secretariat efforts and support in order to fully and successfully operationalize the Pilot Programme.

# Secretariat's assessment of FP100

## Proposal name: REDD-PLUS results-based payments for results achieved by Brazil in the Amazon biome in 2014 and 2015

### Accredited entity:
United Nations Development Programme (UNDP)

### Country(ies):
Brazil

### Project/programme size:
Medium

## I. Overall assessment of the Secretariat

### 1.1 Project background

a. The project presents Brazil's REDD-PLUS results for the years 2014 and 2015 derived from reducing emissions from deforestation in the Amazon region which have subsequently been reported to GCF as part of the pilot programme. More information is available on the REDD-PLUS information hub: <https://reidd.plus/en/reid-plus.html>. These results are presented to GCF for Results Based Payments as part of the REDD-PLUS results-based payments pilot programme and are fully consistent with the requirements set out in the terms of reference (ToR) for this pilot program. The detailed assessment of the proposed REDD-PLUS results is provided in annex 1.

b. The project notes that countries receiving REDD-plus RBP should ensure their contributions to activities in line with their Nationally Determined Contributions (NDC) as established under the UNFCCC Paris Agreement, REDD-plus strategies, or low-carbon development plans consistent with their NDCs.

c. Brazil will use the proceeds from the results-based payments for the following two outputs:

(i) Development of a pilot of an Environmental Services Incentive Program for Conservation and Sustainable Management of Forests (Heritage).

(ii) Strengthen the implementation of Brazil's REDD-plus Strategy through improvements in its governance structure and systems.

### 1.2 Scorecard results

a. As per decision B.8(07), the proposal was assessed using the scorecard for REDD-plus RBP. This assessment was conducted by the secretariat with support of UNEP/CIF experts and the preliminary assessment was reviewed and agreed upon by the technical advisor panel (TAP). The final score was discussed and agreed with the independent TAP.

| Scorecard Section | Results |
| --- | --- |
| Carbon elements | Score: 36 All criteria "pass" |
| Non-carbon elements | Score: 2 |
| Carbon safeguards | All criteria "pass" |
| Use of proceeds and non-carbon benefits | Score: 2 |

Table 1. Scorecard results (see annex 1 for details)

### Strengths and points of caution

#### 1.4 Strengths and points of caution

The funding proposal is presented to the Board for consideration with the following remarks:

**Strengths**

- The project is part of the first batch of projects submitted under the REEDS pilot program.
- The use of proceeds will be reinvested in pilot environmental services incentive programs and further activities. The proceeds will help implement the national REEDS pilot strategy focusing on governance issues.

As the results paid are related to past activities from committed projects, it is important that the Board confirms the legitimacy of the results and the proper implementation of safeguards during the period in which the project was implemented.
- Monitoring for the use of proceeds consistent with the GCF’s safeguard policy is required.

**Points of caution**

- Deforestation rates have increased in the Brazilian Amazon in recent years, which should be accounted for when submitting requests for payments.


---
**Page 115**
---

The REDD+ results presented to GCF have already undergone quality control through the technical assessments carried out by the UNFCCC roster of experts.

Although estimates were found in overall accordance with the UNFCCC decisions, an improvement on the estimates where identified, notably in Brazil.

Acknowledging that the host country is sovereign to decide on the use of the proceeds as long as they are aligned with the GCF investment criteria and that the transaction costs to implement these payments seem to be high in comparison with other similar investments,

The Board may wish to consider approving this funding proposal with the terms and conditions listed in the respective term sheet and addendum XVI, titled "List of proposed conditions and recommendations."

## Assessment of performance against the investment criteria

### 2.1 Impact potential
**Scale: High**

* Brazil's continuous work on improving their forest monitoring have led to in combination with the implementation of various policies and measures to the remarkable result of reducing 6.1 million tonnes of CO2 emissions from deforestation in the Amazon biome between 2004-2013.
* This result matches approximately 50% of all CO2 emissions from all Annex I countries in 2012 alone.
* As a result, Brazil is no longer the highest emitting sector in the Amazon biome. The LULUCF sector is no longer the highest emitting sector in Brazil.

### 10
Brazil's Action Plan for the prevention and control of deforestation in the legal Amazon (PPCDAm) was launched in September 2004 in advance to the start of the pilot phase entering its 6-phase operating until 2020. The PPCDAm has through its innovative approach of forest monitoring, enforcement, and management, been able to achieve environmental monitoring, control and enforcement managed to support Brazil's efforts in combating deforestation in the Amazon biome, which is a significant achievement for the history of the UNFCCC.
* In parallel to this, Brazil has to manage to combat and manage leakage.

### 13
The total volume of emissions reductions achieved by Brazil in this period, including those proposed under REDD+, are estimated at approximately 20 million tonnes of CO2. These results included in the technical annexes to the two biennial update reports submitted to the UNFCCC.
* These results were technically assessed and verified in accordance with GCF decisions on REDD+.

### It is therefore considered that the results achieved provide significant global benefits in mitigating climate change.


### 2.2 Paradigm shift potential
**Scale: High**

The implementation of REDD+ policies and measures has furthermore had an important paradigm shift effect, not only for the protection of forests but maintaining them in agricultural production within years of lower deforestation rates.
* Although deforestation trends have increased in recent years, the project will revert in important areas such as governance structure and land use planning to a more sustainable model of development reducing emissions from deforestation in the Amazon biome. It is expected that the payments provided through the pilot programme for REDD+ results based payments as well as


---
**Page 116**
---

2.3 Sustainable development potential Scale: High

The REDD-PLUS policies in Brazil have since 2006 contributed strongly to economic environmental and social sustainability. The proceeds paid by the GCF are expected to contribute significantly towards achieving this goal. The project will support Brazil to implement the national REDD-PLUS strategy on governance issues should fully lead to sustainable development through better protection and conservation of forests, improved governance structures, and increased participation in decision-making processes.

In addition, the proposed project is expected to engage and improve the livelihoods of women who are mostly responsible for forest management activities. The implementation of the PPCD has over the years brought more attention to the communities of the Amazon region through its many social policies and projects. This proposal will support Brazil to continue with this approach.

Brazil has progressed its gender agenda significantly over the years. Programmes such as Bolsa Família have targeted women, and gender issues playing a central role. Many of these lessons learned will be incorporated into the project design and will be reported through this project.

Brazilian proceeds to continue its investments and strong commitment to combat and reduce deforestation in strengthening its governance and providing incentives to the stakeholders. This is reflected by its commitment to invest in an Environmental Service Incentive Project (ESIP) as part of the National REDD-PLUS Strategy, which will complement the National REDD-plus Strategy. Brazil continues to combat deforestation. And that reinvestment of the proceeds from the GCF into Brazil's PPCD and in achieving continuous results from reducing emissions from deforestation.

2.4 Needs of the recipient Scale: High

There are large number of indigenous peoples from several different ethnic groups and rural population living in the Brazilian Amazon, which also includes some of the most vulnerable populations in Brazil. The literature on this subject is extensive, and there has been much debate in the literature relative to the improvement of economic growth in the Brazilian Amazon, while at the same time notice increased inequality and poverty of original settlers.

GCF's support for the implementation of the Forest+ programme will benefit the rural population living in the Amazon as part of the implementation of the Forest+ program. Not to mention that the project is expected to provide a strong financial incentive for results-based payments that has the potential to attract and catalyse the private sector and other donors and investors with the objective to reduce the current deforestation trends.

2.5 Country ownership Scale: High

The project is in full alignment with Brazil's NDC, REDD-PLUS strategy as well as national policies and strategies. The project is furthermore in full alignment with the strategic areas of

### Assessment of consistency with GCF safeguards and policies

#### 3.1 Environmental and social safeguards

The Government of Brazil through the accredited entity (AE) is submitting a proposal for the payment of results-based payments under the Amazon Fund for the Prevention and Control of Deforestation in the Legal Amazon (PPCDAm). In meeting the requirements of GCF’s pilot programme for the PPCDAm plus results-based payments, the AE has conducted an environmental and social due diligence on the use of results-based payments. As further described below, the AE has also developed a risk management framework for applying the environmental and social risks and potential impacts of activities that will be supported by the payment of results-based payments under the PPCDAm plus results-based payments framework (ESRF) that describes the due diligence and management of these risks and impacts.

The PPCDAm started in 2004 and is among the key programs of Brazil to reduce deforestation. The PPCDAm is implemented through a phased approach with the results period implemented in the third phase. The Amazon Fund was established in 2008 to mobilize resources for land use planning, sustainable forest management, and rural production activities.


---
**Page 118**
---

To support efforts to prevent, monitor, and address deforestation as well as promote the conservation and sustainable management of the Amazon biome, The Amazon Fund is one of the key financial instruments supporting the implementation of the PFCDA. The Amazon Fund follows the REDD Plus Social and Environmental Safeguards (SES) developed by the AI and is subject to stakeholder consultations and is also subject to the environmental and social due diligence of the fund manager Banco Nacional de Desenvolvimento Econômico e Social (BNDES).

The implementation of the REDD Plus activities supported by The Amazon Fund, among others, the activities address and respect the UNFCU safeguards (Cancun safeguards) as described in Decision 1/CP.16. The assessment of how each safeguard was addressed is contained within the country's context and based on the interpretation formed the basis of the assessment on how each safeguard was addressed is contained within the scope of the activities. # E&S risk category

The Accredited Entity has classified the RBP project as having likely moderate environmental and social (E&S) risks and impacts, equivalent to Category B of the GCF project classification framework.

### Safeguard instruments prepared for the project (including their disclosure)

The Accredited Entity has submitted an environmental and social management framework (ESMF) as the main safeguard instrument. The ESMF is a high-level document that sets out the overall approach in terms of specific locations and components, and therefore the ESMF has been prepared to set out the guidance for project activities at the subproject level. The ESMF will be supplemented by other documents particularly those related to output 1. Clarification is needed on the type of safeguard activity or documents that will be required at each stage of the subprojects or activities and the agencies responsible for them.

### Information disclosure

The overall E&S risk level of the programme is moderate risk which warrants disclaimer of the ESMF document. Both the English and local language (Portuguese) versions are provided in the ESMF document, as well as a summary of the key risks and impacts. Furthermore, the Accredited Entity commits to disclosing information about the project's activities including the environmental and social management plans, risk mitigation measures, and monitoring reports in a timely manner and in local languages as much as possible.

### Key impacts and mitigation measures (climbing requirements of triggers P2 – 8) and impacts risks that could be mitigated by safeguard instruments

The project is expected to have significant direct environmental impacts on biodiversity due to project activities or in next to critical habitats or ecosystems. The project may also result in indirect environmental impacts such as increased use of pesticides. Additionally, potential social impacts include land tenure, physical and/or economic displacement, cultural heritage and indigenous peoples. Mitigation measures are included in the ESMF for all identified risks.

### Capacity of executing entities

Implementation arrangements include a project team with E&S safeguard experts, responsible for monitoring and implementation of safeguards for the project. The project team will be supported by an independent third party consultant to ensure that the provided advice is provided where required to ensure implementation of management plans. The Accredited Entity will maintain records of all safeguard activities.

### Monitoring and evaluation

The E&S monitoring, review and reporting requirements are presented in the ESMF. The indicative cost of $17 million related to ESMF implementation was also provided as some areas is at high cost, reducing the amount of effective payments in the final beneficiaries.

### Stakeholder engagement

Stakeholder consultations have been undertaken during the course of developing the project. These include engagement with the national REDD-PLUS committee which represents

### 3.2 Gender policy

#### 40. The AE has analysed gender equality and women’s empowerment issues in the period of the achievement of the project contributing to social and environmental standards against diagnosis questions and review indicators. These projects which contributed to the results that achieved, is that, the going green, catalysing the contribution of indigenous lands to the conservation of Brazil’s forest contribute to the reduction of deforestation and increase of carbon sequestration against these indicators. The alignment review points out that these projects did not directly address gender equality and women empowerment issues in the design and implementation of the project’s activities. For example, in the amazon portal seeds project women participated in seed selection and distribution but there was no specific focus on gender equality and women representation in the theme advisory chamber on safeguard established by CONAREDD-PLUS. However, the AE has noted that since the beginning of the project, the participatory and consultative processes have increased prioritizing the participation of local communities it is not clear whether equitable representation of both men and women was ensured and how participation of women and men in these processes was measured.

#### 41. Having seen that there are cavats in advancing the concerns and needs of women in the REDD-PLUS projects, the AE recommended the integration of gender issues in REDD-PLUS project design, implementation, monitoring and evaluation including conducting gender analyses, collecting data on gender equality and women empowerment indicators and targets, and using these information to inform project design, implementation, monitoring and evaluation indicators and targets. The assessment by the AE determined the alignment of the projects (underpinning the Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs) and the Social, Socially Inclusive and Environmental Standards (SES). The SES includes an overarching principle of gender equality and women’s empowerment.

#### 42. Recommendations on gender-related issues have been taken up in the preparation of the proposal for the use of proceeds. The proposal contains a gender assessment; therefore, it complies with the recommendations made by the AE. The gender assessment is based on desk review of available information which has gathered evidence on gender issues in Brazil and the Amazon region. The assessment also highlights the need to empower women through the implementation REDD-PLUS by mainstreaming gender equality and women empowerment into project design and implementation. Priority points presented by the project to promote equal participation and access of project benefits include, representation of men and women in project governance arrangements and identifying and addressing barriers that prevent women from participating fully in project activities. The gender assessment will be re-assessed and revised as needed as part of the environmental and social impact assessment.

#### 43. The AE has provided a preliminary gender action plan which translates some of the recommendations to the gender assessment into actions corresponding to the project’s outputs, indicators with sex-disaggregated targets, and institutions responsible for implementing the actions

### Risks

#### Overall Program assessment (medium risk)

- Under the Pilot Framework for REDD+ pilot results-based payments, GCF is requested to provide a payment of USD 96,452,220 for the emission reductions achieved by Brazil in 2014 - 2015. The volume of EERs for which GCF funding is sought is within 30% of the total pilot results-based payments that are expected to be made under the Pilot Framework, and will support the implementation of Brazil's ENERGEO-plus and reinvest in the pilot for environmental services incentive programs (Floresta+).

- AE / EE capability to execute the current program (low risk):

  - The project will be implemented under UNDP’s Direct Implementation modality (DIM). There are no specific risks related to this modality. UNDP has a long history and an extensive track record in implementation of projects in developing countries. As an EE for the project, UNDP is responsible and accountable for managing this project, including monitoring and reporting on its progress.

  - The Ministry of Environment of Brazil (MMA) will be UNDP’s government partner and supervisor of the project under DIM modality.

#### Program specific execution risks (medium risk):

- Risk of double payments: The FP indicates that “Info Brasil” is an online tool for national reporting. To avoid any inconsistency of the information and avoid double payments for the same result, the government issued a Federal Decree for coordination of the implementation of the project. Under this decree, in case of double reporting to the national legislation, only four entities may receive REDD+ RBP and the results paid for are registered in the national reports (Info Brasil).

- Use of proceeds: The project will use the proceeds from the payments to pilot an environmental services incentive program (Floresta+) and strengthen the implementation of the National Forest Policy (PNF) and the National System of Conservation Units.

  - Thus, the end-use of proceeds is as permitted in the ToR. The proposed activities will be implemented over two years and thus need to be adequately monitored to achieve the desired results.

- Operation maturity: The Output 1 of the project aims to provide incentives for conservation and sustainable use of forests in Brazil by supporting the implementation of Brazil’s NDC. It will use direct payments and operate through 6 modalities. However, the guidelines, duration, and institutions that will manage funds for Modality 3 & 4 need to be determined. The design of the project is still at an early stage with respect to its structure and the definition of values and priority areas for direct payments are yet to be finalized. As such, it is recommended that AE closely coordinate with relevant indicative project financing.


---
**Page 122**
---

implementing partners for timely decision making on the currently underarranged areas mentioned earlier and monitor the progress on the use of proceeds.

### Disclosure plan (medium risk): The project implementation will be over 6 years and the FP provided information that how AE fee and Project Management Cost (PMC) will be used over such period. It is recommended to disburse the AE fee and PMC in a phased manner during initial months. It is recommended that the disbursement of GCF's financing is done over the 3-year period.

#### Compliance risk (medium risk):
- UNDP is the AE on this program and it was aware of the contractual obligations placed upon it by the GCF. However, despite the AML/CFT and due diligence processes being implemented. Nevertheless, Brazil has experienced major incidents of corruption in recent years, including its energy sector, so it is especially important that appropriate safeguards be implemented to ensure that the project will not be subject to any form of corruption cases which evidences serious attempts to embezzle or address money laundering. The GCF's internal audit and compliance assigns this a high risk rating.

#### GCF's concentration risk (low risk):
- In case of approval, the impact of this proposal on the GCF portfolio risk remains non-material and within the risk appetite in terms of concentration level, results area or single proposal.

### Compliance risk (medium risk):
It is recommended that any approval by the Board is made by considering the above points.

### 3.4 Results monitoring and reporting

Following the requirements of the ToR, the proposal will follow a simplified reporting for which a standard template has been developed by the secretariat. The frequency of reporting will be annually through the AMA.

The monitoring of the use of proceeds will be conducted at the main activity level according to the description provided in the funding proposal.


### 3.5 Legal assessment

The Accreditation Master Agreement was signed with the Accredited Entity on 5 August 2016 and entered into force on 21 November 2016.

The Accredited Entity has provided a legal opinion/certificate confirming that it has obtained all internal approvals and it has the capacity and authority to implement the project.


---
**Page 123**
---

The proposed project will be implemented in the Federative Republic of Brazil, country in which GCF is not provided with privileges and immunities. This means that, amongst other things, GCF is not protected against litigation or expropriation in this country, which risks need to be further assessed by the Board as part of the ratification of the privileges and immunities agreement to the Government of Brazil on 8 December 2017.

The Heads of the Independent Redress Mechanism (IRM) and Independent Integrity Unit (IIU) have determined that it is not feasible for the GCF and their respective secretariats to pursue actions and/or investigations, as appropriate, in countries where the GCF is not provided with relevant privileges and immunities. Therefore, any claims or requests for payments by the GCF are made only after the GCF has obtained satisfactory protection against litigation and expropriation in the country, or has been provided with appropriate privileges and immunities.

### IV. List of proposed conditions (including legal)

In order to mitigate risk, it is recommended that any approval by the Board is made subject to the following conditions:

1. Signature of the funded activity agreement in a form and substance satisfactory to the Secretariat within 180 days from the date of Board approval; and
2. Completion of legal due diligence to the satisfaction of the Secretariat.

**commented Brazil for the efforts made. The new information provided in the modified version of the data through the data available on websites and the examples on how estimates of CO2 emissions from deforestation were calculated, increased the reproducibility of FREL calculations.**

The AT indicated that information provided for the years 1996 and 1997 is less accurate (as the data were not based on measured data) and less reliable than the rest of the time series (which was constructed from 1998 onwards). This is because digital information on deforestation has been available in Brazil since 2000 (i.e., for the period 1998–2000, and on a yearly basis thereafter) while data for 1996 and 1997 were based on manual estimates. In light of this observation, Brazil indicated that it is seeking to complete the annual deforestation maps for these years (which will be available in 2023). This will improve the accuracy of the estimates provided in the submission for the period 1996–2000 and will also contribute to the FREL for 1999 and 2000. Overall, the AT considered that a better estimation of deforestation (e.g., through digitalization of the deforestation map) is an area for future technical improvement.

In the FP, Brazil indicates that since the assessment of the FREL, Brazil received feedback from the AT on the need to improve the general understanding that historical information before 2000 should not be used in future national FREL submission from Brazil to the UNFCCC in 2020. The FP indicates that Brazil's efforts are now focused on building deforestation maps for the period 1996–2000, rather than improving the historical data before 2000.

**[vii] Is the FREL/FRL accurate? (The data and methodologies used neither over- nor under-estimate emissions and/or removals during the reference period, so far as can be judged)**

As discussed above, the AT noted that estimates of deforestation for the years 1996–1997 are less accurate than the rest of the time series. A better estimation of deforestation (e.g., through digitalization of the deforestation map) for the period 1996-1997 may be achieved through digitalization of the deforestation

maps, in addition, that AT recommended consideration of the improvement of the core forest map and note on the already observed effect the ongoing effort on monitoring complex forest cover change dynamics has had.

(viii) Have all REDD+PLUS activities that are a significant source of emissions been included?

The AT acknowledges that Brazil included the most significant activity in the FREL (reducing emissions from deforestation). The AT notes that other countries have also included significant activities, particularly in particular degradation. The AT notes that the current exclusion of degraded areas is conservative and does not reflect the full extent of forest carbon loss. The AT acknowledges the intention expressed by Brazil to (i) continue monitoring forest cover change dynamics; (ii) include emissions from deforestation leading to an increase in forest degradation activities and (iii) consider including other sources of emissions when new, adequate data and better information become available.

The AT considered the exclusion of degraded areas conservative in the context of constructing the FREL.


(in) Have all of the most significant pools been included?

The AT acknowledges that Brazil excluded in the FREL the most significant pools in terms of emissions from forests. However, the AT noted that changes in soil carbon can be significant when forest land is converted to other uses and that Brazil has provided information on this. The AT does not have data on the dynamics of forest conversion for a few years (2013-2015) but has used two proxies for estimating emissions and two sources of information that were used as proxies to estimate the fate of soil organic carbon in deforested areas. The AT considers that the exclusion of soil organic carbon is adequately explained by Brazil, noting that it will provide more detailed information on this pool in the future with the aim of including it as part of the FREL.

The AT also requested clarification on the reasons for the omission of the dead wood pool from the FREL. In tropical forests, there are emissions from dead wood to the atmosphere that would need to be estimated and considered the verification of emissions from dead wood (i.e., the inclusion of this pool in the provision of more
