### (iv) The full and effective participation of relevant stakeholders, in particular indigenous peoples and local communities, in the actions referred to in paragraph 78

The governance structure of the PCPMAs involves representation of civil society organizations and indigenous peoples' representatives in the COMARED+. The thematic advisory boards serve as a forum and platform for social participation on the ENEDS+ with specific criteria for selection. The SOI provided information related to the project level, with the guidelines from BNDES related to monitoring and evaluation of actions aimed at promoting free prior and informed consent, land regularization and resettlement and conservation areas. Provisions were made in the PCPMAs and the COMARED+ related to this safeguard which is addressed by mobilizing resources from other projects.

### (v) That actions are consistent with the conservation of natural forests and biological diversity, ensuring that the actions referred to in paragraph 78 of this document are initiated to incentivize the protection and conservation of natural forests and associated services, and to enhance other social and environmental benefits

Information in the SOI indicated the establishment of various projects aimed at promoting the conservation of natural forests and the implementation of 118 conservation units. There were also policies that promote the sustainable use of biodiversity and natural resources. The SOI identified several challenges in implementing and respecting this safeguard which would need to be addressed by succeeding grants.

# Independent Technical Advisory Panel's assessment of FP100

## Proposal name: REDD+ results-based payments for results achieved by Brazil in the Amazon biome in 2014 and 2015

### Accredited entity: United Nations Development Programme

#### Project/programme size: Medium

## I. Scorecard application

The Independent Technical Advisory Panel (TAP) with the support of a land use, land-use change and forestry (LULUCF) expert selected from the United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change (UNFCCC) roster of experts and with experience in REDD-plus assessment methodologies, has assessed the results-based payments for results achieved by Brazil in the Amazon biome in 2014 and 2015 using the scorecard included in the terms of reference for the REDD+ results-based payments (RBP) pilot programme.

### I.1 Summary of scorecard results

| Scorecard section | Results |
| --- | --- |
| Carbon elements | Score: 36 All criteria "pass" |
| Non-carbon elements | Canopy safeguards: All criteria "pass" Use of proceeds and non-carbon benefits: Score: 2 |

### (d) LULUCF: The Brazilian government considered the treatment of non-CO2 gases as an area for future technical improvement to maintain consistency with international standards and the FREL submission (see paragraph 31 of the TATR). In the funding proposal, Brazil points out that net CO2 emissions from reforestation in the Amazon biome are not expected every year, and hence the recalculations at the end of each reporting period for non-CO2 emissions would not be possible at this point. Brazil further noted that the estimation of emissions from fire events is still under development (see paragraph 31 of the TATR). The inventory, and if possible, non-CO2 emissions from fire will be included in the national FREL submission. The Brazilian government also stated that the accuracy of the time series can be assured and it deemed relevant.

### (b) B.(2)c Accuracy of data and information provided to the technical annexes: The LULUCF experts concluded that the results are accurate, although no uncertainties, although this information was not used in assessing the uncertainties of the emission estimates. The Brazilian government also stated that the accuracy of the results from the implementation of the action on reducing emissions from deforestation could not be assessed. Some accuracy issues were also raised regarding the assessment of the impact of the action on reducing emissions (the "action map") before being defeated, which may imply a potential overestimation of emission reductions. The Brazilian government stated that the LULUCF experts concluded that the results are accurate to the extent possible (paragraph 31 of the TATR). The score is 2: "Significant issues were raised and were material to the accuracy of the results, but the country has provided a plan on how it will seek to overcome them."

# Impact potential

## High

Brazil reduced emissions from deforestation in the Amazon biome in the order of 612,500 GtCO2e between 2004 and 2016 through different effective policies and measures. The Amazon biome comprises approximately 4,197,000 km² and corresponds to 49.29 per cent of the national territory (IBGE, 2011). Brazil has six biomes, but the Government has concentrated its efforts on reducing deforestation in the Amazon and Cerrado Biomes. In 2006 the LULUCF sector represented 83 percent of Brazil's total CO2 emissions. In 2016 that number had dropped to only 45 per cent (IBGE, 2017). Although Brazil is not achieving the absolute and relative reduction, the LULUCF sector still represents a significant source of emission.

In June 2014, Brazil submitted a dynamic FREL to be applied to emissions reduction results achieved in the period 2006–2010 (FREL A) and 2011–2015 (FREL B). Later, in December 2017, Brazil submitted a second version of its FREL (FREL C), which contained an annex with REDD+ results for the period 2006 to 2016. Including the second BUR was not possible because it would have been too late for the UNFCCC COP23 in Bonn, Germany. The third BUR was submitted on December 7th, 2018 and contained emissions results achieved in the Amazon biome in the period 2011 to 2015, based on FREL C, estimated at an annual average of 4,963 km² deforested per year. The third BUR also included the current proposal accounts for only 2 per cent of the total volume of REDD+ plus results between 2014 and 2015 (measured against FREL B), equivalent to a total of 8,793,262.55 tCO2eq or 25 MtCO2eq.

The third BUR is expected to be submitted to the UNFCCC in 2019 and should include REDD plus results for 2016–2017, measured against FREL C. Brazil has intensified its efforts to avoid deforestation mainly through controlled and certified efforts. Over the past eight years, the average annual deforestation rate for the Legal Amazon region was around 3,500 km² with a reduction of 84 per cent since 2004 (IBGE, 2017). Brazil has a long history of reversal and has been doing that through various policies and initiatives.

The FREL considered only Gases in the forest. In the future, the FREL will consider all greenhouse gases as an axis for future technical improvement of the FREL. Further improvement is also needed in understanding the difference between deforestation and degradation, which are both recognized by Brazil as an important source of emissions. There is a recognition of the need to continuously improve the GES estimates.

As noted in the scorecard, Brazil has not submitted the aggregate uncertainty estimates or a justification for not submitting them. Therefore, TAP is recommending further work in justifying the exclusion of the uncertainty estimates and the reasons why Brazil believes that it is placing an emphasis on the Cerrado and Amazon biomes, and the intention is to extend the FREL to include other biomes.

The Brazilian National Policy on Climate Change and Forest Code provide the overarching guidelines for Brazil's REDD+ plus actions. The Federal Action Plan for the Prevention of Deforestation (PREDIF) is a key instrument in this regard. PREDIF is a very important policy to pave the way for avoiding deforestation. The plan is in its fourth phase of implementation and is expected to last until 2028.

2.2 Paradigm shift potential

* **Scale:** High

10. The methodologies provided for calculating emissions reductions are clear and robust, and the progress in 2014 results seem accurate according to the scorecard.
11. The Government of Brazil is transitioning from a command and control strategy promoting a strategy that invests in sustainable development and environmental conservation.
12. This transition will be revisited in the future, understanding that there is a possibility of changes to the policies that could potentially reverse the progress made on deforestation and forest degradation.
13. The view of TAP Brazil has shown a strong willingness to reduce deforestation in the past and has robust policies and strategies as well as institutions in place as its continue to its positive trend to reduce deforestation.

14. Gains from forest conservation from 2006 to 2015 in Brazil have been recognized as a success story that could change the global levels of CO2 emissions due to land-use change and deforestation.
15. The different policies, the information systems that the country has put in place not only contribute to reducing deforestation but also to increasing forest cover.
16. In the last four years the deforestation rates have started to increase as per the National Institute for Space Research (INPE) data, so attention is needed.

* **August 2013:** 5,874 km²
* **August 2014:** 5,612 km²
* **August 2015:** 7,915 km²
* **August 2016:** 7,697 km²
* **August 2017:** 7,809 km²

17. It is important to consider the risk of addressing the risk of reversal and promoting a long-term reduction of GHG emissions within the scope of REDD+ plus actions, thus ensuring consistent results in the future.
18. The Brazilian Forest Code (FC) was recently updated with new provisions in place including the Forest Core (which applies to the Amazon biome), which stipulates that rural landholdings must conserve 30 per cent of their area with native vegetation cover as a legal reserve and another 20 per cent as an environmental protection area.
19. The Federal Program for the Conservation, Restoration and Sustainable Use of Forest Ecosystems (FPCDA) includes a host of actions to monitor, analyze and improve coordinated actions for maintaining forest ecosystems.

20. The use of the proceeds of the BRF received from Brazil by the GCF will be used to ensure the national REDD+ plus strategy (ENEDDEB+) objectives to continue to climate change mitigation initiatives, including the conservation and sustainable management of forest ecosystems, and fostering a low-carbon and sustainable forest economy, while delivering environmental benefits in line with the country’s National Forest Policy and the nationally determined contribution (NDC).

21. Particularly, the use of the proceeds will be used to support the development of a pilot Environmental Services Payment Scheme for Conservation and Recovery of Native Vegetation (Floresta) and strengthen the implementation of Brazil's ENEDDEB+ through improvements in its governance.

22. The rationale to start supporting incentives derives from different studies that suggest that command and control measures have a limit and there is a need to give positive incentives to landowners to promote sustainable forest management.
23. Floresta is a new and innovative pilot program that aims to provide incentives for environmental services in Brazil, which is consistent with Brazil’s Forest Code, the ENEDDEB+ and Brazil's NDC. The programme targets small farmers, indigenous peoples, traditional peoples and communities and public institutions/agencies (including states and

# The programme will work in priority areas based on a selected criterion and has the following specific objectives:

- Promote financial compensation to incentivize native vegetation conservation and recovery and improvement of ecosystems that generate environmental services (including but not limited to carbon)
- Prevent processes of deforestation, forest degradation and forest fires through financial incentives
- Incentivize the conservation and recovery of native vegetation of rural properties, conservancies, indigenous lands, and lands of traditional people and communities
- Promote compliance with the environmental legislation, especially that related to the protection and recovery of native vegetation (Forest Code)
- Offer a financial mechanism to foster the development and implementation of public policies aimed at conservation and the recovery of native vegetation

## The programme will also work in priority areas based on additional criteria and priority area will be developed and refined in consultation with relevant stakeholders, including indigenous peoples and traditional peoples and communities, civil associations, cooperatives, private foundations, and other environmental organizations, among other things.

### The following elements will be used to improve the REDD+ plus elements to strengthen the implementation of ENERBIO, with a stronger governance structure and more transparent data and information systems, supporting the following activities:

- Development of a tool for monitoring and verification (MRV) including additional REDD+ plus activities, pools and gases, considering the mapping process of the National Forest Inventory (NFI) and the Brazilian Forest Program for all biomes, as appropriate, following the guidance from the Working Group of Technical Experts on REDD+. The aim is to submit a national FREL to the UNFCCC by 2028
- Development of a tool to monitor and measure the impacts of REDD+ plus policies and investments and inform decision making regarding the forest component of Brazil's NDC
- Improvement of Brazil’s Safeguards Information System for REDD+ (SISREDD+) and its implementation in the National Forest Inventory (NFI)
- Enhancement of the capacities and access of the various stakeholders for participating in the National REDD+ Committee (CONAREDD) and its Consultative Chambers, including indigenous peoples and traditional peoples
- South-South Cooperation Program in Forests and Climate Change designed by the Ministry of the Environment (MMA) and the Brazilian Cooperation Agency of the Ministry of Foreign Affairs (ABC)
- The activities selected to be funded with the use of the proceeds are well thought-out. The strategy to use both command and control measures and to give conservation incentives through programmes like Floresta + is perceived by TAP as a paradigm shift. TAP understands


---
**Page 145**
---

that there has been a risk in change in policies under the new government, but is confident that the use of the proceeds as proposed will continue to support Brazil's conservation objectives.

21. The development of the National REDD+ Strategy in Brazil had a long history of broad stakeholder engagement. The Federal Decree (8576/ 2014) created CONAREDD for coordination and implementation of the National REDD+ Strategy. The creation of CONAREDD included broad stakeholder engagement, including strong interactions between ENBRIO and the indigenous peoples' organizations. Other key actors involved in the process were the Advisory Chamber on Safeguards (CFC Safeguards), which develops inputs to support the decision-making processes related to the implementation of Brazil's National Forest Law and Safeguards (decision 1/C/PC 15) as being addressed and respected in Brazil’s implementation of REDD-plus. CFC Safeguards is coordinated by the Ministry of Foreign Affairs, Brazilian Council of External Relations and is composed of 30 representatives from CONAREDD and the Advisory Chamber of Safeguards are numerous, diverse and seek to be representative of stakeholders.

22. The proposal presents a preliminary gender analysis including relevant policies that are in place in Brazil, as well as gender gaps and challenges. Based on the analysis, the proposal also proposes actions for implementation with suggested entry points and indicators to measure and track progress on these actions.


### 2.3 Sustainable development potential

**Scale: High**

23. The Amazon biome is the largest repository of biodiversity in the world. Conserving its vast biodiversity contributes to the Aichi Biodiversity Targets in several ways. After 2004, the Amazon biome has been a major contributor to Brazil’s progress towards these targets through the reduction of forest pressure with additional environmental benefits to the local and global communities. The area of deforestation in the Brazilian Amazon biome that was converted area before 2004 had been replaced by secondary vegetation in the subsequent years.

24. The Amazon biome also supports the water cycle in Brazil and the South American continent. It is estimated that one-third of all rainwater falling on the Amazon biome enters our atmosphere and in turn produces clouds and rain. The planet’s land vegetation recycles 48 cubic miles (200 billion cubic meters) of water per day, more than released by the Amazonian rainforest alone – more than the daily discharge of the Amazon River.

25. The economic value of the ecosystem services provided by the Amazon forests have not been fully recognized in Brazil’s policies and legislation. The provision of timber for consumption and agriculture is by itself a huge economic value that surpasses traditional measures such as carbon sequestration, which has been traditionally considered to be valued in a much more comprehensive manner. Accounting for traditional economic measures will inevitably imply transaction costs associated with opening new areas for agriculture. Brazil has already implemented several policies and measures aimed at reducing deforestation, including the restriction of access to credits and other measures that have worked effectively in some regions. However, the implementation of agricultural expansion and reforestation is still a major challenge.

26. Moreover, according to the project proposal to assess VIII, the large reduction in deforestation rates observed region through the implementation of REDD-plus policies and measures has had a positive impact in the region. Since 2005, the Human Development Index for the Amazon region has improved faster than for the rest of Brazil.


---
**Page 146**
---

2.4 Needs of the recipient

* **Scale: High**

    * Brazil has taken leadership in REDD-plus activities under the forestry-related opportunities created by the UNFCCC and the Warsaw Framework for REDD plus.
    * The NGC of Brazil plays a key role in the country's efforts to reduce deforestation and related carbon emissions.

30. Maintaining the Amazon biome requires large-scale investment. According to the project proposal, a variety of finance sources, such as national (federal, state and municipal/local level budgets), non-governmental organizations (NGOs), private sector) and international (donor countries, multilateral development banks, etc.) are needed.
31. The Amazon Fund also made significant investments that have directly and indirectly contributed to the reduction of deforestation in Brazil.

30. The current economic crisis of Brazil has significantly reduced government expenditure.
    * The total budget executed by MMA from 2014 to 2017 was on average R$6,25 billion (USD 750 million) per year, which is a significant reduction compared with the previous years' budgetary expenditure. A large proportion of this budget is spent on activities related to forest protection, especially in the Amazon biome.
    * The economic crisis and budget allocations require innovations within climate finance both internally and with the support of the international cooperation.

30. The task to support command-and-control activities in such a vast area requires lots of investments in information technology (IT) systems, including GIS, remote sensing, monitoring systems and data. Moreover, studies have shown that the linear relationship between fines and reduction rates is not always positive. In fact, there are many cases where fines do not reduce deforestation.
    * Investments in positive incentives are key to maintaining and further expanding upon low deforestation rates.

30. The FIP pilot programme is well designed to test a new approach that combines both carrots and sticks. Therefore, the use of the proceeds to test the combined model and to advance in the fight against deforestation is essential, especially in the light of the economic crisis and budget allocations required by the new government.

31. This funding proposal came to GCF Secretariat as a response to the Brazil's call for projects under the REDD-plus pilot programme. Brazil is presenting an accurate plan to invest the proceeds that are much needed.


2.5 Country ownership

* **Scale: High**

    * In the case of Brazil, REDD-plus implementation has been country-driven.
    * The PPCdAm has been Brazil's core REDD-plus policy for the Amazon region and has been revised in 4 revisions to make it more efficient.
    * The MMA has been responsible for the coordination of the PPCdAm.


---
**Page 147**
---

The FGCFM is in full consistency with the ENERDHS and Brazil’s NDC to the UNFCCC and the Paris Agreement. REDD-plus is a means for the implementation of Brazil’s NDC in the forest sector. The resources from GCF-REDD plus payments through the Floresta+ pilot programme will be used as follows:

• This project proposal has had the approval by COMARRED+ and will be implemented by the MMA with support from the United Nations Development Programme. The MMA has been fully responsible for the implementation of the project, including the management of proceeds as well as providing all input necessary to inform policies on actions implementing REDD-plus.

At project inception, a comprehensive and participatory Environmental Social Impact Assessment (ESIA) will be conducted once the design of the four modalities of the Floresta+ pilot programme has been completed. The ESIA will be carried out in close collaboration with stakeholders and an necessary, such that all appropriate mitigation measures are put in place before implementation.

Brazil has been a pioneer in regulatory and institutional arrangements for the monitoring and conservation of the Legal Amazon’s tropical forests. The Forest Code of 2012 determines the minimum area of forest cover required to be maintained on private land and the integrity of the climate system for the well-being of present and future generations. This law establishes that all areas with native vegetation are considered public domain, including private properties. In the Legal Amazon the law stipulates that rural lands must conserve 80% of their area with native vegetation cover, or as a legal reserve, applying to landholdings larger than 50 hectares.

The National Policy on Climate Change (PIMCC) (Law No.12.187/2009) outlines the objectives and actions for Brazil’s national commitment to reduce its greenhouse gas emissions by 36% in relation to projected emissions by 2020. With regard to specific REDD-plus actions, Brazil has made a commitment to reduce deforestation rates on public and private lands located within the Amazon biome and by 40 per cent in the cerrado biome.

As part of its national strategy for climate change, Brazil has structured plans for forest conservation and the recuperation of degraded land in alignment with the PPCDMs and the PNBE.

Brazil has demonstrated experience in managing the REDD-plus programme with resources from the donor community and specifically the programme with the Norwegian Government. The GCF payment will build on these experiences with the new modality in the Floresta+ pilot project.

### 2.6 Efficiency and effectiveness

Scale: Medium

• Brazil has presented the payment for results, accounting only for 2 per cent of the total volume of REDD-plus payments between 2014 and 2015 (measured against FREL), equivalent to a total of 25,983.262 tCO2e or 25 MEGtCO2e. The publication of the scorecard has reduced this amount by 7 per cent.

• The effectiveness of the use of proceeds could only be evaluated in the future; as the Floresta+ pilot programme is still under design. Floresta+ pilot programme funds will be managed according to the needs of the four modalities, which will be determined based on the needs of Modalities 3 and 4. These will be selected as part of the design of the Floresta+ programme.

• Direct payments under Floresta+ will be calculated based on the area (hectares) of native vegetation cover in each property, according to the information provided by the National Rural Environmental Registry System (SICAR). The amount of payment per hectare


---
**Page 148**
---

will be defined in norms to be published by the Project Advisory Committee and should consider:

- The relationship between the preserved native vegetation area and the area of deforested, degraded or burnt native vegetation within the rural property limits;
- The opportunity cost of land use in areas with alternative land use; and
- The potential for co-benefits from the implementation of environmental services,
when applicable.

The effectiveness of these incentives will depend on several factors, including the administrative capacity and the ability to scale up the programme.


With regard to the question from TAP about the sustainability of Foresta® after project completion, the answer was that the programme is rooted on national and legal elements. (UNFCCC) projects are not sustainable in the long term without a strong institutional framework for implementation after the GCF project, including: (i) biological resources from the BMAs and the RMA; (ii) financial resources from the Brazilian Government, the Green Climate Fund or the Amazon Fund; (iii) resources from environmental compensation and conversion of forest areas into rural properties; and (iv) resources from private initiatives that benefit from environmental services, such as sanitation and energy companies. These elements have not been presented to GCF, and therefore it is not possible to judge if they will be effective.


With regard to legal elements: Brazil's Forest Code (Law 12.651/2012) establishes that rural properties must be mapped and registered in the Rural Property Registry (RMA). This law also foresees the creation of a programme for environmental services in its article 48, which is called Foresta®. The Foresta® programme will allow quotas for environmental reserves (Decree 5440/2010); this aims at easing the identification and transaction of forest activities on rural properties. This will facilitate the process of identifying areas that can be used to generate credits in the Foresta® program in the whole country.


Moreover, Foresta® will be supported by the development and application of information technology for addressing the issue of deforestation over recent years. In 2010 the Brazilian Government made it mandatory for all rural properties to be mapped and registered directly with the RMA. The Rural Property Registry (RMA) is now fully operational and has over 4 million properties registered. It is a powerful tool that will allow the identification of areas where Foresta® can be applied. This tool will form the basis for the monitoring and evaluation of the Foresta® Programme.


In terms of efficiency, GCF does not dictate terms for RPEP under REDD+, since it is at the discretion of the TAP to define them. However, in order to ensure that future transactions to be properly assessed by the TAP, it would be important to receive better guidelines from the TAP on how to assess these transactions. It will also be useful to see on the use of processes for consolidating REDD+ plus goals and achievements.


The efficiency of this REDD-plus process strongly depends on preventing ongoing forest degradation and deforestation in Brazil. This is a complex issue involving land tenure uncertainties, forest degradation processes and the displacement of emissions. These three elements will be critical in accounting for a real estimate of emissions in the future.


---
**Page 149**
---

### Overall remarks from the Independent Technical Advisory Panel

The Brazil REDD-plus project proposal presents a good case for financing under the GCF REDD pilot programme. However, some of its nuances in the project proposal lack sufficient information on the efficacy or utilization of the proceeds for the TAP to make specific and decisive remarks.

The ITAP recommends that Brazil and the AE give special attention to the following:

(a) Consider further analysis on how aggregated uncertainties affect the results and means to improve the FRELA and reporting;

(b) Taking into account that there may be significant emissions caused by forest degradation, consider the continuation of efforts to understand the effects of forest degradation in emissions reporting;

(c) Consider incorporating lessons from the future submission to UNFCCC on the actions that the country is taking to prevent or minimize the displacement of emissions; and

(d) Ensure the sustainability of the Floresta+ pilot programme with a plan around scalability, monitoring expected outcomes and indicators that goes beyond the initial six-year period.

### Has the country provided information on aggregate uncertainties, taking into account national capabilities and circumstances?

The LULUCF experts note that Brazil already provided information in uncertainties in its FREL submission in October 2014, and this remains valid. The uncertainties are related to the effectiveness of the action on reducing emissions from deforestation for the period 2006-2038. These uncertainties are associated with the carbon density map, including the data collection, the allocation factors used for calculating the Carbon content, the biomass from smaller trees, and other variables such as the volume of timber harvested to estimate the carbon density of forest type per the RAMBARRAG. The uncertainties in the estimates of the volume of timber harvested are not assessed using the uncertainties of the emissions estimates. Thus, the effect on reducing emissions is also not affected by this.

# Response of the accredited entity to the independent Technical Advisory Panel's assessment (FP100)

Proposal name: REDD+ results-based payments for results achieved by Brazil in the Amazon biome in 2014 and 2015

Accredited entity: United Nations Development Programme (UNDP)

## Impact potential

UNDP is in full agreement with the ITAP assessment finding that:

- The relevant mitigation and/or adaptation impact is clear.
- The indicators for calculating non-GHG impacts are provided with specific values.
- Methodologies provided for calculating non-GHG impacts are clear and robust.
- The proposal compares the indicator values against appropriate benchmarks to demonstrate the impact potential.

## Paradigm shift potential

UNDP is in full agreement with the ITAP assessment finding that:

- Describes the potential for scaling up to the country's NDC, national REDD+ plus strategy and/or low-carbon development plans and policies.
- Explains how the programme will enable knowledge sharing and learning.
- Describes how proposed measures will create an enabling environment and contribute to institutional strengthening.
- Explains how the programme strengthens the regulatory framework and policies.
- Demonstrates paradigm shift potential for catalyzing impact beyond a one-off payment.

## Sustainable development potential

UNDP is in full agreement with the ITAP assessment finding that the proposal demonstrates environmental, social and economic impacts, including the gender sensitive development impact.

## Needs of the recipient

UNDP is in full agreement with the ITAP assessment finding that the proposal clearly:

- Describes the degree of vulnerability of country/population.
- Demonstrates that the programme addresses financial, economic, social and institutional needs.

## Country ownership

UNDP is in full agreement with the ITAP assessment finding that the proposal:

- Sufficiently explains how the programme contributes to a national climate strategy and/or policies.

# Introduction

The Green Climate Fund (GCF) recognizes the central importance of gender considerations in terms of both impact and access to climate funding, and requires a Gender Assessment and Gender Action Plan to be submitted as part of the funding proposals that it assesses. The main objective of the Gender Assessment is to screen the gender aspects of the GCF project, and with subsequently strengthen the gender response of the GCF project. This assessment will also provide an overview of the gender dynamics in Brazil, with a focus on REDD+ and related thematic areas, specifically as they relate to the results period (2014 and 2015) as well as the investment of proceeds for the period 2016-2018. The Gender Assessment will be an integral part of the proposal and should not be considered additional, but rather part of the basis of the proposal, including its Stakeholder Engagement and Environmental and Social Management Framework (ESMF) Annexes.

This gender assessment also identifies gender gaps that are relevant to the project and examines potential opportunities for addressing these gaps. The essential data will be supplemented with available data from studies conducted by the Government of Brazil, United Nations (UN) agencies, and multilateral and research organizations; and included:

1. Undertaking a desktop review and aligning approaches in this proposal with the national gender policy.
2. Reviewing and considering national aspirations as detailed in the national policies and agenda on gender and women's empowerment;
3. Incorporating information from lessons learned from past studies and assessments on gender in Brazil from the Government of Brazil, the UN and international development and research organizations; and
4. Integrating gender considerations in the project indicators, targets, budget and activities.

# Existing Gender Dynamics and Inequalities in Brazil

Over the last two decades, Brazil has made good strides in promoting gender equality within the country as a whole. The Brazilian government has been proactive in its efforts to promote and integrate gender equality into the broader policy-making agenda and has achieved substantial advances in women's education and health equity before the law. Brazilian women now even outperform men in various education indicators.

Progress has been weaker, however, in the areas of women’s economic opportunities, particularly in outcomes related to women’s decision-making power and agency (i.e., the capacity to make decisions and take control over one’s life projects). Additionally, aggregate advances by women often hide the more racial or geographic differences in every dimension of gender equality. This means that, even in areas where progress has been made, large groups of women are being left behind due to


---
**Page 171**
---

# Annex XIII (c)

## Gender Assessment and Action Plan

their racial, ethnic or geographic identities. And when these types of inequalities remain, these same women face double discrimination for being female as well as for being part of a different culture, race, ethnicity, etc. In Brazil, the existing gender dynamics can be detrimental to men as well. The contribution of women to economic development is not only important in itself but also has far-reaching implications for development. These existing inequalities are discussed in more detail within the sections below.

### 2.1 Gender inequality index

Through the years, several indices have developed to quantify the concept of gender inequality. The United Nations Development Programme uses the Gender Inequality Index (GII) and Gender Development Index (GGDI). The GII measures the gap between women's and men's achievements in education, health, and economic resources. The GGDI measures the gender gaps in development.

Brazil has a GII value of 0.424, ranking out of 139 countries in the 2015 index. The female HDI value for Brazil is 0.754 in contrast with 0.751 for males, resulting in a GGDI value of 1.005, which places the country into Group I (high equality in HDI achievements between women and men).

The Global Gender Gap Index (GGGI) of the World Economic Forum examines the gap between men and women in four categories: economic participation and opportunity, educational attainment, health and survival, and political empowerment. Out of 144 countries, Brazil's rankings, based on GGGI in the 2016, are given below:

| Description | Value | Rank |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Economic participation and opportunity | 0.640 | 91 |
| Educational attainment | 0.998 | 42 |
| Health and survival | 0.950 | 1 |
| Political empowerment | 0.132 | 86 |
| Gender Gap Index 2016 | 0.687 | 79 |

* Inequality = 0.00; Equality = 1.00. Source: The Global Gender Gap Report 2014

The Organization for Economic Cooperation and Development (OECD) developed the Social Institutions and Gender Index (SIGI), a composite index that scores countries (i.e., 0 to 1 on 14 indicators grouped into five sub-indexes: discriminatory family code, restricted physical integrity, son bias, restricted resources and assets, and restricted civil liberties) to measure the discrimination against women.

### 2.2 Education

The literacy rate of youth (ages 15-24) is 98% for males and 99% for females, which is up from 86% in 1993. Some 70% of all 13-year-olds currently attend school, with 98.1% of girls and 98.5% of boys of this age attending school.

School attendance rates drop between age 14 and 17 years of age; however, only with over 87% of 15-year-old girls and 73% of 17-year-olds attending school. This decline does not differ substantially between boys and girls in the Northeast region (90% for both) compared to the Southeast (84% for boys and 62% for girls). Among women 21 years of age attend schools, versus only one in four of their male counterparts. With the increase in women's and girls' schooling, a gender gap in favor of girls has developed in secondary (female-to-male ratio is 0.9) and tertiary education (1.3) according to the latest data from 2008. The increase in female tertiar yeducation is notable given that in 1976, the female-to-male ration at tertiary education was as low as 0.6.

To note, with education, disparities emerge when disaggregating by race, with white women reporting over a year more of education than women who self-identify as black or pardo (Afro-Brazilian), 9.1 versus 7.2 years of schooling in the Northeast and Southeast and the Center-West report 8.8 years of schooling compared to 7.3 for those in the Northeast.

Gender disparities in education increase with levels of education. While equal shares of boys and girls (92.5%) ages 4 to 10 were enrolled in school in 2013, the shares dropped at ages 15-17 to 60.2% for girls versus 80.7% for boys. The gender gap widens further with the next age group, 18-29 year-olds, to 18.8% for women, which is still meaningfully higher than the male share of 14.0%. Currently, close to two-thirds of graduates from tertiary education each year are women.

### 2.3 Political participation and decision-making

Women's political participation and representation are still very weak in Brazil and have not much changed over the last decades. While Brazil is one of the few countries in the Latin America region that has had a female president, Dilma Rousseff (from 2011 until 2016), only six out of 39 ministers in her government were women. Women are still underrepresented in decision-making positions and in areas such as human rights, racial equality, and policies for women. None of the new ministers whom current president Michel Temer selected for his government in May 2016 are female.


---
**Page 173**
---

# Annex XIII (c)

## Gender Assessment and Action Plan

There has been a recent change to the Brazilian electoral code which now requires that at least 30% of political party's candidates must be women, and that at least 5% of financial resources must be allocated to promoting female political participation and meeting this quota. As a result, the number of female candidates in the last general election, in 2014, increased by 47% when compared to the election in 2010.

Nevertheless, the election itself did not significantly change the gender make-up of law-making bodies: In 2014, 45 women were elected to the Chamber of Deputies; in 2014, 51 female candidates secured seats for the Senate. In 2014, 39 women were elected to the Chamber of Deputies and four for the Federal Senate, which renewed one third of its 81 seats in 2014. Five female senators were elected, joining another six previously chosen. According to IIGE (2016), in 2017, women were occupying 13.2% of Chamber of Deputies seats and 15.6% of the Senate seats and 19.8% of the Chamber of Deputies seats. Additionally, the Inter-Parliamentary Union’s “Rank Brazil 132nd in the world” in its “List of Women in National Parliaments.”

At state level, leadership roles are equally male-dominated, with only one female governor elected in 2014 for the 27 posts available. In state parliament, the number of women elected decreased between 2013 and 2014, from 58 to 56 seats in 2014, compared to 141 in the previous cycle. Finally, at the local level, only 11.8% of elected mayors and 13.3% of city councilors are female (CEPAL Gender Observatory, data for 2014).

Within this context it is important to see the level of the organization of women, who live in rural and forest areas. Since the 1970s, the movements of rural women workers began to grow with the emergence of several regional organizations, among them the Movement of Rural Women Workers of the Northeast (1986) and the Inter-Parliamentary Movement of the Women Babalu Coconuts Breakers of the State of Bahia (2003). In 2005, the National Confederation of Peasant Women (2003) and The Dashes March which took an important place in the political arena in the recent years. In its 5 editions (2006, 2001, 2007, 2008, 2011), the Dashes March mobilized thousands of women from all over Brazil to demand land and credit for small farmers, fighting hunger, poverty, violence and requesting sustainable development, democracy, autonomy and freedom for women.

Recently, new organizations of women are being created, representing specific minorities. As an important example, in 2010, the Brazilian indigenous movement founded the Union of the Indigenous Women from the Legal Amazon (UMIAE).

### 2.4 Labour force participation<sup>1</sup>

Female labor force participation (LFP) and employment have risen only slightly over the last 20 years, from a rate of 54% in 1995 to 59% in 2014. To note, while the respective rates for men declined ever so slightly and female rates increased over the last twenty years, most of this happened between 1995 and 2003. Between 2003 and 2014, female LFP in Brazil did not deviate by more than a percentage point.

While labor force participation changed little for men or women over the past decade, large drops for some groups is present when looking at age and educational level. For example, LFP among unskilled young men and women dropped significantly between 2004 and 2014. At the same time, skilled people aged 35–64 increased their participation after retirement.

In households with children present, the LFP participation increases for both men and women when they have just one child; but then drops continuously for females and stays constant for males with every additional child in the household.

LFP among women is higher in the top quintiles of income distribution, regardless of the household composition. Women in households with both parents and another female adult tend to participate less than those who live alone or have a male partner. In contrast, LFP participation rates are lowest in the poorest two quintiles; LFP does not change much whether they have children or not in the household. In single-parent households, which are typically led by women, LFP is higher when there is no other adult present in the home.

Even though female LFP rates increase, women also face higher levels of unemployment relative to men, particularly among younger cohorts. In 2013, the average unemployment rate was 6.7% for the population aged 15 and higher. For women, it was 8.2%, whereas for men it was only 4.8%. This gap widens significantly in the younger population, with 17.1% of women ages 18–24 being unemployed compared to 10.3% of men in the same age group.

These LFP rates are also influenced by occupational segregation, which shows up not only in the differences in gender representation in different sectors, but also in vocational training and entrepreneurship. In Brazil, female employment is still concentrated in sectors related to traditionally female occupations, such as education, health care, social services, administration, and domestic services. Together, these sectors account for approximately 43% of working females, while about 48% of employed men are engaged in the agricultural, industrial, and construction sectors.

The rate of female entrepreneurship in Brazil is close to the regional average, with 15% of working-age women打算to start or currently running a new business. This is also very close to the male rate of 13%. However, the proportion of female entrepreneurs who are self-employed is significantly lower than that of male entrepreneurs—71% versus 55%. This data, together, suggests that there is a willingness among women to start business but that there are barriers—actual or perceived—for the growth of entrepreneurship.

# Annex XIII (c)

## Gender Assessment and Action Plan

### female-owned businesses.

Business training offered to women is often gender-based, particularly in rural areas, where women often take courses on traditional roles, such as cooking and sewing. Female-owned businesses are concentrated in the micro and small end of the spectrum. They grow more slowly, use less labor and physical capital, and are focused in the commerce, services, and manufacturing sectors, which tend to be less productive.

Differences in hours worked in paid work and domestic tasks are also present between men and women. In Brazil, women tend to spend an average of 27.3 hours a week on domestic work and men 5.5 hours. This division of labor not only reduces women's earnings by limiting the time they spend on paid activities but also contributes to gender inequality in the household.

This work is more likely to be found in the informal sector or in low-growth entrepreneurship. Brazilian women also work for more hours than men, when both paid and domestic work are taken into account. Women work an average of 37.8 hours a week compared with 29.5 hours worked by men. The difference between sexes increases substantially when considering employed persons, where women work about 55.4 hours a week, or eight more hours than men.

## 2.5 Access to land and social resources

In Brazil, distortions in agrarian, forest and environmental policies, laws and regulations and their implementation have contributed to insecure property rights over both land and timber, leading to persistent conflicts between indigenous peoples and settlers. In the Amazon region, for example, it is estimated that 10% of the land area is occupied by illegal settlements. The Brazilian government has recognized some areas along with sustainable use reserves, smallholder colonization settlements, and quilombola control rights to over 37% of lands in Brazilian Amazonia. These claims are territories" single for their inhabitants and not for the state. They are based on historical occupation and implying the right to self-determination and self-governance of the common property using local practices that may follow a different logic from formal property institutions. In Brazil, "traditionally occupied lands" are those where indigenous peoples have lived for generations, recognized their land properties and recognition of de facto rights related to specific extractive, agricultural, fishing, hunting, crafts and animal husbandry activities. Securing access to territory often is a precondition for survival in the Amazon region.

In Brazil, joint titling of both women and men became an option in the 1988 Constitution, female land ownership remains very low (12% as of mid-1990s) because of the historical exclusion of women from land rights. The National Institute of Colonization and Resettlement (INCRA) resisted joint titling because the forms they used for land registration did not have space for two names until 2001 reforms.

Additionally, land tenure in the country is difficult to track and follow. There is no central database containing information on land ownerships and titles and there is no official land registry system. Rather, each location often has its own paper-based system. This situation makes it equally

# Annex XIII (c)

## Gender Assessment and Action Plan

difficult to compile complete gender data and statistics on land ownerships.

Nevertheless, there has been efforts undertaken by the Government of Brazil to improve this situation. The Terra Legal Program which supports regularization of land titles for small holders in the "Legal Amazon" (including Maranhão and Tocantins) have included actions to empower the position of women through, for example, registration of the land titles in the name of both wife and husband (in that case, the wife is the co-owner). The program also includes a component called Programa de Reforma Agrária which seeks to improve the distribution of land to meet the principles of social justice, sustainable rural development and increased production. This land distribution program has improved the women's access to land titles by means of prioritizing the access for women who are head of household.

In terms of access to social services, the Government of Brazil has invested in partnerships with local authorities and in mobile units (54 buses and five boats) that provide specialized services to women living or working on rural areas. The program is called "Aqui na Terra" ("Here at Home"). While plans exist to extend these services, ensuring these services reach rural women remains a challenge. Specialized and nonspecialized services within the network are concentrated in urban areas. Even though there has been an increase of 10% in the number of calls received by the "Aqui na Terra" program since 2013, at the same time, the number of calls to the DIF (which covers 70 percent of Brazilian cities, originated from rural areas), and quadrupled from 2013 to 2014.

## 2.6 Agriculture

A less well studied sector in Brazil is the relation between gender and agriculture, particularly agrilusness. Some studies have concluded that although agrilusness is often seen as a generator of wealth for women, it also contributes to the social exclusion of women from participation in the labor market.

According to the 2017 Farm Census (IBGE, 2018), Brazil has 5.7 million rural properties with a labor force of 15 million people. Most of them, 11 million, have some level of kinship with the farmer: men represent 65% of this labor force and women 35%.

In terms of race, 45% of the rural farmers (Pardens) are white, 52% blacks and pardos, 1% are Asians and 1% indigenous.

[1] http://mda.gov.br/contudo/(subastro-unico-que-e-n-e-para-ver-seve)/programa-nacional-de-reforma-agricola

The bulletin "Aqui na Terra" (2014) reports that in the period from 2013 to 2014, women could dial 6 or 7 to report cases of violence. Complaints filed through this service are forwarded to law enforcement agencies and to state public prosecution offices.

**Page 177**

The 2017 National Farming Census (IBGE, 2018) is revealing a slight improvement of women protagonists in the agricultural sector: the number of women who are in-charge (the manager) of the production in the farms has grown from 12.6% (2006 National Farming Census) to 18.6% (2017 National Farming Census), while man participation has decreased from 87.3% to 81.4%.

In terms of access to credit, according to the Brazilian Central Bank (Bacen), from July 2015 to June 2018, women have signed 69% of the contracts for rural credit in the agricultural sector and agribusiness system. Women have also signed 24% (851,534) of the total contracts of rural credit. In this context, it is reasonable to assume that access is more likely for those who are the owners of the land and responsible for its management.

The country has also developed strategies for supporting credit access, mainly for the most vulnerable groups. The National Program on Family Agriculture (Programa Nacional de Fortalecimento da Agricultura Familiar) does offer a dedicated line of credit to rural women.

From 2013 to 2018, the PROINAF/Nation has destined around R$127 million for supporting women – rural producers, indigenous, quilombolas, extractivists and fisherwomen.

In practice, however, accessing it is quite difficult, because female applicants must submit a technical project for approval, which requires technical knowledge and thus imposes barriers particularly for less educated women. The Ministry of Health has also launched several initiatives to provide other services to assist in proposal design, but it is clear that this agricultural credit does not generally reach the poorest or least-educated women in rural areas.

### 2.7 Note

In Brazil, maternal mortality rate fell from 120 per 100,000 live births in 1990 to 69 in 2013. 98% of births are currently attended by skilled personnel, compared to 88% in the mid-1990s.

However, it should be noted that the rate of births by cesarean section in Brazil is high, at 57%, for all births and even higher (64%) among women aged between 20 and 39 years. It is not known why the rate is well above this WHO recommended target; it could be due to a combination of financial incentives for obstetricians, lack of training in cesarean section, and the preference of pregnant women to schedule their births in advance. As medically unnecessary cesarean procedures are now prohibited for women in 2015, the Ministry of Health and the National Agency on Supplementary Health launched a program called "Cesarean Section Awareness" (Conscientização sobre Cesárea). Obstetricians are now required to provide a written explanation for each cesarean delivery. Pregnant women are given leaflets with information on vaginal and cesarean deliveries, and health insurance companies are required to disclose obstetricians' share of cesarean sections relative to total

# Annex XIII (c)
## Gender Assessment and Action Plan

Concerning pregnancy prevention, contraceptive use is as high as 80% among married women aged 15-49 years of age. Unmet need from family planning has fallen to 6%, well below the regional average of 13%. In regards to contraception use, there is significant variation in the type of use across income quintiles. Condom use gradually increases by socio-economic level, from 10.3% among the poorest to 14.5% for the highest quintile. Among women aged 20-29 years old, the most common method of contraception is most common among the poorest quintile (32.3%) and drops to 20.2% for the two higher quintiles. In contrast, the rate of male sterilization is only 1% among the poorest but 13.3% among the two wealthiest.

The prevalence of teenage motherhood has fallen significantly since 2001. To illustrate, in 2001, 6.6% of women aged 15-19 years of age reported having had a child. By 2014, this figure had dropped to 3.1%. Among those aged 15-17 in 2001, 7.8% reported having had a child, where in 2014 it had decreased to 6.2%.

## Legal and Administrative Framework Protecting Women and Protecting Gender Equality

### 3.3 Key International Protocols and Frameworks Ratified by Brazil in Support of Gender Equity and Women's Empowerment and Human Rights

Brazil has ratified and/or signed many key international conventions, treaties and plans of action on gender equality, women’s empowerment and human rights. Critical among them are:

- UN Declaration on Human Rights;
- International Covenant on Civil and Political Rights;
- The Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination against Women, including its Optional Protocol;
- The Rome Statute for the Convention to Prevent, Pursue and Eradicate Violence against the Woman “Convention of Belém do Pará”;
- International Covenant on Economic, Social and Cultural Rights;
- The Beijing Declaration and Platform for Action;
- The Declaration on the Rights of Indigenous People;
- The International Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Racial Discrimination;
- The Convention on the Rights of the Child;
- Convention on the Rights of Persons with Disabilities

### 3.2 Key National Gender Equity and Women's Empowerment Frameworks, Policies and Laws

#### 3.2.1 National Constitution and laws

Feminist and women’s movements have successfully advocated for the inclusion of gender equality


---
**Page 179**
---

Annex XIII (c)

Gender Assessment and Action Plan

principles in the 1988 Constitution, as well as for legal changes aimed at enforcing such principles. In addition to this, Article 3 of Brazil's Constitution states that the fundamental objectives of the Federation Republic of Brazil are to: 1) build a free, just, and solidarity society; 2) guarantee national development and social progress; 3) ensure the full exercise of human rights and citizenship (including equality, non-discrimination, and promotion of equal opportunities); and 4) promote the well-being of all, without prejudice as to origin, race, sex, color, age, and any other forms of discrimination.

Additionally, Article 1 of Brazil's Labour Laws states that it is prohibited to adopt discriminatory practices or conditions in relation to access to the employment relationship, or its maintenance by reason of sex, origin, race, color, marital status, family status, or age, except in case the chances of child protection provided for in paragraph XXXIII.7 of the Federal Constitution.

The "Feminicidio/Femicide" law, signed by ex-President Dilma Rousseff on March 9, 2015, is new legislation under Brazil's Penal Code that imposes harsher penalties for those who harm or kill women or girls on account of their gender.

Law 13.112/2015, which was signed into law by President Dilma Rousseff in March 2015, legally requires mothers and fathers to have an obligation to register the newborn and allows mothers to seek registration of birth of their children at registry offices without the presence of the father and Law 13.109, also sanctioned in March 2015, provides six months maternity leave for women in the military armed forces.

### 3.2.2 National Policies, Plans and Programs

Four National Conferences (in 2004, 2007, 2011 and 2016) on Policies for Women (Conferência Nacional de Políticas para as Mulheres) have taken place under the direction of National Secretariat for Women (Secretaria Nacional de Políticas para as Mulheres), which is part of the Ministry of Social Development and Human Rights, in collaboration with civil society. The first three conferences each led to a National Plan of Policies for Women (Plano Nacional de Políticas para as Mulheres). The latest National Plan of Policies for Women was for the period from 2013-2016. It is based on the results and recommendations of the fourth conference, with the purpose of crafting or strengthening plans of policies for women and institutionalizing gender-related politics. Thereafter, state conferences were convened in order to elect delegates for the conference that would be held at the national level to discuss the Plan of Policies for Women. (Currently, twelve states have adopted their own state plans of policies for women.)

Decree No. 6340, of February 7, 2007, established the National Policy for the Sustainable Development of Traditional Peoples and Communities. The goal of this policy is to strengthen programs and actions aimed at enhancing gender relations in traditional peoples and communities and women's contributions and participation in government initiatives, while also valuing the historical importance

# Gender Assessment and Action Plan

## Annex XIII (c)

### of women and their ethical and social leadership.

The "Maria da Penha Law" (also known as Brazil's Federal Law No.13.709) has the objective of reducing domestic violence against women. The law expedites court orders and domestic violence cases, as well as imposes harsher sentences for perpetrators. In 2015, women were afforded greater protection when the "Lei de Desapego" was adopted, which distinguished regular homicides from homicides specifically targeting women. This law also established a special regime of punishment for repeat offenders, ranging anywhere from twelve to thirty years. While this is a federal law, its implementation varies widely across states and municipalities due to lack of enforcement mechanisms or conditionality. While the law has been internationally recognized as one of the most comprehensive legal instruments to address this problem, there are still several bottlenecks regarding its implementation, notably questions of jurisdiction and insufficient funding from states and municipalities.

The National Plan of Action on Women, Peace and Safety of Brazil, with a duration of 2 years (2017-2018), was created to promote gender mainstreaming and empowerment of women within strategies for adopting an inclusive and sustainable perspective of peace and security. It is structured into four thematic areas: 1) the promotion of women's participation in decision-making processes; 2) the following: 1) gender mainstreaming in all actions undertaken by the country in the context of international peace and security; and (3) the empowerment of women and girls as last peace.

With the fifth edition of the Pro Gender and Racial Equity Program, the federal government has also taken steps to encourage public and private businesses to adopt measures promoting equal opportunities and treatment.

### 3.2.3 Additional frameworks and policies supporting gender equality and women's empowerment

There are also other national laws, policies and legislation frameworks that support gender equality and women’s empowerment in Brazil. These include:

- National Policy for Comprehensive Attention to Women’s Health
- National Plan of Action on Women, Peace and Safety
- Laws 10.296/2005 and 11.324/2006 [Focus on reducing the gap and ensure domestic workers’ rights, including minimum wage, social protection and rights such as job stability for pregnant employees and a 30-day leave]
- National Pact for Combating Violence Against Women I and II

### 3.3 Institutional Arrangements

In 2003, the Federal Government created the National Secretariat for Women's Policies (SPM). Originally a ministry, it later lost this ministerial status in September 2015 and was merged with the


---
**Page 181**
---

# Gender Assessment and Action Plan

## Annex XIII (c)

### Ministry of Human Rights and Racial Equality and is now called the Ministério das Mulheres, da Igualdade Racial, da Juventude e dos Direitos Humanos (Ministry of Women, Racial Equality, Youth and Human Rights). This was done as part of a national ministerial reform to cut government spending. Women's rights are also addressed in the National Plan for Women (PNAF), which is implemented by SPM. SPM's mandate is to create enforce policies for gender equality, and led to the design and implementation of National Plans for Policies for Women." The SPM also has an Ombudsman.

The National Council on Women's Rights (Conselho Nacional dos Direitos da Mulher – CNMD), created in 1985 by the Ministry of Women, Racial Equality, Youth and Human Rights, is a national representative body of other areas of government and civil society and is comprised of 36 members from various departments of the federal government and 21 elected civil society representatives. The Council formulates guidelines for the promotion of women's rights and enforces gender-related policies.

Gender equality is also a relevant subject for the environmental sector. Since 2012, the Ministry of Environment has a Gender Committee, which is in charge of discussing and proposing actions to ensure gender equality in programs and policies, specifically those as they relate to Sustainable Development Goal 5 (SDG 5).

### 4. Gender Issues Around Forests and REDD+

In Brazil, the gender division of labor commonly associates men with timber and women with milk. Use, small-scale, local informal activities. Resource-based economies are predominantly controlled by men, including logging, fishing, hunting, and cattle ranching. Women in these areas have opened opportunities for rural and urban women, despite economic risks. Men's and women's gender knowledge also differs according to these areas of expertise. For example, men can identify 100 species of trees but women list more NTFPs compared to men. Men are more likely to be involved with timber production and the sale of timber-based products, whereas women cite a variety of species used for food, nutrition, medicines and other cultural uses. Women's knowledge is also important in relation to the use of medicinal plants as the principal health care providers for their families. Women's accumulated knowledge of forests within the extractive reserves, the ecology, habitats, and specific location of therapeutics are intimately connected with their role as healers. The spatial distribution of these resources and the distinct gender domain provides a valuable, collective adaptive resource for rural communities.

It can, conversely, also slow realities. To illustrate, in western Brazil, one study found that over 64% of women had cut and collected rubber at some point and 78% had regularly collected latex usually tapped by men. Yet tapping rubber, nevertheless, continued to be viewed as a male occupation. These gender categories are not static; they are subject to change, especially with patriarchal norms, the spatial


---
**Page 182**
---

# Annex XIII (c)

## Gender Assessment and Action Plan

division of labor, social isolation and the exclusion of women from community and public arenas have strongly influenced women's roles and livelihood strategies, and often limited their access to resource rights and decision making at household and community levels.

Cultural values within forest communities often influence the roles of women and men in Brazil as well. For example, patriarchal cultural values among some indigenous groups and among the general population can limit women’s participation in decision-making processes related to land use and public arenas, leaving women without independent autonomous property rights and representation in decision making related to forest management.

## 5. Gender integration within forest conservation efforts and REDD+ action in Brazil

### 5.1 Women's collective microenterprises in the Brazilian Amazon

Over the past two decades, women in the Brazilian Amazon region have organized themselves into collective microenterprises to work with forest resources and support more sustainable land use practices. These efforts in turn have contributed to their economic empowerment within their households and communities. The goals of these enterprises have typically centered on improvements for women’s livelihoods, including income generation, access to credit, education, health, social visibility, political awareness, and environmental knowledge. Unfortunately, little information is available about the diverse types of women's collective microenterprises, as many of them are informal.

These collective microenterprises, lacking support from public policies attuned to their particular characteristics and potential, often instead are supported through church-based or NGO groups. These efforts have resulted in many positive outcomes. For example, these enterprises have helped women to change the way they manage their households and communities by increasing their income and improving their social status within their families.

They also have increased economic gains for women, and added value to the NTFP they produce. Given that women have also contributed more to their family’s income composition, they have gained more voice and power in decision making at home. Women who are part of these enterprises tend to be from smaller households with smaller land size, because they implemented profitable productive activities and expanded their management of the family property.

However, the prospect of scaling up for these promising small-scale women-led initiatives presents challenges, given their continued “insularity,” their hybrid nature that incorporates information and credit access, and their lack of integration into public policies. Public policies are also lacking in support women's collective microenterprises, as they do not provide women with flexibility in accessing technical assistance and credit lines appropriate to enable them to learn and grow.

# Annex XIII (c) Gender Assessment and Action Plan

## 5.2 Social Movement of National Council of Extractivist Populations (CNEL)

The extractivist movement in Brazil, which started with the rubber tappers, began not as an environmental movement but as a social rights movement and was later tied with the agrarian reform movements. The rubber tappers movement then came to be allied with the environmental movement when international environmentalists adapted the purpose of the rubber tappers of defending their forests. CNEL was founded in 1985, and its work has been unprecedented visibility and effectiveness throughout the 1980s until today. The council, first founded in 1985, works to advocate for the social and political empowerment of forest communities. In 1995, the council recognized the need to better reflect women's views and needs, creating the Secretariat of Women Extractivists.

Over the years, the Women’s Secretariat of CNEL has built on strengths of forest-relevant women by strategically mobilizing support across sectors and scales, working from the personal to the political while blurring the boundaries between lay and professional. The Secretariat helped shift the paradigm in Brazil regarding women and health in Brazil. For example, the Secretariat worked closely with the Ministry of Health to amend health policies. They were also successful in modifying policy language to include women’s rights and needs as well as the right to access land that serves peoples. The name of this policy became the National Policy of Health for Rural and Forest Populations. This was a significant achievement in recognition of extractivist communities as well as calling for more equitable health care services. In addition, they worked with the Ministry of Health on forest policies; they also created a project entitled, A Bagagem das Mulheres do Floresta (Baggage of the Women of the Forest) signifying the knowledge that women possess and the tools and outside knowledge that the project aimed to bring into the health care system. The Secretariat’s work with the Ministry of Health allowed them to broaden their workhops to reach every extractive reserve in Amazonia.

The Women's Secretariat of CNEL helped to break down barriers in social movements around land use and forest management in Brazil as well. Once barred from entry into rural workers’ unions and social and political organizations, women now have a voice in the politics of social movements that work with forests and land reform. Inequalities, nevertheless, persist. Women are secondary to men in institutional politics and as of yet, there are no women leaders of forest extractive reserves. Women living within reserves generally have little voice in land use and forest management decision and have little access to formal education or public health care.

## 5.3 National-led programmes in rural and forest areas

### 5.3.1 Florestar Pilot Program

As highlighted in the proposal, the results-based payments that would be received by Brazil through


---
**Page 184**

# Annex XIII (c)

## Gender Assessment and Action Plan

The proposed project will contribute to the implementation of Brazil’s forest sector actions, one of which is the Forest+ Pilot Program. It is a new and innovative pilot program that aims to provide incentives for environmental services (ES) in the Legal Amazon region, in accordance with Brazil’s Forest Code (Law no. 12.651/2012). For more information on the legal framework related to this Program, please consult Section C.2.1 of the Proposal). The target beneficiaries for the Forest+ Pilot Program are the following:

* Indigenous peoples;
* Traditional peoples and communities according to art. I do L; art. 3, of decree n°6.040/2007 (that use their territory collectively);
* Public entities (including States), civil associations, cooperatives and private law foundations that act in topics related to conservation and recovery of native vegetation.

The amount of payment per hectare will be defined in norms to be published by the Forests Program Management Committee. Direct payments will be calculated based on the area of native vegetation remnants and environmental liabilities to be recovered, in hectares, according to the data in the National System of Environmental Information (SIN) and the Rural Environmental Registry (CAR). The CAR is a national electronic public registry, mandatory for all rural properties, with the purpose of integrating the environment into land management. It contains information on property boundaries, cadastral monitoring, environmental and economic planning and deforestation. It is also important to highlight that proof of ownership of land is not necessary to register environmental information on a property in CAR; it must be linked to another registry (e.g., Cadastro Ambiental Rural).

Given this, while the CAR provides critical geographical and biophysical information on the properties, it does not display publicly who the property owner is or give any information about the owner (male/female, age, etc.). Rather, CAR assigns a code to each property, which public officials can then use to link the property with other databases. This makes it difficult for third parties to obtain information about a property owner. This format and design of CAR makes it very difficult and complicated to assess information pertaining to the sex of property owners (in those instances where the land is individually held). Given this, it will be necessary to establish additional measures in order to make data collection more difficult, as additional steps will need to be taken for example, to collect sex-disaggregated data when quotas are established to ensure Forest+ provides incentives equitably among women and men property owners. The Gender Action Plan below sets out mitigation and address some of these limitations (see Gender Action Plan below for more information).

One key entry point for gender-responsive action will be ensuring that the consultations on criteria and priority areas for Forest+, which are taken with relevant stakeholders, fully integrate a gender approach in their design and implementation. This will help to ensure that women from different stakeholder groups can identify and equitably participate and that their interests, concerns, and perspectives are taken into account.

### 5.3.2 Bolsa Família and Bolsa Verde

In recent decades, some fundamental programs focused on mainstreaming gender took place in the country, among them the social welfare program known as Bolsa Família, which has defined women as preferred beneficiaries. Bolsa Família has contributed to keep millions of Brazilian women (who account for 58% of the total) out of poverty and has shown positive impact on forest dependent communities.

Another important programme from the Federal Government in forest areas is the Bolsa Verde, which provides conditional cash transfers in return for the maintenance of forest cover. Bolsa Verde shares targeting criteria with Bolsa Família but also includes women as beneficiaries. The programme aims to promote conservation of the ecosystems while also improving the livelihoods of people living in extreme poverty—the condition of nearly 17% of the population in the Amazon region. The traditional people are a group of the balsa wood breakers (largely constituted by women), is an important group that benefited from this initiative.

### 5.4 REDD Design

#### 5.4.1 National REDD+ Strategy

In 2013, Brazil launched its National Strategy for REDD+ (ENREDD+) with the objective to scale up the implementation of actions to reduce deforestation and forest degradation from the Amazon and Cerrado biomes to the national level. ENREDD+'s overall objective is to contribute to climate change mitigation by eliminating illegal deforestation, promote conservation and restoration of forest ecosystems, and improve the livelihoods of local communities through environmental, economic, social and cultural benefits. It notes that these actions will be achieved through three specific objectives: (i) improving the monitoring and impact assessment of public policies for REDD+; (ii) integrating REDD+ into Brazil's National Development Plan; and (iii) strengthening the capacity of Brazil contributing to the mobilization of resources at the scale compatible with Brazil's commitments to mitigate climate change in accordance with Brazil's NAMAs by 2020.

The ENREDD+ discusses social and economic aspects, and mentions gender aspects, and identifies women as a group to be engaged with and who will benefit from REDD+ action in the country. However, it does not provide specific information on how women are targeted within its objectives, governance arrangements or actions line. Thus, in order to ensure that Brazil's efforts on REDD+ do contribute to meeting the needs of groups such as women, youth and the elderly, and actively promotes their meaningful involvement in such efforts, Brazil will implement gender-sensitive practical measures to ensure a gender perspective is fully integrated into the revised ENREDD+ and REDD+ implementation.
