### 5.4.2 REDD+ Safeguards

The application of the UNFCCC REDD+ safeguards requires adapting them to the reality of each country, taking into account national and regional context, and aligning them with existing policies and national REDD+ actions. Given this, Brazil's National REDD+ Committee (CONAREDD+) commissioned the REDD+ Working Group on Safeguards (LG-REDD) to adopt the series UNFCCC REDD+ safeguards for the Brazilian territory, taking into account the diversity of ethnic groups in the country. Within this national interpretation of the REDD+ safeguards[10], the following gender considerations were incorporated:

* **Safeguard (b):** Ensure that all actions are based on and effective within national forest governance structures, with a view to their representation at national and regional level. Governance bodies of ENEDFs[1] and policies or bodies receiving REDD+ resources will be guided by principles of representativeness, participation, commitment, accountability, transparency, effectiveness and integrity. These instances should include the full range of stakeholders, including representatives of indigenous peoples, traditional peoples and communities, traditional and family agriculture, and the private sector, taking into account gender and generational representation.

* **Safeguard (c):** Ensure that all actions are based on and effective within local forest governance structures, with a view to their representation at national level. Governance bodies of ENEDFs[1] should include representatives of indigenous peoples, traditional peoples and communities, traditional and family agriculture, and the private sector, taking into account gender and generational representation.

## Gender Assessment and Action Plan

To note, and as illustrated above, gender equitable participation is an important consideration for REDD+ planning within Brazil. This has been reflected in various COMARRED+ working groups, including its CCTLs. To illustrate, as of 11 July 2016, 67% of the members of CCT-Salv, the Thematic Advisory Board on Safeguards, are female. The System for Sustainable Development and Environmental Information (SISREDD) is currently underway, with four workshops having already taken place. These workshops are representative of various social sectors/groups. Of the 158 representatives thus far, 80 have been female and 78 have been male.

### 3.5 REDD+ Implementation

#### 5.5.1 REDD+ Governance

While there are gender gaps in its REDD+ design efforts, Brazil has taken steps in REDD+ implementation to ensure equitable representation of women, including within its governance for REDD+. For example, the Ministry of Environment (MMA) and COMARRED have appointed a female to represent them in it. Also, COMARRED has gender balance as one of the selection criteria for the representatives within the CCTLs. As of 17 July 2016, all 35 CCTLs are active, with 18 being male and 17 female. Of these CCTLs, including the one dealing with safeguards, have a majority of women.

#### 5.5.2 Early REDD+ implementation

There have been efforts undertaken by the international community to assess REDD+ implementation impacts on women, both in terms of their roles in REDD+ implementation, as well as any changes of their well-being. The results of these studies can help shed light on existing gender gaps in REDD+.


---
**Page 188**
---

# Annex XIII (c)
## Gender Assessment and Action Plan

implementation, inform Brazil’s efforts in how it integrates a gender perspective in its REDD+efforts moving forward, including in its use of REDD+ proceeds, as well as demonstrate areas in which good practices and lessons learned can be drawn upon.

Within the study “The role of women in early REDD+ implementation: lessons for future engagement”, led by CGIAR in 2016**, an assessment was undertaken to analyze women’s roles in REDD+ initiatives in four Brazilian states. The sites were chosen based on their willingness and capacity for implementation and future outcomes. The results from this assessment are not meant to be representative of the entire country, as the sample size is small and the selection process was not random and the sites for the REDD+ initiatives were chosen based on proponents’ interests. However, the sites were selected with their prior identification of specific villages for REDD+ interventions. The following four sites where evaluated in Brazil (from the period from 2009 to 2011):

- Ace State System of Incentives for Environmental Services
- Nongovernmental organizations
- Sustainable settlements in the Amazon: the challenge of family production in a low carbon economy
- The sustainable landscape Pilot Program in São Félix do Xingu

In the Brazil sites, results from the survey found that women’s focus groups demonstrated a basic understanding of REDD+ in all the villages where the village focus groups demonstrated an understanding. However, in Ace, Brazil, although all villages had at least one woman in the main decision making bodies, a majority of these women were not elected and therefore their voices were not adequately represented, able to influence and participate actively in meetings. Additionally, in the Brazil sites, women often did not have access to information about REDD+ initiatives, as they were not involved in the decision-making processes and used the forest less related to men. They were also very dissatisfied with their level of participation in decision-making bodies. At the household level, the majority of women believed that they did not influence household decisions on land and forest use.

This study concluded that incorporating women’s participation in REDD+ alone is insufficient. This is not only due to lack of information, distrust, and lack of experience, confidence and skills and power relations that may limit women's voices in the public sphere; but also because of the limited analysis and understanding of gendered forest uses and community and household relations that may be affected by REDD+ initiatives. Therefore, it is important for future research to include analyses are also needed to understand real and perceived gender differences and anticipate risks.

In another study on “Gender lessons for climate initiatives: A comparative study of REDD+ impacts on subjective wellbeing**, subnational REDD+ initiatives in six countries (one of which was Brazil) were

(2016). “The role of women in early REDD+ implementation: lessons for future engagement.” CGIAR Research Program on Forests, Trees and Agroforestry.

# Annex XIII (c)
## Gender Assessment and Action Plan

analyzed to assess the gendered impact of the initiatives on women’s perceived wellbeing. The results show net negative movement in Brazil, wherein more women in REDD+ villages perceived that their wellbeing decreased and were worse off than those in non-REDD+ villages. In cases where women in the village had a higher level of education or greater access to resources, they were more likely to perceive positive or negative, the reasons given were primarily due to perceptions that the expected benefits may not materialize, was not or will not be distributed fairly, or that the payment value was low. Concerns about the environmental and health impacts of REDD+ also influenced women’s wellbeing in the case of Brazil; some women in villages viewed such environmental policies as detrimental to their livelihoods and wellbeing. The results show a significant relationship between women’s perceived wellbeing and living within a REDD+ village, with women who lived in a REDD+ village reporting worse for health and wellbeing, the reasoning focused on environmental and health benefits of less deforestation and burning.

This study concluded that perceived wellbeing decreased in REDD+ villages both by the villagers as a whole and for women specifically. Women living within a REDD+ village reported being much worse for health – a decrease that is significantly associated with living in a REDD+ village.

These analyses reveal, moving forward, the need for REDD+ efforts in Brazil to fully mainstream gender into design, monitoring and evaluation. These efforts need to more comprehensively explore the ways in which men and women interact and differ with regard to REDD+ implementation in their respective sites, and how these differences may be exacerbated by REDD+ activities. The findings reveal gender inequalities and worsening the situation of women; REDD+ action in Brazil needs to focus on empowering women, increasing their control over assets and safeguarding their rights.

## Recommendations

This preliminary gender analysis acts as an entry point for gender mainstreaming throughout design and implementation of the proposed project. The results and findings of the gender analysis has also informed and guided the development of a Gender Action Plan (please see Section 7 below).

This gender analysis, conducted through desk review, resulted in the following actions:

- Identification of gender gaps and inequalities within Brazil’s land tenure system and incentives for environmental services;
- Identification of gender inequalities and challenges and faced by women and other marginalized groups in Brazil around themes areas relevant to REDD+ activities such decision-making processes, labor force participation, forest use, land tenure, safeguards, etc.;
- Identification of gaps, entry points and opportunities for mainstreaming gender in the proposed project design, implementation and monitoring;
- Development of the gender approach used within this proposal to effectively integrate gender and women’s and girls’ youth empowerment considerations within the social, economic, political and technical aspects of REDD+ activities;
- Identification of sectors and thematic area of particular relevance in REDD+ which gender-responsive actions can catalyze transformative positive change for marginalized groups;

### Key entry points and recommendations for gender action within programme design and implementation

Key entry points and recommendations for gender action within programme design and implementation are as follows. In order to ensure that these are based on a comprehensive assessment of this assessment (based solely on a desk review). Thus, the findings and recommendations of this gender assessment, including the gender-responsive actions, indicators, etc., noted in the Gender Action Plan, should be integrated into the design and implementation phases of the project during the ESIA process, as well as will need to be consulted with and validated by state and non-state stakeholders, including those more marginalized, such as women, youth, indigenous people, etc.

- Undertake measures within work on Florinat (e.g. establishment of quotas, capacity building, etc.) to address gender identified gender gaps and inequalities within Brazil's land tenure system.
- Develop a gender-responsive ESIA which also focuses on the collection of additional baseline data on gender gap in land tenure, women's involvement in decision-making at local/community level, etc.
- In partnership with MMA, work with the applicable government agencies to identify gender gaps and inequalities in land tenure and other related areas. This will ensure that these are addressed as best extent possible to help ensure that women can actively participate in and receive equitable benefits from Florinat.
- Ensure that all agreements and commitments with Florinat (e.g. Program Management Committee) and ENREDO (e.g. CONADEH) are gender-equal in their memberships. In cases where there are seats for civil society groups, ensure women and youth are represented.
- Take into account the gender gaps and inequalities identified during the ESIA process in the design, implementation, monitoring, and evaluation of the proposed outputs of this proposed project.
- Have a gender specialist within the project to support the mainstreaming of gender throughout the design and implementation of the outputs of the project, as well as any associated gender action plans. This will ensure that all gender gaps and inequalities are developed during the safeguards work and social and environmental assessments, etc.
- In stakeholder engagements activities, ensure a gender approach is fully integrated into their design and implementation phases to ensure women and youth can actively and equitably participate and that their interests, concerns and perspectives are taken into account.
- Build capacity of women and youth from local and indigenous communities on REDD+ and provide them with adequate resources (e.g. financing, know-how, etc.) to actively participate

- Given its responsibility to promote the integration of gender equality principles within MMA programs and policies, train and build capacity of the MMA's Gender Committee on Forest+ and REDD+ related issues. The committee will be responsible for developing a manual as a resource to help mainstream gender into REDD+ activities.
- Require all project partners to collect and maintain accurate disaggregated data around REDD+ activities with respect to male and female participation, benefit sharing, positive and negative impacts; then share it across government institutions, and make such data publicly available.
- Devote and allocate adequate funds, resources and expertise for implementing gender-related strategies, monitoring the results of implementation, and holding individuals and institutions accountable for achieving gender equality.
- During project implementation conduct qualitative assessments on the gender-specific benefits that can be directly associated to the project.

Addressing the gender dimensions and gaps identified within this preliminary assessment and implementing the gender-responsive actions noted above and gender-responsive actions noted below in the Gender Action Plan, within project design and implementation, we will help promote the project provides gender-responsive results.

**Page 192**

# Annex XIII (c)
## Gender Assessment and Action Plan

### Preliminary Gender Action Plan

Data presented above provides content and an overall baseline assessment on the gender dynamics, inequalities and state-of-play within Brazil, its forest sector and in its efforts on REDD+ action to date. This analysis identified the differences between men and women and has helped to identify gaps and provide a baseline for comparison.

Addressing the gender gaps noted above, this preliminary Gender Action Plan provides suggested entry points for gender-responsive actions to be taken under the applicable activities of the proposed project. In addition, specific indicators are also proposed to measure and track progress on these actions at the activity level. This can be incorporated into the detailed M&E plan which will be developed during project implementation, and provides concrete recommendations on how to ensure gender (including disaggregated data) continues to be collected and measured throughout implementation.

The gender budget assigned to each of the outputs below reflects the portion of the output and corresponding budget, which either has gender equality as a significant objective or has gender equity as an auxiliary objective within it. So, for example, for Output 1, the overall budget is XX USD. However, the portion of the activities and corresponding budget for Output 1, which either has gender equality as a primary objective or has gender equity as a principal objective, is XX USD. Thus, this is what is shown in the column labeled "Gender Budget" below.

To note, as additional data collection in the field still needs to be undertaken in the forest sector and around REDD+ issues within the country, detailed gender baseline data, as they pertain to the project activities, in many cases are not yet known. In these instances, baseline information for the activities will need to be collected during implementation of the project. At this time, such information is not yet available. Such information will be collected during the process of developing the gender-responsive ESIA, where the generation of gender-responsive baseline data for the proposed project will be a key output. The preliminary Gender Action Plan below will be revised as necessary and gender baseline information will be provided.

It will be critical to directly support the women organizations, those like UMEAH, Bahusoe Community Breakers and other regional organizations of women or supportive of women (OIKAR, the most important indigenous organization from Amazon, created in 1980), which represents 160 people. 75 organizations have now a woman as its General Coordinator. These organizations lack technical skills and financial support. The project could address these issues: i) identification of these organizations; ii) evaluation of their needs; iii) designing and implementing a program.
